Battelle | May 2026                                                                             i

Chemically Enabled CO2 -Enhanced Oil Recovery in Multi-Porosity, Hydrothermally Altered
Carbonates in the Southern Michigan Basin (DE-FE0031792). The overall objective of this
research project was to unlock the production potential in the Trenton-Black River (TBR)
conventional reservoir and improve recovery using chemically enabled CO2. This first-of-a-kind
project, targeting the oil resources of the TBR, involved injection of chemically-enabled CO2 into
an existing well with production/monitoring from a nearby well. For Budget Period (BP) 1, the
focus was on reservoir and field characterization, laboratory experiments, machine learning
(ML) and data analytics (DA), and risk assessment. Budget Period 2 was devoted to developing
and simulating field pilot tests, and implementation of field injection tests. Budget Period 3
focused on production monitoring, performance assessment, and the final development plan.

This six-year program is funded by the US Department of Energy’s (DOE) Office of Fossil
Energy and Carbon Management (FECM) and managed through the National Energy
Technology Laboratory (NETL). This project is led by Battelle Memorial Institute (Battelle) in
Columbus, Ohio. The project team includes West Bay Exploration Company, Innova
Exploration, Inc., Core Energy, LLC, Western Michigan University, The University of Texas at
Austin, The University of Texas Bureau of Economic Geology, Messer America, Hadra Safety
consulting, Baker-Hughes, Valero, and Sasol. The project relied on in-kind contributions,
including site access, well access, collaboration, and guidance from West Bay Exploration and
Innova Exploration.

This project was completed under eight tasks, as described below. This report summarizes all
eight tasks, but a separate, highly detailed report for Task 2 is provided as an attachment to this
submission.
Task 1 – Project Management and Planning: This task included oversight of project
milestones, deliverables, reporting, and budget, and coordination by the Program Manager.

Task 2 – Advanced Field Characterization and Machine Learning Based Data Integration:
This is the primary geotechnical and subsurface analysis task and involved a systematic field
characterization of the TBR play in the Southern Michigan Basin. The task developed
comprehensive datasets, executed advanced data analytics, and lead piggyback field
characterization to address the complex carbonate systems by evaluating the extent of
fractures, dolomitization, facies, and reservoir properties.

Task 3 – Laboratory Experiments: The pore structure of the TBR is complex and the rock may
be oil-wet/mixed-wet. Laboratory experiments were conducted to optimize CO2-enhanced oil
recovery (EOR) fluids for this complex carbonate reservoir. Reservoir cores were characterized
by computed tomography (CT) scanning, mercury porosimetry, wettability testing, and relative
permeability. Slim-tube displacements were conducted to determine the minimum miscibility
pressure (MMP) with CO2. CO2 and water-alternating-gas (WAG) core floods were conducted to
determine the efficiency of the CO2 flood. Foam floods with wettability altering agents were
conducted to test improvement in CO2 flooding with foam.
Task 4 – Design and Simulations of Injection Scenarios: This task developed numerical
models that represent the unique geologic characteristics of the field testing site. Simulations are
geared toward maximizing recovery from EOR under geologic uncertainty.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             ii

Task 5 – Field Injection Tests: This task focused on implementation of the CO2-EOR design to
inject chemically enabled CO2 into the TBR, collect advanced characterization data from the
injection well, and establish baseline measurements.

Task 6 – Production Monitoring: This task focused on monitoring field repressurization with
water, surfactant-enhanced CO2 stimulation, and post-stimulation production and evaluated
parameters affecting reservoir performance during and after CO2 injection. Density,
temperature, pressure, and gas composition measurements inform updates to pre-injection
models.

Task 7 – Performance Assessment: This task evaluated reservoir performance and
assessment of the efficiency of the CO2-EOR injection. Results were integrated into the dynamic
model to update simulations, scale-up results to the field, and assess potential recovery in the
TBR.

Task 8 – Development Plan: Based on learnings from Tasks 5 through 7, performance
assessment and modeling were used to generate high-level economic analysis of CO2-EOR for
the TBR trend, including CO2 sourcing, transportation, and storage. This task also created a
development strategy plan to guide project development in the TBR. The plan was intended to
be utilized by individual operators because production in the region is conducted by small to
medium, independent producers. Additionally, the plan included geologic characterization,
required oil properties, necessary surface infrastructure, Underground Injection Control (UIC)
permitting and regulation, meeting greenhouse gas reporting requirements, qualifying for 45Q
tax credits, and developing monitoring, reporting, and verification plans to facilitate wide-scale
deployment in the TBR.

Summary and Path Forward: The TBR is a large oil and gas field that has produced oil since
the 1970s. Drilling activity and production have fallen off significantly in the last decade, despite
United States Geological Survey (USGS) estimation that half of known reserves remain in the
reservoir, and an additional 800 million barrels of oil may be as-yet undiscovered. This is in
large part due to the highly complex, heterogeneous nature of the hydrothermal dolomite
reservoir. Geological characterization and lab studies completed during the project confirm that
a dual-porosity system dominates production within the TBR trend, with the majority of oil stored
in small but abundant matrix porosity, but the majority of the production coming from large,
highly permeable vugs and fractures that make up the most transmissive, easily-swept portions
of the reservoir. Based on lab testing of surfactant effectiveness and core floods with CO2 and
surfactant, a stimulation plan was developed utilizing CETAC-30, a foaming surfactant, and
CO2.

In order to test the efficacy of chemically enabled CO2-EOR in the TBR trend, West Bay
Exploration’s Lee 26 field, a small, linear reservoir developed with four wells, was repressurized
using water. The project then implemented a huff & puff-style stimulation at the Fischhaber 2-35
well in the Lee 26 field, injecting CETAC-30 laden water and 2,000 tons of CO2 across two
distinct injection phases. After a soak period where CO2 and the surfactant were allowed to
interact with the reservoir and residual fluids, the well was production tested for approximately
two months. The production test did not yield any additional hydrocarbons from the Fischhaber
2-35. However, hydrocarbons were recovered from an offset well, indicating at least partial
success in mobilizing residual oil via chemically enhanced CO2 stimulation. Laboratory and field
work associated with this project was completed with no injuries, no unintended release of
chemicals or hydrocarbons, and no damage to the environment. Based on the data recovered
from the geological characterization program, laboratory studies, modeling and simulation

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               iii

efforts, and field testing, the project team completed an economic analysis of CO2-EOR as
applied to the entire TBR trend and propose a development plan that can be used by small local
operators to effectively implement CO2-EOR within their TBR assets.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                        iv

1.0 Task 1: Project Management & Planning
1.1 Introduction
The primary objective of the proposed research was to carry out a comprehensive laboratory
experiment, computer modeling, and field testing-based evaluation of chemically enabled
carbon dioxide enhanced oil recovery (CO2-EOR) in the Southern Michigan Basin (SMB) with a
focus on the conventional Trenton/Black River (TBR) play. This work was conducted in the
complex multi-porosity (i.e., matrix, vuggy, and fracture porosities) and/or hydrothermally altered
dolomite (HTD) reservoirs developed along fault systems. Heterogeneities,
compartmentalization, and the presence of thief zones make these reservoirs especially
challenging for EOR, so they require customized CO2 injection programs where injection of pure
CO2 alone may not be effective for satisfactory incremental oil recovery. These challenges are
addressed through advanced field characterization, integrated physics-based machine learning
(ML) and data analytics, laboratory process development, and optimized field tests. The
research concept involved the integration of multiple data types to evaluate fields in the study
area to determine which have the lowest technical and environmental risk and are optimal
settings for EOR. Laboratory experiments were used to optimize a CO2 flood specific to HTD
rock properties and subsequently design and simulate injection scenarios that offer wettability
alteration, foaming, and reduced surface tension. The goal of this approach is to improve oil
recovery from matrix porosity and mitigate the impact of fracture zones. The results provided
strategies to improve oil recovery in complex carbonate formations and development plans for
historical plays, thus increasing the likelihood of the application of EOR methods for similar
reservoirs and numerous operators across a wide range of reservoirs and fields. The project
consisted of eight tasks organized into three budget periods for planning and budget tracking
purposes, with an overall period of performance (POP) from October 1, 2019 through March 31,
2026. The total budget for this project was $7,999,998 with$2,153,667 (21.2%) coming from cost
share provided by the Project Team.

1.2 Project Objectives
The project supported a comprehensive research program with the following novel and
innovative features/goals, as identified in the project management plan and illustrated in Figure
1:

 • Develop and apply methods for improved geologic definition and characterization of
 HTD reservoirs to support CO2-EOR efficiency evaluation
 • Develop and apply physics-based ML and data analytics tools to fully integrate multiple
 datasets and accurately predict key reservoir properties in the TBR fields
 • Perform detailed laboratory experiments to optimize chemically enabled CO2-EOR
 fluids in a multi-porosity carbonate system
 • Develop and apply reservoir simulation workflows to design an efficient pilot test with
 integrated insights from geologic characterization, ML, laboratory tests, and simulations
 • Perform a field injection test in a conventional, TBR well to demonstrate engineered
 CO2-EOR design, production, and monitoring strategies in a field setting
 • Prepare a Development Strategy Plan to promote field-wide deployment of optimized
 EOR design in the test site

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 1

These goals were accomplished through the execution of all eight technical tasks, described in
Table 1.

1.3 Scope of Work
The project included tasks aimed at characterizing the TBR play through the use of existing
data, collection of new geologic data, and data integration through the application of ML/DA
techniques. Laboratory experiments were performed to determine optimum composition of CO2
and flood design to enhance recovery of oil. The geologic characterization and laboratory
experiment results were used to design and implement a field injection test at the Lee 26 field in
Calhoun County, Michigan. Based on the results of this work, a full-field development plan,
including economic analysis, is proposed in Task 8.

1.4 Organization & Project Partners
This project was led by Battelle Memorial Institute (Battelle) in Columbus, Ohio. The Project
Team included West Bay Exploration, Inc., Messer LLC., Hadra Consulting, Baker Hughes,
Innova Exploration, Inc., Core Energy, LLC., Western Michigan University/Michigan Geological
Survey, and University of Texas at Austin, and the University of Texas Bureau of Economic
Geology. Other partners included Sasol providing in-kind cost share, Valero providing advisory
support, and DTE Energy.

Figure 2. Battelle is the project leader, while West Bay Exploration is the primary project partner,
providing cost share as well as field site access. Additional cost share and field site access was provided
by Innova Exploration. Analytical and technical expertise has been provided by Core Energy, Western
Michigan University, UT Austin, and UT BEG.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                           3

1.5 Project Timeline and Changes to Project Structure

Project work was initiated in late 2019, focusing on data compilation, review and advanced field
characterization. Work proceeded through 2020, including three-dimensional (3D) geophysical
analysis, image log analysis, core analysis, and laboratory testing of rock, fluid, and surfactant.
Field injection testing was also planned with project partner Innova Energy, which intended to
drill a development well, the McCann 1-20, in the Napoleon field. The project assisted with
permitting and risk assessment for this well. On completion of drilling, the intention was to test a
two-well, inter-well field flood scenario in virgin or near-virgin reservoir conditions, targeting
injection of 10,000 tons of CO2. By comparing the production of the injector-producer pair to the
production of offset wells, the team would be able to establish the value of surfactant-enhanced
CO2 injection in multi-porous systems.

The McCann 1-20 well was successfully drilled and completed in early 2021, but petrophysical
analysis and production testing of the well indicated sub-economic porosity and oil saturation,
and Innova Exploration chose to halt production. Given this poor reservoir character, it was
determined that the well was not a sufficient analogue to the rest of the Trenton-Black River
Trend and CO2 flood would not deliver the data quality necessary for project success.

The decision not to proceed with injection in the McCann 1-20 well triggered a search for a pair
of already-drilled wells that may be a suitable for replacement. Multiple well pairs were
evaluated. West Bay Exploration, another TBR field operator, approached the project with the
idea of injecting in one of its fully depleted fields, the Lee 26 field. The field was quickly
evaluated, determined to be suitably analogous to the rest of the trend, and accepted. Work
proceeded at the Lee 26 field until project completion, with West Bay Exploration taking on the
role as the project’s primary field partner. This pivot to West Bay Exploration’s project site
necessitated a no-cost time extension, which extended the project’s period of performance until
September 30, 2024.

In the time between project inception and the start of CO2 procurement efforts, changes to CO2
supply and demand in the United States domestic market resulted in a significant increase in
the CO2 price (Figure 3), reducing the project’s purchasing power and necessitating a reduction
in planned injection volume to 2,000 tons of CO2. The project reviewed possibilities for
circumventing commercial price pressure by building a CO2 offtake facility at a local Core
energy facility, but a facility of the correct size was too expensive (see block flow diagram and
cost breakdown in Figure 4 and Table 2).

For a well-to-well flood to be effective, injected CO2 volume needs to be greater than 50% of the
pore volume between the wells. Given the broad well spacing at the Lee 26 field, 2,000 tons of
CO2 was substantially less than 50% of the flooded pore volume, so the Lee 26 field was unable
to be efficiently flooded. It was determined that switching to a huff-and-puff strategy (where a
single well is used first for CO2 injection and for production, see example in Figure 5) would still
provide sufficient data to understand the performance of surfactant-enhanced CO2. Combined
with the external supply chain challenges, the pivot to a huff-and-puff operation required the
second no-cost time extension for the program, which extended the program through its end
date of March 31, 2026. The project proceeded to repressurize the targeted reservoir between
August 2023 and December 2024. The goal was to increase reservoir pressure to above the
CO2-oil miscibility point at approximately 1,200 pounds per square inch (psi), where interactions
between oil and liquid CO2 are enhanced. This pressure was not achieved. However, dynamic
reservoir modeling indicated that injecting gas-phase CO2 would be similarly effective.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    4

The project proceeded with injection in February 2025, which triggered the transition into the
third and final budget period. Due to increased CO2 cost and the timing of Michigan Frost Laws,
the project team was limited to two, approximately 1,000 tonne injection intervals and, in April
2025, injection operations were completed and followed by a 6-month soaking period. In
September, West Bay Exploration installed an electronic submersible pump (ESP) into the
Fischhaber well and planned to produce the well until December. After two months of producing
a combination of reservoir and injected water, West Bay Exploration halted production early and
removed the ESP to perform swab tests on the Beattie and Jacoby wells. The final four months
of the project were spent on the performance assessment, including updates to the models and
development planning tasks.

2.0 Trenton-Black River Geologic Overview
Michigan Basin- Formation, Structure, Stratigraphy

The Michigan Basin is a roughly circular cratonic sag structure of unclear origin. The basin is
approximately 400 km in diameter and underlies the Lower Peninsula of Michigan (Figure 6).
Basin fill consists primarily of sediments deposited during three structural downwarping
sequences: a Late Cambrian to Early Ordovician sequence deposited in a trough-shaped
basin, a Middle Ordovician to Middle Silurian sequence deposited in a broad sag basin, and an
Early Devonian to Mississippian sequence deposited in a symmetrical to eastern-tilting basin
(Howell and van der Pluijm, 1999). The TBR Group and overlying Utica Shale that comprise the
reservoir system for this study were deposited during the second of these sequences (Figure 7).
Minor overlying sediments were deposited in the Pennsylvanian and a thick, unconsolidated
glacial cover deposited during the Pleistocene (Kirst and Lusch, 2004).

Figure 6. Regional depth-to-Precambrian basement map illustrating the shape of the Michigan Basin.
From Catacosinos and Daniels, 1991

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  9

Figure 7. Paleozoic stratigraphy of the Michigan Basin. Modified from Swezey et al., 2015.

The Trenton-Black River Group

The Ordovician-age TBR Formations are a thick stack of shallow shelf carbonate rocks
occurring in the subsurface of Michigan’s Lower Peninsula. The formations are primarily
limestone, except in localized areas of dolomitization across the southern three tiers of counties
and in a broad belt along the western margin of the Lower Peninsula. These dolomitized areas

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             10

contain isolated commercial accumulations of oil and gas in discrete fields. Forty classified
Michigan TBR fields have documented production, all in the SMB (Figure 8). Cumulative
production from all fields exceeds 160 million barrels of oil and 303 billion cubic feet of natural
gas. The most notable is the Albion-Pulaski-Scipio Field with more than 135 million barrels of oil
(MMBO) cumulative production. All of these fields are classic examples of geometrically
complex dolomite reservoirs described and modeled by the HTD reservoir facies concept
(Davies and Smith, 2006).

The TBR Formations are predominantly micritic limestone in the Michigan Basin (Catacosinos
and Daniels, 1991) although dolomite is both stratigraphically and aerially significant. The
Trenton is lithologically similar to the Black River Formation and generally indistinguishable in
well logs. Prominent, regionally correlative Middle Ordovician K-bentonites are used to separate
the Trenton from the Black River in Michigan and elsewhere in the eastern United States. These
bentonites represent ash falls throughout the Michigan and Appalachian basins resulting from
Taconic orogenic events and volcanism (Kolata et al., 1996).

The Trenton is overlain by the Utica/Maquoketa shale in the Michigan Basin and adjacent
cratonic arch areas (Fisher et al., 1988). This shale unit is an important top seal for the
hydrocarbon reservoirs. The TBR succession increases in combined stratigraphic thickness
from 400 ft to 1,000 ft from west to east sides in the Michigan Basin. In the basin, the Trenton
consists of open marine limestone facies deposited in an intermittently high energy, subtidal or
deeper water ramp environment (Keith, 1985), and lacking basin margin or tidal flat facies
(Wilson and Sengupta, 1985). Local intervals of grain-rich fabrics are interpreted as either
carbonate shoals or storm (tempestite) deposits (Fara and Keith, 1988).

TBR hydrocarbon accumulations occur mainly as stratigraphic traps resulting from porosity and
permeability variations between porous, hydrothermal dolostone that comprises the reservoir
and tight, unaltered regional limestone that forms the lateral and (occasionally) top seals to the
reservoirs. The Albion-Pulaski-Scipio-Stoney Point trend, which was discovered in 1957 (Figure
9) is by far the largest field in the Michigan Basin, consisting of large interconnected reservoir
compartments (and numerous adjacent, isolated compartments) of heavily fractured and
dolomitized strata occurring at all stratigraphic levels within the TBR formations. Hurley and
Budros (1990) showed that production in the Albion-Scipio trend is from fracture-controlled
dolostone reservoirs related to northwest-southeast fault and fold trends running parallel to a
regional structural trend. Although these formations are present in the subsurface throughout
the Lower Peninsula and in parts of the Upper Peninsula, commercial discoveries have only
been found in the southern part of the Lower Peninsula of Michigan, adjoining parts of Indiana
and Ohio and in portions of southwestern Ontario, Canada.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             11

Figure 8. Map showing distribution of major known hydrothermal dolomite trends in and around the
Michigan Basin. Total production from the Albion-Scipio trend (approximately 135 MMBO), Stoney Point
field (approximately 12 MMBO) and the Indiana-Lima trend (>500 MMBO) are illustrated. Modified from
Hurley and Budros (1990).

Figure 9. Location of the TBR fields, area of interest, Innova Exploration property, and pipeline rights of
way. Images provided by Innova Exploration, Inc.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                           12

Depositional Facies and Stratigraphic Architecture

The TBR rocks of the Michigan Basin were formed in a widespread, Middle Ordovician,
carbonate-rich deposition system of a shallow marine shelf, platform and gently-dipping ramp
that occurs in the subsurface and as outcrops throughout the upper Midwest and Eastern United
States. The primary carbonate depositional facies in these formations can be descriptively
classified using the system of Dunham (1962) which is based on texture characteristics of the
carbonate particles comprising the deposit. The Dunham classification uses the ratio of coarser
grains to fine-grained carbonate mud as a proxy for depositional energy of the sediments.

Mudstones contain mostly fine-grained material with less than 10% coarser grains.
Wackestones also contain considerable mud, but more than 10% grains. Packstones contain
mostly grains with lesser amounts of mud. Grainstones are mostly grains with less than 10%
mud.

Layers with these different textures or facies are stacked vertically and variably distributed
laterally in response to changes in the energy conditions in the regional environment. An
example of an idealized diagram of the regional distribution of these facies was used by
Robinson (2012) to reconstruct stacking of these facies through time and to interpret their lateral
distribution (Figure 10).

Figure 10. Idealized facies track distribution in TBR rocks in the Michigan Basin. This cross-section from
nearshore (left) to offshore (right) depositional energy. Texture code letters correspond to Dunham (1962)
categories, M-Mudstone, W-Wackestone, P-Packstone, G-Grainstone. From Robinson (2012).

Robinson (2012) made detailed descriptions of cores in the collection of the Michigan
Geological Repository for Research and Education at Western Michigan University and used
the classification of Dunham (1962) to group the rock layers into similar packages that reflected

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    13

their original depositional environmental conditions. He interpreted the mud-rich rocks as
forming in distal, deep water marine settings. Coarser grained facies were presumed to have
formed in shallower, more proximal, higher energy settings. Major storms may have occurred
frequently and would temporarily increase the seabed energy and sort and redistribute the
coarser grains. Robinson (2012) examined cores from 14 wells in the southern Michigan basin
and subdivided the strata into six facies based on textures, grain type and sedimentary
structures (Table 3).

Structural Elements, Thermal Evolution, and Hydrothermal Mineralization in the
Michigan Basin

Studies of the structural evolution of the Michigan Basin (summarized by Fisher et al., 1988)
have identified a predominantly northwest-southeast structural grain composed of faults and
folds transecting the basin (Figure 11). A subordinate, antithetic southwest/northeast component
is thought to have developed due to periodic reactivation of basement faults and left lateral
wrenching (Sanford et al., 1985; Prouty, 1988).

Fault reactivation timing is unclear, although stratigraphic studies indicate that the basin
periodically hosted isolated, positive bathymetric features throughout the early Paleozoic.
These features were probably the result of syndepositional growth faults (Fisher et al., 1988).
Regional stratigraphic studies (Lillienthal, 1978) and structural analysis (Prouty, 1988) suggest
that the most widespread and intense period of basement fault reactivation occurred at the end
of the Mississippian (~350 mybp), coincident with Alleghenian orogenesis in the Appalachian
orogen. This coincides with a basin-wide diagenetic event (age dated at about 346 +/- 11
mybp), that includes the formation of “burial” dolomite in the Ordovician St. Peter Sandstone
and siliciclastic-dominated Glenwood Formation (Barnes et al., 1992; Girard and Barnes, 1995).
Models for hydrothermal mineralization associated with basement fault reactivation have long
been used to explain linear fault and fracture-related oil fields (Hurley and Budros, 1990), as
well as pervasive epigenetic dolomitization and related Mississippi Valley Type (MVT)

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                14

mineralization (Budai and Wilson, 1991).

Battelle | May 2026                        15

Figure 11. Schematic diagram illustrating the general northwest-southeastern trend of faults and folds in
the Michigan Basin. Thin, NW trending lines are a schematic representation of this trend. Bold lines
named structural features: AS = Albion-Scipio trend, LM = Lucas-Monroe Anticline, HL= Howell Anticline,
SC = Sanilac fault. Scale in miles (left) and kilometers (right).

The global association of giant oil fields, hydrothermal dolomite, MVT type mineralization, and
basement-involved wrench tectonics is documented by Davies (2000). The “hydrothermal
dolomite reservoir facies” model and major hydrocarbon accumulations world-wide are thought
to be controlled by the localization of hydrothermal fluid movement and mineralization, including
hydrothermal dolomitization associated with faults (Figure 12), especially extensional or
transtensional faults (Davies, 2000). Numerous fracture-based giant hydrocarbon reservoirs
controlled by reactivated basement faults exist, including the Albion-Scipio pool and other
Ordovician TBR fields (Tedesco, 1994).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    16

Figure 12. Diagram showing various associations of hydrothermal dolomite related to extensional
tectonics. Modified from Davies (2000, unpublished data). Cited in Davies and Smith, 2006.

Studies of the thermal evolution of the Michigan Basin (summary in Catacosinos and Daniels,
1991) propose at least three different models to explain the thermal maturity of hydrocarbon
source rocks, and temperatures and timing of formation of authigenic minerals in the Michigan
Basin relative to current depths of burial (no more than approximately 10,500 ft at the deepest
for the Ordovician TBR Formations). Nunn et al. (1984) suggested that anomalously elevated
geothermal gradients existed during the early Paleozoic throughout the Michigan Basin.

Cercone (1984) hypothesized erosional stripping of thousands of feet of late Paleozoic strata.
Hogarth and Sibley (1985) and Hurley and Budros (1990) emphasize the importance of spatially
isolated hydrothermal fluid conduits that created hydrothermal chimneys, explaining both the
spatial distribution of thermal maturity and the distribution of authigenic minerals, including
hydrothermal dolomite (Figure 13). Girard and Barnes (1995) suggested that a Late Devonian-
Mississippian (345 +/- 11 mybp) episode of authigenic mineralization in the Ordovician St. Peter
Sandstone, including hydrothermal dolomitization, suggested anomalously high thermal regimes
near faulted and fractured Precambrian basement within the Mid-Continent Rift in the central
Michigan Basin.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               17

Figure 13. Example model for hydrothermal dolomitization with dolomitizing fluids concentrated along
fault-controlled conduits and typically dolomitize rocks close to the fault plane. Note however, how the
lateral extent of reservoir dolomite is illustrated schematically to vary in a vertical sense.

Fluid inclusions in calcite cements in the Mississippian Bayport Limestone of Eaton County,
Michigan, are present with mean homogenization temperature of 146 +/- 12° C (Blaske, 2003
and 2018). The calcite cements are found in conjunction with pyrite, fluorite, and other fracture-
filling minerals suggestive of hydrothermal mineralization. Other examples of highly fractured
upper Paleozoic bedrock formations in the surface and shallow subsurface are known to contain
exotic suites of authigenic minerals not in chemical equilibrium with modern, fresh groundwater
(Westjohn and Weaver, 1996).

Occurrence of Dolomite in the Trenton-Black River

The spatial distribution of limestone versus dolomite in the subsurface can be mapped using
conventional wire-line log data. Cross plot of formation density an compensated neutron log
responses, in conjunction with gamma ray logs, can be used to distinguish dolomite from
limestone and provides a basis for preliminary geological models relating sedimentary facies
and structural deformation to dolomite occurrences and hydrocarbon reservoirs on a regional
scale.

Geological models for the spatial distribution of dolomite based on conventional, subsurface,
wire-line log mapping and rudimentary well sample analysis, however, typically provide only
incomplete and equivocal evidence to distinguish the geological origin of dolomite.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        18

Confident prediction of dolomite reservoir geometry and distribution, on all relevant scales in
any hydrocarbon system, requires rigorous, scientific testing and development of geological
models for dolomite genesis. Petrographic and analytical methods are required to differentiate
dolomite types and infer mechanisms of dolomite formation. Reliable geological models for the
spatial distribution of dolomite reservoirs and dolomitized hydrocarbon reservoir rock properties
is fundamentally dependent on data relating geological conditions and controls during dolomite
genesis to the spatial distribution of dolomitized reservoirs.

Dolomite, of various origins, is an important lithologic component of the TBR Formations in the
Michigan Basin, and contains distinct spatial and stratigraphic distribution as well as variable
petrologic character. Well-documented petrographic and analytical characterization of dolomite
types in the TBR have resulted in at least three distinct dolomite types recognized in the
Michigan Basin.

These are:
 1. a dense, finely crystalline, ferroan “cap dolomite” that occurs in the top 10 to 40 ft of
 the Trenton below the Utica Shale throughout much of the basin (Taylor and Sibley,
 1986). This dolomite contains moderately well-preserved primary fabrics. Oxygen
 isotopic composition, trace element chemistry, and stratigraphic/cross cutting
 relationships in this dolomite are interpreted to indicate a relatively early, burial origin
 related to diagenesis and fluid expulsion from shales in the overlying
 Utica/Maquoketa formation (Taylor and Sibley, 1986; Coniglio et al., 1994).
 2. a coarsely crystalline, “saddle” dolomite that typically fills fractures and vugs, that is
 common in highly faulted and fractured zones such as the Albion-Pulaski-Scipio
 trend. Petrologic and analytical data (Allan and Wiggins, 1993; Coniglio, et al., 1994),
 including those from oxygen isotopes and primary fluid inclusions, clearly indicate
 that this “fracture related” dolomite was produced during “burial” from fluids with
 elevated temperatures (> at least 100°C), that were most likely transported through
 fracture systems and involved formation fluids of complex origins.
 3. a medium- to coarsely-crystalline, variably ferroan, “regional”, fabric destructive
 dolomite that may be volumetrically more significant compared to “cap” and fracture-
 related dolomites discussed above. Genesis of this dolomite type is somewhat
 enigmatic. Taylor and Sibley (1986) initially identified this “regional” dolomite to be of
 early diagenetic origin, and thought it to be distributed mainly in the margins to the
 south and west of the Michigan Basin. Later studies (Fara and Keith, 1988; Coniglio
 et al., 1994; Budai and Wilson, 1991) related Taylor and Sibley’s “regional dolomite”
 to “fracture related” burial dolomite with distribution controlled by proximity to
 fractures. This dolomite type is typically matrix replacive, may or may not preserve
 primary limestone textures, and probably constitutes substantial volumes of porous
 and permeable reservoir facies in fracture-trend TBR fields.

Routine petrographic analysis is typically insufficient to discriminate multi-phase dolomite that
results from differing mechanisms of formation. Complex, multistage, diagenetic alteration
including partial to complete replacement or recrystallization of pre-cursor limestone and
dolomite textures can superimpose petrographic characteristics that are genetically ambiguous.
Analytical methods to distinguish the conditions and mechanisms responsible for dolomitization,
including the temperature and composition of dolomitizing fluids, are required to relate dolomite
occurrences to mechanisms of dolomite formation and hence the prediction of spatial
distribution and properties of dolomitized hydrocarbon reservoirs within the framework of primary

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 19

depositional fabrics.

Geochemical analyses (Allan and Wiggins, 1993) of core material could provide more detailed
information for characterization of diagenetic alteration through the TBR interval. Taylor and
Sibley (1986) and Budai and Wilson (1991) examined the geochemical characteristics of the
three main types of dolomite in the TBR rocks using carbon and oxygen isotopic analyses.

All types of dolomite have delta O18 values considerably lighter than Ordovician or Silurian
seawater, suggesting that they were all formed at elevated temperatures relative to surface or
shallow burial conditions. Although there is some overlap in oxygen isotopic values, Taylor and
Sibley (1986) found that these three types of dolomite could be reasonably distinguished from
each other by the oxygen isotopic values. The “regional” dolomite had oxygen isotopic values of
-5.5 to -7.5 per mil, whereas the “cap” dolomite had values ranging from -6.5 to -8.5 per mil. The
most distinctive was the “fracture-fill” or “saddle” dolomite with values from -7.5 to -11.5 per mil.
It is interpreted that the larger negative value for oxygen, the higher the temperature of
formation.

Variations in Dolomitization and Reservoir Geometries in the Michigan Basin

Fundamentally different mechanisms of dolomitization will result in distinctly different reservoir
geometry and rock properties. Primary depositional facies controls result in regional scale
geometric distribution and rock properties in early diagenetic dolomite that are distinctly different
from burial, epigenetic dolomite typically related to structural controls and fracturing (Zempolich
and Hardie, 1997; Melim and Scholle, 2002). From a reservoir perspective, outcrop studies such
as that by Zempolich and Hardie (1997) offer key insight into the preferential control on
distribution of dolomite by primary sedimentary facies and resulting variability in the spatial
distribution of dolomitized reservoirs. For example, Zempolich and Hardie (1997) showed that
the size and distribution of fracture-related dolomite bodies may range from kilometers in areal
distribution and hundreds of meters thick when fractures cut across certain primary sedimentary
facies/fabrics, to only tens to hundreds of meters in area and only a few meters thick.

All mechanisms for dolomitization require fluid flow and mass transport that is imposed or
imprinted on pre-cursor facies patterns and primary facies-controlled fluid flow pathways. A well-
documented geological model for the volumetric significance, spatial distribution, and the
various mechanisms of dolomitization in a petroleum system is essential to decreasing risk and
supporting new exploration, step-out and enhanced recovery activities.

For the Michigan Basin, existing geological models for the origin of dolomite in Paleozoic TBR
hydrocarbon reservoirs are diverse. In different regions and stratigraphic intervals of the
Michigan Basin (and surrounding areas), the origin of dolomite is interpreted to result from a
variety of mechanisms and processes:

As a result of the apparent complexity and diversity of dolomite origins in these units, and a
general lack of understanding of the spatial distribution of dolomite facies basin-wide, the
implications of dolomite genesis on hydrocarbon reservoir distribution and quality is poorly, or at
least incompletely, understood in the Michigan Basin. In the absence of well-documented
geological models for the origins and spatial distribution of disparate dolomite types in these
units, predictive models for the spatial distribution, field-scale geometry, and properties of
hydrocarbon reservoir rocks are not well established. Although patterns of spatial distribution
and types of dolomite have been studied in these reservoir units individually and in selected

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               20

fields, only Coniglio et al. (1994) have suggested that cross-formational mechanisms common to
the TBR zones are probably important to the creation of many/most dolomite reservoirs in these
units.

Trenton-Black River Production History

TBR discovery and development drilling began in 1884 at Findlay, Ohio, along the Findlay-
Kankakee Arch in Indiana and Ohio (Davies, 2000) resulting in the first TBR discoveries. This
led to the drilling of over 100,000 wells and the production of 500 MBO across the region. The
first commercial TBR discovery in the State of Michigan occurred in 1920 in Monroe County.
This resulted in the development of the Deerfield Field located along the Lucas-Monroe
monocline, an extension of Bowling Green Fault zone from Ohio. Reservoir quality dolomite
lenses in the upper 125 ft of the Trenton Group has produced approximately 768,000 barrels of
oil from 40 wells drilled on 360 acres.

Two small oil fields were discovered in the 1940s in southern Wayne County about 20 miles
northeast of the Deerfield field. Sumpter field discovered in 1941 has only one producing well
that had very limited production (227 barrels) before being abandoned in 1942. New Boston
field, 3.8 miles farther to the northeast, was discovered in 1943 and had two producing wells. It
had a cumulative total of 2,349 barrels of oil produced before being abandoned in 1949.

The Northville field, in portions of Oakland, Washtenaw and Wayne Counties, was discovered in
1947 for the Dundee pool. The 1954 discovery of the Salina-Niagaran and TBR pools in
Northville expanded the commercial potential of this field which sits astride the Howell Anticline,
a large northwest-trending basement-rooted structure to the northeast of the Lucas-Monroe
monocline. At least 50 producing wells were drilled in the TBR reservoir and cumulative
production exceeds 1.1 million barrels of oil. The small Freedom field (1 Trenton well) was also
discovered in 1954 and produced around 8500 barrels of oil.

The Albion-Scipio Field, a giant (>135 MMBO) TBR field located in Calhoun, Jackson and
Hillsdale Counties, Michigan, was discovered in 1957 (Ells, 1962; Davies, 2000). The Scipio
portion of the field discovery well was the Houseknecht No. 1 (Sec 10, T5S-R3W – Hillsdale
Co.) which was originally drilled for Devonian gas but proved dry. Based on consultation with a
local psychic, the well was then deepened and oil was encountered oil at 3,900 feet. The well
was completed and flowed 140 barrels of oil per day (BOPD) with “considerable” gas. The
Albion Field was discovered by the Rosenau No. 1 (Sec 23, T3S-R4W, Calhoun Co.) and
completed for 200 BOPD. Subsequent drilling extended production to the northwest and
southeast to become part of the Albion-Scipio-Pulaski Trend. Over 961 wells were drilled by
1986, although most wells were plugged and abandoned by the early 2000s. Currently, the
State of Michigan divides the Albion-Scipio-Pulaski Trend into seven sections for production
reporting purposes: Albion-Scipio 1 South through Albion Scipio 7 South. In 2006, renewed
drilling in and around the Albion-Scipio-Pulaski producing wells commenced based on high
resolution 3D seismic surveys. More than 70 new wells have been drilled opening several new
compartments in the old field. An additional 4.4 million barrels of oil has been produced through
the end of 2020 from these new wells.

Stoney Point Field (5 miles east and sub-parallel to Albion-Scipio) was not discovered until 25
years later in 1982 when the JEM Casler No. 1-30 (Sec 30, T4S-R2W, Jackson Co.)

encountered dolomite reservoir 115 ft into the Trenton at 3,910 feet. This well hit lost circulation
at 4,248 ft. Casing was set and the well was tested at 2,000 BOPD from perforations at 4,161 to

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               21

4,179 ft. The bottom hole pressure (BHP) drop never exceeded 3 psi and the well was put on
production at 220 BOPD. Two hundred and ten wells were drilled around the Stoney Point
Trend between 1983 and 1987. Seventy-five wells were oil and gas producers.

Estimated oil-in-place figures for these older fields are difficult to accurately calculate due to
difficulties in establishing a reliable porosity number as well as total reservoir volume, however,
Buehner and Davis (1969) estimated the trend to have 320 to 340 million barrels of oil originally
in place.

Since the discovery of new compartments in and around the Albion-Scipio-Pulaski Trend, the
application of 3D seismic surveying has provided for the discovery of at least 17 new fields, six
of which have already produced at least 200,000 barrels of oil.

Forty TBR fields are classified by the State of Michigan. Fourteen of these fields have produced
at least 100,000 barrels of oil, five fields have cumulative production greater than 1 million
barrels, two fields produced greater than 10 million barrels and one field greater than 100 million
barrels.

Figure 14. Map of major TBR Fields with greater than 100,000 barrels of oil cumulative production

Confining System

The ultimate top seal for TBR reservoirs is the stratigraphically, immediately overlying Utica
Shale. This shale is up to 400 ft thick and is predominantly a silty claystone or silty marl lithology
(Sattler and Barnes, 2017).

The Utica Shale has very low intrinsic porosity and permeability due to the compact fabric and
small size of the mineral grains (Sattler, 2015, Sattler and Barnes, 2017). Sattler (2015)

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 22

reported porosity of 0.77% and permeability of 0.003 millidarcies (mD) for the Thompson #1-30
well core at a depth of 2,277 ft. The use of mercury injection-capillary pressure (MICP) analysis
is a useful indicator of the capacity of fluids to pass through a rock or, conversely, to resist the
passage of fluid through the sample. MICP measures the pressure necessary to inject a fluid
(mercury [Hg]) into various sized pores in a sample. This provides a quantitative measurement
of pore throat sizes which directly affects permeability. Sattler (2015) analyzed five samples
from throughout Michigan using MICP (Figure 15). Sattler (2015) was analyzing the capacity of
the Utica Shale to act as a seal to retain CO2 that might be injected into underlying units and
therefore function as a seal for preventing vertical migration of CO2. The same principle applies
to being a seal for retaining hydrocarbons in the reservoir.

Figure 15. Capillary pressure versus percent Hg saturation of pore volume with injected mercury (Hg). For
most samples, pressures of Mercury of 1,000 to 10,000 psi are required to move through more than 20%
of the sample pore volume (from Sattler, 2015).

MICP analysis of selected samples of Utica Shale in Michigan show that pressures greater than
10,000 psi for Hg and 2,000 psi for CO2 are required to pass fluid through more than 70% of the
pore throats in these tested samples. That suggests that the Utica Shale is able to retain a thick
column of hydrocarbons in the TBR reservoir.

Multiple thin “shaley” beds are interbedded within the Trenton and Black River carbonate layers.
The Black River Shale, near the contact of the base of the Trenton with the top of the Black
River, is the best known (Hurley and Budros, 1990; Kolata et al., 1996), however some may
serve as minor internal seals acting as vertical barriers to fluid movement and dolomitizing
fluids. These beds may be true shales with compositions similar to the Utica Shale or they may
be volcanic ash beds (K-bentonites) such as the informally named “E”-shale near the top of the
Trenton Formation (Hurley and Budros, 1990). Feutz (2012) documented the mineralogy of

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  23

these thin shaley beds and their capacity to serve as vertical barriers to vertical movement of
dolomitizing fluids (Figure 16).

Figure 16. Core photo of 10-foot interval of core from the Marathon-Mann #6 well showing a 2.5
centimeter thick shale bed. Approximately four vertical feet of dolomite lies directly below the shale as
indicated by the purple highlights. All adjacent strata are undolomitized limestone (from Feutz, 2012).

Feutz (2012) suggests a model for dolomitization of selected intervals of the TBR in portions of
the formation that are laterally distanced from the main fault and fracture conduits that moved
the dolomitizing fluids up from deeper in the geologic section (Figure 17).

Additional seals are present in the very tight “cap” dolomite just beneath the Utica Shale and the
limestone lithologies of non-reservoir layers within the reservoir and as a lateral seal at the
margins of the reservoir. As an example, the “cap” dolomite in the ARCO-Conklin #1-31 core
has an average porosity of 2.0% and an average permeability of 0.01 mD in all three
dimensions over the top 25 ft of the Trenton Formation. These values are very similar to data
from a limestone core at the margin of the field.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         24

Figure 17. Two-dimensional theoretical model of Anderson Oil Co.-Whitaker #2 role of shale baffles in
helping dolomitize strata. Dolomitizing fluids invade permeable facies away from vertical faults, illustrating
the role heterogeneity of the original rock can influence fluid migration Red arrows = the migration of
hydrothermal fluids, purple = dolomitized zones, yellow = Facies 1, green = Facies 3, red-orange = Facies
4. Drawing is not to scale (from Feutz, 2012).

It is also possible to recognize the occurrence of these thin shale layers that may serve as
barriers to fluid migration by using modern wireline logs and borehole imaging logging (Figure
18).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       25

Figure 18. Shale baffle on wireline logs. Logs are from the West Bay Exploration-Kilbourn Farms #5-2
well in Rice Creek Field Northern portion of Albion- Scipio field area, paired with the respective borehole
image log illustrate what is interpreted to represent a thin shale seam (at 4,290 ft in the upper Trenton
Formation) with dolomite diagenetic alteration directly below (from Feutz, 2012).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       26

3.0 Task 2: Advanced Field Characterization &
 Machine Learning Based Data Integration
Task 2 involved a systematic geologic characterization of the TBR play in the SMB which
included the development of comprehensive datasets, advanced data analytics, risk
assessment, and piggyback field characterization. These activities aimed to address the
complex carbonate systems by evaluating the extent of fractures, dolomitization, facies, and
reservoir properties with the primary objective of informing the static and dynamic modeling,
field injection test, and providing input into the development strategy plan. The task was divided
into four subtasks:
 • Subtask 2.1 – Data Compilation, Review, and Analysis
 • Subtask 2.2 – Risk Assessment
 • Subtask 2.3 – Advanced Field Characterization
 • Subtask 2.4 – Integrated Physics-Based Machine Learning and Advanced Data Analytics

Several activities were planned and executed under Task 2 to meet the project objectives.
These are summarized in Table 4, including subtask, approach, and outcome. Figure 19
illustrates the flow of results between subtasks, which will lead to the development of a static
earth model (SEM) in Task 4. This began with the database compilation of geological and
geophysical data from multiple wells and fields. Three different analysis tracks included
geophysical (seismic), petrophysical (wireline logs and core), and vug and fractures (advanced
data). Each of the analyses produced results which fed into the ML database for the prediction
of electrofacies, permeability, and vugs and fractures. All information was fed into the static
earth modeling conducted in budget period 2, under Task 4.

3.1 Subtask 2.1: Data Compilation, Review, and Analysis
Data compilation, review, and analysis was one of the most time and effort intensive portions of
this study. Review of basic processes and results is provided below. Full reporting of this effort
is contained in the Task 2 Final Report that was delivered to DOE in June 2021.

Database Development

Numerous data types were compiled and integrated from public and private databases in
collaboration with project partners Innova Exploration, Inc. and Western Michigan University.
Datasets, detailed in Table 5, included historical well records, reservoir and production data,
digital wireline logs (LAS), raster logs (images of wireline logs), well header and formation top
data, image logs, core data, and seismic data. Additional data were collected by NETL which
was comprised of CT scans, high-resolution x-ray fluorescence (XRF), and high-resolution
velocity data for five wells in the study area. Overall, data were compiled for 607 wells in the
Albion-Scipio (AS) and analogous fields (Napoleon, Stoney Point, Northville) and was integrated
into a comprehensive database in Geographix. A map of all wells in the database are shown in
Figure 20.

Formation Tops

Formation tops were provided by project partners (Innova Exploration and Western Michigan
University) for the AS field and Napoleon Field, and tops were quality checked to ensure
consistency. Tops for the formations of interest were picked for wells in the nearby Stoney Point
field, which focused on wells with good spatial coverage and quality of well logs. Guidelines
were developed for consistent formation top picks for the Utica Shale, Trenton, and Black River
Shale. The Utica was picked at the base of the first consistently high gamma ray (Figure 21a).
The Trenton was picked at the base of the high gamma ray, where is drastically decreases
indicating clean carbonate (Figure 21b). The Black River Shale was picked occasionally as
many logs ended above this. It was picked where the gamma ray spikes. The formation top data
compilation and top picking processes resulted in a total of 579 wells with formation tops in the
database.

Figure 20. Map of all wells compiled in Albion-Scipio and analogous fields

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  31

Figure 21. Example formation tops for the Utica Shale (A) and the Trenton (B). The top of Utica in this
well comes in at about 3285 ft measured depth as progressive downward transition to higher gamma ray
values; the top of the Trenton comes in at about 3650 ft measured depth as a sharp transition from very
high to very low gamma ray values.

Core Analysis

Core measured properties obtained from physical whole core and/or sidewall cores are critical
information across multiple activities as depicted in Figure 22. The data were used for wireline
log correlation, reservoir characterization, permeability prediction, and vug and fracture
characterization, and integrated into the 3D modeling efforts including seismic analysis and
property modeling.

Analysis of core measured properties were compiled for 48 wells in the Albion-Scipio, Stoney
Point, and Napoleon fields, and wells in nearby small fields and wildcat wells. Data included
porosity, permeability, fluid saturations, grain and bulk density, and flags for facies, vugs,
fractures, and lithology. A map of wells with routine core analysis is shown in Figure 22.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  32

Figure 22. Locations of wells with routine core analysis (blue).

Anomalous data points can occur where analyses were conducted on a section of core with a
fracture or large and/or isolated vug, resulting in porosity and permeability values that are
outliers compared to other values in the same intervals. Damaged sample materials, poor
analytical work, and/or outdated techniques are other possible sources of data quality problems
to be considered. These data points were reviewed to determine whether the measurements
are reliable. Porosity and permeability points were compared to fracture and vug flags. In cases
where anomalously high values corresponded with isolated vugs or fractures, the points were
removed from the quality dataset.

Core Workshop

A core workshop was held at Western Michigan University (WMU) including team members
from Battelle, WMU, Innova Exploration, Inc., and University of Alaska (now BEG) (Figure 23).
Using previous work completed by WMU, facies were linked from whole core to wireline log
signatures to ultimately predict the location and extent of each facies throughout the field using
data analytics. WMU studied seven unique lithofacies in the TBR, the associated core-
measured reservoir properties, and the extent of dolomitization as associated to the facies.
Resources available at WMU and the Michigan Geological Repository for Research and
Education (MGRRE) include about 60 wells with TBR core samples and over 1,300 wells with
digitized logs in the Albion-Scipio area. The workshop served as a knowledge and data sharing

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             33

opportunity and allowed the Project Team to observe and discuss trends in core and log data
from TBR reservoirs in Albion-Scipio, Napoleon, Stoney Point, and Northville fields. Cores
evaluated during the workshop are shown in Figure 24.

Figure 23. Photograph of workshop attendees observing a TBR whole core.

Figure 24. Map of 12 wells with core viewed during the workshop.

Lithology

Dolostone was the dominant lithology observed, making up ~71% of the data (excluding
unknowns). This was followed by limestone and the fewest observations in dolomitic-limestone
(Figure 25).

 Lithology

 dolomitic-limestone dolostone limestone unknown
Figure 25. Pie chart of lithology types observed in cored intervals.

Texture

Shale is commonly used as a flag or classification for lithology, but due to its occurrence as thin
beds or traces, it has been grouped in the texture category to represent rock texture with low
porosity. There were 1,079 total observations with texture evidence, of which fine-crystalline
was the most common (56%). This was followed by shale (26%), medium crystalline (16%), and
coarse crystalline (1%).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 35

Dolomite lithology was dominated by fine-crystalline textures with the highest number of
medium-crystalline and shale observations. Dolomitic-limestone was fine to medium crystalline
with one occurrence of shale. Limestone was dominated by fine crystalline with few
observations of medium and shale. Coarse crystalline occurred in intervals with unknown
lithology (Figure 26).

Figure 26. Pie chart of texture/matrix type (left), and bar chart of texture type by lithology/rock type.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         36

Features
Fractures were observed 135 times over the cored intervals. Fractures dominantly occurred in
dolostone (84%) (Figure 27). Few occurrences were noted in dolomitic-limestone (4%),
limestone (7%), and unknown lithologies (5%).

Figure 27. Pie chart of fracture observations by lithology.

Vugs were observed 1,325 times over the cored intervals and were dominantly present in
dolostone lithology (89%). Only 1% of observations were in dolomitic-limestone, zero in
limestone, and remaining 31% in unknown lithology (Figure 28). The majority of vugs observed
were pinpoint to small, followed by medium to large. A small percentage were pervasive (4%)
which occurred in dolostone lithology.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                        37

Figure 28. Pie charts of vugs by type and lithology (upper), and vug type by lithology (lower).

Facies

There were only 806 observations available for WMU developed facies (Figure 29) for the cored
intervals. Facies 2 had the greatest number of observations (46%) closely followed by facies 3
(39%). The remaining facies had few observations occurring from <1 to 6%.

Porosity and Permeability

Porosity and permeability values were measured for more than 2,800 samples from the cored
intervals in 48 wells. Overall, the values ranged from 0.1 to 20.7% porosity and 0.01 to 939 mD
permeability. Individually, many wells followed a similar distribution of values, with a subset
ranging into higher values (Figure 30). The porosity to permeability relationship was poor due to
broad scatter, likely influenced by lithology and secondary porosity changes (Figure 31). The
dataset was further explored using data analytics and ML.

Dolostone lithology had the highest average porosity at 3.3% with a range from 0.3 to 12.6%.
The resulting permeability ranged from 0.09 to 772 mD with an average of 8.5 mD (Figure 32).
Dolomitic limestone had a slightly lower average at 2.9% with bi-modal peaks near 1% and 4%.
The limestone had the lowest average at 1.5% and the narrowest distribution.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                         41

The core analysis activities aimed to catalog and characterize the TBR reservoir through use of
physically measured properties. The team began with a core workshop to familiarize themselves
with the geology and heterogeneity of the system. During this time, the team selected core to be
sent to NETL for advanced scanning including CT scans, XRD, and high-resolution velocity. A
set of flags was generated for the cores based on their physical descriptions to capture changes
in lithology, textures, secondary porosity features, and facies. Porosity and permeability were
evaluated for all wells to determine a trend for use in petrophysical analyses. In summary, the
TBR was observed to be highly heterogeneous which varied from tight limestone to porous
dolomite, with varying occurrences of fractures and vugs.

Petrophysical Analysis

The goal of the petrophysical analysis of the wells across the fields is to characterize the
reservoir (TBR) to better understand the distributions of porosity, permeability, thickness, and
facies. These characteristics will later be integrated into a 3D conceptualization of the Albion-
Scipio field and used to understand the feasibility of CO2-EOR in the TBR play.

Approach

The petrophysical analysis approach involved multiple steps including zone definition, type well
selection, log preparation, core data preparation, final log computation, and zone calculations.
Reservoir intervals, or facies, were defined for each well and later refined through a series of
cross sections to ensure consistency. The results were mapped in a series of bubble maps and
statistical plots.

Zone Definition

A zone is defined as an interval of rock with a top and a base. For the purposes of the
petrophysical analyses, the zone was defined as the top of the Trenton and the base of the
Black River. In many cases, the wellbore did not fully penetrate the Black River due to the oil-
water contact. In these instances, the base of the zone is defined as the last wireline log
measurement. When calculations are dependent on thickness or represent the thickness of the
zone, it is important to note that the calculations only capture what is logged and not the true
thickness of the TBR.
Formation tops were provided by project partners (Innova Exploration and Western Michigan
University). Tops were quality checked to ensure consistency.

Type Well Definition

Several wells were selected from each field (Albion-Scipio, Stoney Point, and Napoleon) to be
designated as the type wells for the study. Figure 33 shows the geographic location of the wells
used. The wells were selected for their data availability, with preference given to wells having
core data. Additionally, the lithology and features were considered to ensure the wells captured
the expected geologic variability. When available, these rock properties were compiled from
driller’s logs and previous studies.

fields.

similar values in the Albion-Scipio and Napoleon fields (Figure 34). Cap dolomite thickness was
similar between the three fields with Albion-Scipio field having slightly lower than Napoleon and
Stoney Point. Napoleon field had more regular occurrences of thicker cap dolomite than the
other fields (Figure 35). The total thickness of dolomitic intervals was greatest in the Albion-
Scipio field while Napoleon and Stoney point were similar. Dolomite was thickest in the northern
part of the Albion-Scipio field, southern part of the Stoney Point field, and southern part of the

The net thickness was higher in the Napoleon field than observed in Albion-Scipio or Stoney

the lowest values and fewer occurrences greater than 20%. The northern portion of the Albion-

The three fields have noticeable differences in reservoir quality. The number of dry holes in the
Albion-Scipio field skews the results to be more reflective of non-reservoir intervals. The
Napoleon field was developed using modern approaches and technology which resulted in
fewer total wells, and fewer percentage of wells in non-reservoir intervals. Stoney Point has
poorer reservoir quality across multiple attributes than the other two fields.

Vug and Fracture Characterization

Vugs and fractures play a critical role in the reservoir development of the HTD reservoirs in
Michigan. Secondary porosity, such as vugs and fractures, contributes significantly to the total
porosity, permeability, and storage potential throughout the reservoirs. However, these variable
features have not been fully understood or predicted. The goal of the vug and fracture flag
analysis was to detail the distribution of vug and fracture networks observed in image logs and
CT scans and incorporate results into a ML/data analytics workflow for enhanced classification
and prediction of reservoir property distributions. ML techniques, such as deep learning (e.g.,
convolutional neural network) and causality-based algorithms (e.g., Bayesian Networks) will be
implemented to identify features of interest such as faults and fractures within the 3D space of
the seismic volume and predict features where well data are missing. Additionally, key reservoir
facies and characteristics will be predicted and mapped in the 3D space using regression and
classification techniques.
CT scans from five wells and image logs from 10 wells were selected across the study area
(Figure 39). The objectives of this task were to:

 • Compile a dataset of CT scans and image logs that represent the variability
 in lithology, secondary porosity features across Albion-Scipio and
 analogous fields,
 • Observe secondary porosity features (i.e., vugs and fractures) and trends visible
 in the core and log data,
 • Quantify secondary porosity features in CT data using the CTViewer,
 • Develop vug and fracture flags from core and log data that will be used to train
 selected wells with ML and data analysis (DA) techniques.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             46

Figure 39. Map of study area wells selected with CT data, CBIL, and both.

CT Data Overview

Detailed two-dimensional (2D) and 3D image analysis of CT scans can provide the necessary
data for quantifying and predicting secondary porosity and understanding trends in rock
properties. CT scans are performed by emitting an x-ray source on a helical trajectory around
longitudinal sections of cores to derive 3D images of density variations for each core volume.
Sample densities are expressed in gray-scale color variations such that light-colored, higher-
density areas can be distinguished from darker-colored, low-density areas to identify internal
fractures, pore distributions/geometries, and silicate versus carbonate minerals.

In the past, CT scans were used qualitatively to interpret characteristics in the whole core for
each 2D cross-sectional slice via 2D images and descriptions of diagenetic features. The
Battelle-developed CT scan analysis tool, CTViewer, processes raw CT scan data to produce
not only 2D slices, but also a 3D visualization of the data. Specific rock density ranges can be
isolated using CTViewer to quantify the percent of the features of interest in a given interval of
core. The tool has provided a platform where CT scan data can be analyzed and quantified in
3D to better characterize the formation rocks of interest.

The raw CT data can be supplemented by various logs and core analysis providing data on
physical properties such as lithology, composition, and increases and decreases in density and
porosity. Images of CT scans are also provided and can be useful for conducting qualitative
descriptions and analysis of the core and CT logs. Vug concentration and size distribution,
fracture sizes and orientations, occurrences of salt and anhydrite plugging, core density, core
porosity, and major changes in lithologies are all major features that can be interpreted from CT
scan images. Matrix features such as clasts, laminations, bedding, and features of biologic
origin can also be observed with qualitative analysis.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              47

Battelle worked with MGRRE and Innova to select 124 ft of TBR core from Albion-Scipio,
Napoleon, Stoney Point, and Northville fields. Table 7 details the intervals of CT scan data
available for each of the five wells across the TBR fields. Well locations are shown in Figure 39.

Table 7. List of core intervals selected for additional data collection.

NETL conducted core analyses for the five wells, including CT scans and multi-sensor core
logging (with X-ray diffraction, P-wave velocity, gamma density, and magnetic susceptibility).
Cores were boxed and shipped from MGRRE to NETL for data collection. NETL acquired the
CT scan data after core samples were inventoried and loaded into core barrels. Each barrel was
scanned once by the low-resolution medical CT scanning machine, resulting in DICOM and tiff
stacks with an approximate resolution of 0.1 mm. Concurrent to industrial CT scanning, NETL
performed multi-sensor core logging analyses on competent samples to produce raw XRF, p-
wave, and gamma density measurements.

Approach

One of the key goals of the CT scan analysis was to characterize the reservoir heterogeneity
with regards to vugs and fractures in the TBR using recovered core. Detailed 2D and 3D image
analysis of CT scans can provide the necessary data for quantifying secondary porosity and
fracture porosity and predicting reservoir quality. CT scans were collected from core samples
from five wells within TBR reservoirs with a variety of reservoir characteristics to ensure a
representative dataset. The Battelle-developed CT scan analysis tool, CTViewer, was used to
processes raw CT scan data and conduct the vug and fracture analysis.

After the raw data were acquired, they were processed using CTViewer for three major features:
1) void space (i.e., vugs and fractures), 2) matrix, and 3) anhydrite plugs. Specific density
ranges can be isolated using the tool to conduct image segmentation and quantify features of
interest. Thresholds for each feature of interest were identified using the slice-by-slice 2D CT
number images (lower CT number values/darker shades are lower density material and higher
values/lighter shades are higher density material). CTViewer determines the percentage of each
feature in every slice of data and generates a percent threshold versus depth curve that can be
exported into Excel. Figure 40 shows an example of the CTViewer interface with a 3-ft section
of core loaded and segmented. The features and their corresponding density thresholds,
derived from the CT number in grayscale, are listed in the table in the upper right corner of the
interface, and the CT image is overlaid with the feature-segmented layer. Below the table is the

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  48

percent of each feature by depth (left) and a segmented 2D slice down the middle of the core
(right).

Figure 40. The CTViewer 2D interface with feature-segmented overlay on CT data.

The percent threshold curves from CTViewer require manual quality assurance/quality control
(QA/QC) to flag erroneous data associated with missing core, the endcaps for each core
interval, and drilling-induced fractures to create a secondary porosity curve. During the QA/QC
process, flag curves to be used in subsequent tasks were created for vugs and fractures. Two
binary flag curves were developed for each well to indicate whether vugs were present and
whether fractures were present. Additionally, zero to three fracture curves were developed for
each well to indicate if a fracture was present and if the fracture was natural, induced, or if both
were present at a depth point. These features were detected and validated using the exported
percent threshold curves, the CTViewer interface, and core images. Once the curves were
finalized, they were ready to incorporate into statistical correlation with well log and seismic data
to aid in training wells to predict reservoir characteristics where data is not available.

Full CT core analysis is included in the Task 2 Final Report, included as an attachment to this
document.

CT Scanning Conclusions

Battelle’s CTViewer allowed for qualitative and quantitative CT data analysis of vugs and
fractures, resulting in the creation of several vug and fracture flags. These flags assisted with
ML and data analytics efforts in subsequent tasks to predict secondary porosity features across
TBR reservoirs. The key observations of the CT data analysis are as follows:
 • The dolomitized core samples from Nerreter #2, Rowe #A-2, and West Bay #1-22
 showed more vugs and natural fractures than the limestone wells, resulting in relatively
 higher secondary porosities in quality checked data.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               49

 • The limestone wells, Casler #5-30 and Wooden #5, showed few to no vugs and natural
 fractures compared to the dolomitized wells, resulting in relatively lower secondary
 porosities in quality checked data. These wells, however, did show high levels of
 induced fracturing. Core analysis provided evidence of moderate matrix porosity.
 • The 3D rendering tool was able to detect smaller porosity features not included in flags
 for both dolomitized and limestone wells. Many of these small features could represent
 pinpoint porosity or could be associated with larger vugs and fractures where the
 CTViewer did not detect connectivity.
 • The CTViewer’s ability to differentiate between void space within the core (i.e., porosity)
 and on the edge of the core was hindered by effects of core sizes and/or core
 placement, resulting in difficulty differentiating between vugs and fractures during 3D
 rendering. Relative secondary porosity curves were created for these wells. Analysis of
 intensity and size distributions were conducted with uncertainty and supplemented with
 qualitative analysis.

Image Log Analysis

Acoustic and resistivity-derived image logs provide high-resolution, 360-degree wellbore images
that can be used to derive structural and sedimentary features for detailed reservoir
characterization. Planar features such as faults, fractures, and bedding can be identified, as well
as stress fields, soft sediment deformation, and pore spaces. Image logs are instrumental in
identifying and characterizing vugular porosity in carbonates, as these pore types are difficult to
discern from other basic and advanced log signatures. Image logs can also be used to
differentiate breakout and drilling-induced fractures from natural formation features.

A set of image logs was used from Albion-Scipio and Napoleon fields to characterize the secondary
porosity features (vugs and fractures), and to develop flags for machine learning. If successful, the
flags would be integrated with the basic wireline log data to develop a predictive model.

Data Set and Processing

Image log data were limited in the Albion-Scipio field due to the age of the wells. Two were
available from non-producing horizontal wells, which cross over from limestone to dolostone and
capture changes in the secondary porosity. Napoleon field had 27 image logs collected, most of
which did not undergo advanced processing. The Napoleon image logs were reviewed and
ranked to identify the top priority wells to be processed by the original vendor. Processing is
important to enhance the resolution of the images for more accurate analysis of the secondary
porosity (Figure 41).

Well records and mud logs were reviewed for each well to describe the type of well, lithology,
and features observed. Wells which produced/producing oil, dolomitic, and had noticeable vugs
and fractures were given a rating of 1, or high priority. Priority 2 was given to wells which had
dolostone present, but could also have limestone, and produced oil. There may or may not have
been visible vugs and fractures. Priority 3, the lowest priority, was given to non-producing
limestone wells. In total, 11 wells were assigned priority 1, 8 wells were given priority 2, and 8
wells were given priority 3 (Figure 42). The results were reviewed with partners to down select,
as highlighted in Figure 42 with red circles. Additionally, an image log was collected on the
McCann 1-20 wells (producing/monitoring well for field injection test) and was used in this
analysis. Altogether, nine wells were used in the analysis, and are summarized in Table 8.
