Approach

Static and dynamic imaging of the logs was done to determine vugular porosity, bedding,
fractures, and dip of these features. Each log was analyzed per foot for the entire logged
interval. Identified vugs were evaluated by the percentage of vugular porosity (Figure 43) and
indicated as 0-5 according to the percentage of vuggy occurrences at the identified depth.
Fractures were classified as no fractures, open fractures, partially filled, filled, induced, or
unknown (Figure 44).

Figure 43. Vug classification based on percentage of vugular occurrences at depth. Unknown (5) indicates
a data gap or image distortion.

Figure 44. Fracture classification based on open, partially filled, filled, or induced fractures. Unknown (5)
indicates a data gap or image distortion interval.

Quality image data were determined by evaluating the static and dynamic data as well as the
borehole conditions (caliper and gamma ray) of the well. If the static and dynamic image data
was clear and the caliper and gamma ray were consistent (borehole conditions) then the image
log data were considered adequate for the vug and fracture log analysis.

Full image log analysis is included in the Task 2 Final Report, included as an attachment to this
document.

Summary

Fractures in the Trenton are primarily partially opened. Analysis of fracture occurrences by
depth across the wells indicates that they typically start at approximately 100 ft below the top of
the Trenton. However, the Lantis 1-29 and Whalen Byron et al 1-16 wells have consistent
fractures that start from the base of the Utica through the top of the Trenton formation (Figure
45). Swank and Hilden wells have little to no fractures that occur in the Trenton. Wells with
fractures are primarily partially filled with little to no filled occurrences (Figure 46). Indications of
open fractures are low with the majority occurring in the Utica. The image log measurements did
not cover the entire Utica in the wells, so the assessment of the Utica is limited. The Curtis,
McCann, Hilden, West Bay, and Whalen image logs all indicate one or more open fractures that
measure approximately 0.5 inches in width between 40 to 150 ft from the top of the Trenton
formation (Figure 47).

Figure 45. Fracture occurrences in the Curtis, Lantis, Swank, and Whalen Byron wells showing fractures
at the base of the Utica and top of the Trenton formations.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                   54

Figure 46. Fracture occurrences across all image log analysis wells, indicating open, partially filled, filled,
and induced fractures.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                          55

Figure 47. Open fractures at the base of the Utica in the Hilden and West Bay wells.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                    56

Vugs in the Trenton are primarily between 10% and 20% and occur approximately 100 to 200 ft
below the top of the Trenton (Figure 48). Most of the wells have few occurrences of greater than
40% vuggy occurrences in the wells. However, vugs occur throughout the Swank and Hilden
wells which have little to no fractures in the well. Additionally, wells with a greater occurrence of
vuggy percentage over 10% have fewer fractures in the Trenton.

Figure 48. Vuggy occurrences across all image log analysis wells, indicating percentage of vuggy
occurrences in the well.

Two advanced data sets were analyzed to characterize the vugs and fractures in the TBR. This
included the use of medical CT scans which provided a 3D representation of selected cores.

Thresholds were defined to isolate void spaces and quantify their occurrences. The second
dataset included image logs from the Albion-Scipio and Napoleon fields which provided a 2D
representation of the logged interval. The image logs were analyzed to identify and characterize
fractures and vugs, following a defined classification scheme. Overall, vugs and fracture
frequency occurred, with a higher concentration in dolomitic intervals.

Seismic Analysis, Albion-Scipio Field

This section describes the preliminary seismic analysis of the Albion-Scipio field, which was
completed ahead of drilling the McCann well. Seismic analysis at the Lee 26 field, completed by
the University of Texas Bureau of Economic Geology, is reviewed below.

Project field partner Innova Exploration, Inc. previously collected a high-resolution 3D seismic
volume over 13 mi2 in the Albion-Scipio field, covering the planned injection site. A preliminary
seismic interpretation was conducted in collaboration between University of Alaska, Innova
Exploration Inc., and Battelle to lay the groundwork for more advanced seismic analysis and
database integration.

Approach

The project team first developed a project database including wireline logs and 3D seismic data.
The wells were linked to the seismic through a well-tie analysis, which correlates seismic
horizons in time to formation tops in depth. Next, key seismic horizons were identified and
picked throughout the 3D volume to map the extent and changes in formations in the time
domain. Seismic attributes were then explored to determine which attributes might best
represent reservoir changes. Finally, a seismic inversion was conducted to invert amplitude to
acoustic impedance for a preliminary analysis of reservoir changes. Additionally, a fault and
fracture analysis was conducted to better understand the structure in the study area.

Seismic Well Tie

A seismic well-tie is important to recognize the geologic horizons on seismic data. This is done
by generating a synthetic seismogram from the wireline log data. A synthetic seismogram is the
result of forward modeling the seismic response based on formation properties such as density
and velocity. The Travis 1-18 well in Figure 49 shows an example of the results. Conventional
logs were used to generate a synthetic seismogram to recognize the geologic horizons. An
Ormsby filter (10/15-50/70-70/90 Hz) was designed and convoluted with reflectivity derived from
the density and sonic logs to generate a synthetic seismogram. In this example, no phase
rotation was applied. The results show that the Trenton has the strongest positive peak, which is
observed in the seismic data. The Trenton reflector was used as the key marker to reference
and pick the overlying Utica Shale and underlying Black River, Glenwood, and basement
horizons. Figure 50 illustrates the geologic horizons traced based on the correlation to wireline
log data.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             58

Figure 49. Example results of the seismic well tie for the Travis 1-18 well showing log responses on the
left and synthetic seismogram on the right.

Figure 50. Example horizons traced using the results from the seismic well tie.

Seismic Horizon Interpretation

Four seismic reflectors, or horizons, were mapped which included the Utica, Trenton, Black
River, and Precambrian Basement. The Trenton was a recognizable reflector due to its strong
peak and overall lateral continuity. The subsurface above the Utica seemed laterally
homogeneous based on continuous seismic reflectors. However, the TBR interval was complex
and showed both vertical and lateral complexity, likely due to the presence of vugs and
fractures. Figure 51 illustrates picked horizons on a west-to-east line through the 3D volume.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        59

Figure 51. Example horizons picked along a W-E line through the 3D seismic volume.

The Utica Shale is shallower in the southern portion of the volume and deepens the north and is
continuous across the region, making it an effective confining unit (Figure 52). The Trenton
horizon follows a similar trend, shallower in the south and deeper in the north. However, there is
more heterogeneity due to the changes in reservoir properties and the influence of tectonic
structures (Figure 52). The Black River and Glenwood show similar trends and lateral
heterogeneities, likely due to faults (Figure 53).

Figure 52. Horizon maps of the Utica (left) and Trenton (right) showing overall trend and heterogeneity.
Cool colors are deeper, hot colors are shallower.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        60

Figure 53. Horizon maps of the Back River (left) and Glenwood (right) showing overall trend and
heterogeneity. Cool colors are deeper, hot colors are shallower.

Attributes

Seismic attributes are mathematical quantities, geometries, or statistical values derived from
seismic data using a set of equations or transformations. Extracting attributes from seismic data
allows geoscientists to understand structural, lithological, mineralogical, and other
characteristics of the subsurface that are too subtle to be evaluated via simple seismic
reflection. There are several types of seismic attributes, of which more than 12 were applied to
the seismic volume. This included root mean square (RMS) amplitude, instantaneous phase,
frequency, curvature, variance, sweetness, envelope, and ant tracking. Complex trace
attributes, such as RMS amplitude, instantaneous phase, and frequency are used to visualize
changes in reflection characteristics which could be related to lithology and/or porosity changes.
Geometric attributes, such as curvature and variance, reveal structural and stratigraphic
changes such as faults. Ant tracking is an additional method that can be applied to the original
volume or computed attribute to highlight structural features in more detail. During the
preliminary analysis, three attributes were identified which highlighted heterogeneity in the TBR
including RMS amplitude, instantaneous frequency, and variance (Ming and Zhao, 2014).

The RMS displays amplitude values. This attribute can be used to highlight hydrocarbon
indicators or higher porosity intervals. The resulting RMS attribute shows areas of high values
towards the north, middle, and scattered throughout the south (Figure 54). In the middle of the
field is a structural feature known as the Pulaski break, which is along the high RMS amplitude.
Additionally, known faults coincide with the north and south areas of high amplitude. The areas
of high amplitude extend away from the faults which could indicate zones of dolomite and/or
porosity.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               61

Figure 54. Results of the RMS amplitude showing areas of high amplitude along known structural
features. High amplitudes are yellow; low amplitudes are red.

Instantaneous frequency is the time rate of change of the instantaneous phase. The attribute is
a known indicator for low impedance beds, fracture zone, and hydrocarbons. The frequency is
lowest in the northern section of the seismic, which correlates with the area highlighted by RMS.
However, it does not capture the larger structural features observed at the Pulaski break and
the division between the North and Central Scipio pools (Figure 55).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              62

Figure 55. Results of the Instantaneous frequency attribute showing low frequency to the north. High
amplitudes are cool colors, low amplitudes are warm colors.

The variance attribute measures the similarity between waveforms or adjacent traces over given
lateral and vertical windows. It can image discontinuity, such as that caused by faulting. The
variance is highest towards the middle of the field near the Pulaski break and in scattered
intervals throughout the rest of the volume (Figure 56).

Figure 56. Variance attribute showing areas of greater variance. Grayscale colors are low variance, warm
colors are high variance.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    63

Preliminary Inversion

Seismic inversion is the process of inverting the seismic amplitude traces to acoustic
impedance. Acoustic impedance is a characteristic which represents changes in rock properties
more distinctly than regular seismic amplitude. It is the multiplication of acoustic velocity and
density of the rocks, resulting in highlighting changes in lithology and porosity. Figure 57 shows
the acoustic impedance for the Black River in 3D, with areas of low acoustic impedance or
suggested higher porosity (red).

Figure 57. Acoustic impedance of the Black River showing areas of low impedance in red which could be
associated with high porosity.

Fault and Fracture Identification

The southern Michigan area has known complex, wrench fault systems. There are primary fault
stems that propagate upward from the basin into the TBR, with several branches coming out of
the main faults. Manual fault interpretations were conducted along with the use of ant tracking to
pick smaller features. Figure 58 illustrates interpreted faults on a west-to-east section and
Figure 59 on a north-to-south section with potential sag fill. At least 50 faults were interpreted
during this analysis. Few large faults originated in the basement, but most were smaller faults
that branched from the main faults. The majority of faults are oriented in the northwest-
southeast direction (Figure 60).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                64

Figure 58. Interpreted seismic W-E line showing complex fault system.

Figure 59. Interpreted seismic N-S line showing complex fault system.

Battelle | May 2026                                                     65

Figure 60. Interpreted faults on ant-track slice showing a series of faults throughout the seismic (left), and
3D view of faults (right).

Preliminary analyses revealed the complex structural nature of the Albion-Scipio field. Attribute
analyses are a promising application to the high-quality seismic volume and will be used to
correlate attributes with changes in rock properties such as porosity, vugs, and fractures. The
results will be integrated into a 3D static earth model to better predict and understand the
changes in the reservoir.

Seismic Analysis, Lee Field

Seismic analysis at the Lee 26 field was completed by Dr. Shuvajit Bhattacharya at the
University of Texas Bureau of Economic Geology, using an 8 mi2 3D seismic dataset provided
to the project by West Bay Exploration along with two wells used to generate synthetic seismic
ties.

Approach

The project team first developed a project database including wireline logs and 3D seismic data.
Key seismic horizons were identified and picked throughout the 3D volume to map the extent
and changes in formations in the time domain. Next, the wells were linked to the seismic
through a well-tie analysis, which correlates seismic horizons in time to formation tops in depth.
Seismic attribute analysis was explored to determine which attributes might best represent
reservoir changes. Finally, a seismic inversion was conducted to invert amplitude to acoustic
impedance for a preliminary analysis of reservoir changes. Additionally, a fault and fracture
analysis was conducted to better understand the structure in the study area.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         66

Seismic Horizon Interpretation

Utica, Trenton, Black River, and Glenwood surfaces were interpreted across the seismic volume
(Figure 61) and QA/QC was performed using various interpolation approaches (Figure 62), which
were also used to complete the horizons in areas of the volume with high complexity or noise.

Figure 61. Interpreted seismic section showing interpreted surfaces. Note the Trenton Formation top has a
strong peak on seismic reflection (in black), whereas the Utica Formation top has a generally negative
peak (trough) characteristic (in white).

Figure 62. An example of the Utica surface interpolation and clean up. The first image on the left shows
the map before processing, and the one on the right shows the map after processing.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        67

Seismic Inversion

The surfaces and well data were then used to generate a low-frequency model of the
subsurface, followed by a post-stack seismic inversion to derive P-impedance (Figures 63 and
64). Figure 64 shows the relationship between inversion-based impedance and log-based
impedance. One well was used for training and another for blind testing in the inversion
process. The relationship between well-log-based porosity and P-impedance was used to
convert the seismic-derived impedance to porosity (Figure 65).

Figure 63. An example of a single well-based inversion. The blue curve in the 1st track shows original
impedance, and the red curve shows the seismic-inverted impedance. The error, the difference between
the seismic trace and synthetic, is minimal (~0.07).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  68

Figure 64. P-impedance from seismic inversion in the Lee field. There are two wells inserted in the cross-
section. Inversion-based impedance matches with well-log-based impedance. Impedance curves in both
wells and seismic have the same scale and color pattern for

Figure 65. Comparison between well-log-based impedance and seismic inversion-based impedance,
showing overall good match.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     69

Seismic Porosity Estimation

The seismic-based to log-based impedance relationship was used to produce the final porosity
volume from seismic inversion in the Lee field. Because the porosity log curves provided did not
correct for clay bound water effect in the Utica Shale, artificially high porosity of >10-16 porosity
units (p.u.) was displayed in some places, whereas the Trenton Formation showed negligible
porosity in places. Importantly, the TBR section was parameterized in the seismic inversion
process, while the Utica overlying section was not. Therefore, a filter was applied in the Utica
and above to address its apparently high porosities relative to the TBR section. This helped
better visualize reservoir property variation in the TBR section.

Figure 66. Estimated porosity from seismic inversion in the Lee field.

The possibility of porosity prediction from multiple seismic attributes was also explored using a
probabilistic neural network (PNN). Multiple seismic attributes were used as inputs to the PNN
model, such as average frequency, coherence, and seismic data decomposed in different
frequency bands, as well as inversion-based P-impedance to predict porosity (target). However,
the results from PNN-based porosity prediction were poor after several iterations, with a
correlation coefficient of 0.63.

Lee Field Seismic Analysis Conclusions

This subsurface characterization workflow revealed the structural and reservoir complexities in
the TBR in both fields, which are critical for understanding potential sweet spots for CO2-EOR
deployment. Seismic inversion-based impedance highlighted the potential reservoirs. It is
anticipated that the workflow and lessons learned will lay the groundwork for future oil and gas
and CO2-EOR-related work in these HTD reservoirs in Michigan.

3.2 Subtask 2.2: Risk Assessment
McCann Well Pre-drill Risk Assessment

For the McCann well drilling, a preliminary site-based risk assessment was completed to identify
potential items that may affect project performance, safety, and/or the environment for a

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               70

chemically enhanced CO2-EOR project in the Albion-Scipio field. The risk analysis addressed
overall project performance risk factors, wellbore integrity concerns, and the identification of
potential geohazards in the area such as seismicity or environmental concerns. The preliminary
assessment focused on the immediate area being considered for the project test at a scale of
approximately 15,000 metric tons CO2 injection. A summary of the risk assessment objectives is
presented in Table 9. An expanded assessment was also completed to evaluate risk factors
related to larger scale application of the EOR technology for the larger Albion-Scipio trend in
southern Michigan. Table 10 summarizes conclusions of the preliminary risk assessment.

Overall, no high-impact items were identified in the risk analysis. The caprock over the Trenton
reservoir is thick and impermeable, which provides containment for upward migration of injected
chemically altered CO2. Some fractures near the contact of the Trenton and Utica rock
formations should be considered. No widespread or prevalent well construction or well plugging
deficiencies were present in Albion-Scipio wells. However, the risk assessment did find that
some wells had incomplete records or a limited number of smaller cement plugs. For example,
three wells were identified in the project area with marginal plugging and abandonment records
that may need consideration if they intersect the reservoir. A more detailed inspection of well
conditions and/or testing of selected legacy wells may be required for expanded EOR
operations. Subsurface stress conditions indicate a mainly strike-slip regime with stress
magnitudes typical for the general region. If very high injection pressures are used for EOR
operations, the fractured nature of the CO2-EOR reservoir presents risks related to EOR
performance and geomechanical deformation. Results of the risk assessment may be used to
guide site characterization, injection testing design, monitoring, and evaluation of further
applications of chemically enhanced CO2-EOR along the Albion Scipio, and potentially across
the TBR play in southern Michigan.

3.3 Subtask 2.3: Advanced Field Characterization
Fischhaber 2-35 Wellbore Integrity Evaluation

Ahead of the CO2 injection campaign, to ensure that there was no possibility of underground
CO2 leakage from the operation, Battelle and West Bay concluded that it was necessary to
evaluate the condition of the Fischhaber 2-35 wellbore. To understand the current corrosion
status of the Fischhaber 2-35 casing, DarkVision’s Hades-R and Hades-T microacoustic casing
analysis tools were run, which provide sub-millimetric radial measurements of casing thickness
and shape (Figure 67). These tools were run from the base of the well at 4,580 to 2,925 ft
measured depth. Six casing joints with Class 1 or Class 2 features were noted (Figure 68), and
based on DarkVision’s analysis, none of these features constitutes a significant risk for later
corrosion breach or reduces the well’s maximum operating pressure enough to warrant concern.
One casing joint was noted with full breach; these are the production perforations and do not
constitute a risk. One item of note is that the Fischhaber 2-35 completion report indicates that
perforations should exist from 4,551 to 4,561 ft measured depth, however, perforations appear
to be missing from 4,555 to 4,560 ft measured depth. This reduced perforation coverage is not
believed to be an issue; the existing perforations should be sufficient to deliver CO2 to the
reservoir. A follow-up run for these tools is planned for approximately six months into the well
production period to evaluate the impact of CO2 corrosion within the wellbore.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      72

Figure 67. Description of DarkVision's Hades-R and Hades-T casing evaluation tool capabilities. From
Dark Vision.

Figure 68. Casing wall loss features identified by Hades tools. None of these features is a concern for
wellbore integrity.

Spectral Pulsed Neutron (SPN) Logging

In December 2024, ahead of CO2 injection into the Fischhaber 2-35, Baker-Hughes ran its SPN
tool to evaluate saturations of oil, gas, and water in the pore space surrounding the well (Figure
69). The tool was run successfully from 4,580 to 4,300 ft. The data established a baseline
against which data collected after CO2 injection can be compared.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       73

Figure 69. Wireline logging at the Fischhaber 2-35 well pad during December, 2024.

Follow-up logging was completed between June 19 and 22, 2025. Initial, minimally processed
logs were delivered on June 25, 2025, and indicate changes in oil, gas, and water saturation
within the reservoir as compared to the baseline logs gathered in December, particularly in the
portion of the reservoir immediately surrounding the perforations. Baker Hughes delivered final
logs with detailed modeling, interpretation, and reporting on August 27. The primary conclusions
of this time-lapse logging effort are as follows:
 1. Differences in wellbore completion (caused by well control concerns) between the
 2024 and 2025 logging runs significantly impacted the ability to compare data
 between the pre-injection and post-injection SPN runs (Figure 70).
 a. 2024 logging was completed with production tubing in the well and water
 occupying the casing annulus and tubing.
 b. 2025 logging was completed without tubing and with the wellbore loaded with
 diesel to maintain well control. The lower portion of the wellbore, below 4,555
 ft measured depth, was filled with reservoir water.
 c. Baker-Hughes’ wellbore modeling struggled to resolve the differences in
 metal mass between the two runs, resulting in significant uncertainty in
 calculated saturation.
 d. Differences in wellbore fluid between the two runs also resulted in uncertainty,
 particularly in the carbon/oxygen measurements.
 e. There are indications that CO2 was bubbling up through the fluid column
 during the follow-up run, adding uncertainty to density measurements.
 f. Future efforts should plan for a wellbore configuration that can be replicated
 both before and after CO2 injection, regardless of differences in reservoir
 pressure and fluid saturations.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                74

 2. No significant change in reservoir fluid saturation was detected in either the vuggy or
 matrix porosity systems.
 a. There does not appear to be significant differences in gas saturation values
 between the 2024 and 2025 runs.
 b. Elevated gas saturation detected below the lowest perforations are likely due
 to differences in wellbore configuration.
 c. The reservoir directly adjacent to the well perforations was strongly
 saturated with CO2 after injection. Lack of gas saturation observed by SPN
 logging is due to the extended soak period between injection and logging.
 d. It is not clear why there are no indications of increased gas saturation in the
 upper parts of the reservoir.
 3. Low porosity values throughout the reservoir resulted in high uncertainty when
 modeling the logged response, leading to low confidence in the output.
 a. Baker-Hughes recommends the suite of tools be used in reservoirs with
 greater than 10% average porosity. GasView has acceptable uncertainty
 down to 5% porosity, but the tool’s vertical resolution is too low to effectively
 resolve the vuggy intervals within this reservoir.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                75

4,616 feet measured depth, covering the primary reservoir zone. Note hardware effects from tubing in the
well in 2024 (tracks 3-6) as well as transitional effects from water-diesel interface in 2025 log (tracks 3&7).

3.4 Subtask 2.4: Integrated Physics-Based Machine Learning and
 Advanced Data Analytics
The data integration, ML, and data analytics subtask aimed to integrate all datasets and apply
ML techniques to predict key reservoir properties and compare different TBR fields using a
workflow for enhanced classification and predictions. The first part of the task focused on
wireline log data from the Albion-Scipio field in southern Michigan, then integrated other data
types to build predictive models. The key objectives included:
 1. Preprocessing of the wireline log LAS files
 a. Conversion to consumable formats for Python to process
 b. Linking formations to each depth in all wells
 2. Exploratory data analysis of the wireline log data for the Albion-Scipio field
 a. Remove observations/rounding of log data which fall outside of geologic constraints
 b. Checking histograms and distributions of the cleaned dataset to
 ensure geologic soundness
 3. Identification of electrofacies
 a. Use cluster analysis approaches including K-Means and hierarchical clustering
 4. Imputation of missing data
 a. Determine which logs have highest correlations to others
 b. Determine evaluation metrics for models being used to impute missing data
 c. Compare distributions between imputed and observed data
 5. Core data integration and exploration
 6. Vug and fracture data integration and exploration
 7. Build predictive models for sparsely sampled reservoir characteristics
 using machine learning
 a. Includes permeability, fractures, vugs, and seismic attributes

There are many challenges when evaluating carbonate reservoirs, especially when they have
undergone significant diagenetic alteration. This introduces complex heterogeneity which cannot
always be captured by routine data and analysis. Additionally, the vintage of the data in the
Albion-Scipio fields adds another challenge when trying to link wireline log signatures to predict
reservoir features. Because of this, the predictive models for permeability and vugs and
fractures were not successful. However, the task yielded important results which will be useful in
upcoming tasks to model the reservoir in 3D. This includes the classification and prediction of
electrofacies, distributions of porosity and permeability by electrofacies, estimated permeability
for each well, and the frequency of secondary porosity features observed by electrofacies.

Moving forward, the data will be integrated with the 3D seismic to link seismic attributes to areas
of enhanced porosity and permeability to inform the modeling efforts.

Full reporting on the ML and advanced data analytics efforts are provided in the Task 2 Final
Report as an attachment to this report.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               77

4.0 Task 3: Laboratory Experiments
The pore structure of the TBR is complex and the rock may be oil-wet/mixed-wet. Laboratory
experiments were conducted at the University of Texas (UT) Austin to optimize CO2-EOR fluids
to be effective in this complex carbonate reservoir. One reservoir core was characterized by CT
scanning, mercury porosimetry was completed on two cores, wettability testing was completed
on three cores, and relative permeability was completed on two cores. Slim-tube displacements
were conducted to determine the minimum miscibility pressure (MMP) with CO2. CO2 and
Water-Alternating-Gas (WAG) floods were conducted to determine the efficiency of the CO2
flood. Foam floods with wettability altering agents were conducted on three cores to see
improvement in CO2 flooding with foam. These laboratory experiments were modeled using a
compositional simulation approach, generating a calibrated model that was used to inform the
design of the chosen EOR process to the field pilot scale. Results from these experiments are
summarized below; the full study report from UT Austin is summarized in Attachment B and
provided as an attachment to this report.

4.1 Subtask 3.1: Core Characterization
The University of Texas completed a laboratory test to determine petrophysical characteristics
of reservoir core samples. The project team obtained 13 1.5-inch diameter core samples (Table
11) from the Napoleon field and performed four tests to better understand the conditions in the
reservoir: (1) CT scan, (2) mercury porosimetry, (3) oil/water relative permeability test, (4)
imbibition tests.

CT Scan

Core Sample #6-44 was scanned in this test to identify the vug and fracture content of the core
sample using a micro-CT with a resolution of 34.6 μm (Figure 71). The conclusion drawn from
this test was that there are many vugs of varying sizes but no obvious signs of fractures in the
cores that were scanned.

Figure 71. CT-scan of core sample #6-44 showing the largest pore present on the sample which
measured 11.102mm.

Mercury Porosimetry

Two core samples, #1-40 and #6-41, were used for mercury porosimetry. The air permeability of
#1-40 was 34.6 mD and the air permeability of #6-41 was 3.7 mD. Intrusion volume of mercury
versus applied pressure was obtained, and the pressures were converted into pore throat sizes.
Figure 72 shows the mercury porosimetry curves for core sample #1-40, including the
cumulative intrusion and incremental intrusion versus pore throat diameter. It indicates that core
sample 1-40 has a large set of vugs that are accessed at a pore throat size of 15 μm. For the
rest of the pores, it has another dominant pore throat size, 81 μm, which might be attributed to
vugs present in the core sample. Figure 73 shows the mercury porosimetry curves for core
sample #6-41. It has one dominant pore throat size, 26.9 μm; core sample #6-41 is more
homogeneous than core sample #1-40.

Figure 74 shows the capillary pressure versus mercury saturation for these two core samples.
For mercury saturation of less than 0.75, the two curves are similar. The capillary pressure
curve of #1-40 is higher than that of #6-41 at higher mercury saturation, which means that core
sample #I-40 has many smaller pore throats and is more heterogeneous at the pore scale.

In the unsteady state method, the core mount is first saturated with crude oil at the irreducible
water saturation and aged at 100°C for two weeks. Next, crude oil is injected into the core
mount at several different flow rates to calculate the oil permeability at the irreducible water
saturation. Then, formation brine is injected into the core mount at a constant flow rate of 0.15
ml/min (5 ft/day) to displace crude oil. The effluents coming from the core mount and the

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 81

pressure difference between the inlet and outlet are monitored during the process. Finally, the
water relative permeability at the residual oil saturation is calculated, when no more oil comes
out.

The calculated results are shown in Table 15. Figure 75 shows the oil-water relative
permeability curves from the unsteady state experiment. It should be noted that the relative
permeability curves are obtained over a limited saturation range of 0.458≤Sw≤0.64. The relative
permeability curves between Swirr=0.298 and Sw = 0.458 cannot be obtained from the
unsteady state method. The Corey-correlation (Equations 1 and 2) is used to match the
experimental results. The parameters in Corey-correlation are shown in Table 15 and the Corey
type relative permeability curves are shown in Figure 75. The oil relative permeability curve is
matched very well; however, the water relative permeability curve match is worse.

4.2 Subtask 3.2: MMP Determination
MMP is defined as the lowest pressure at which the injected gas and the reservoir oil become
miscible through a multi-contact process at the reservoir temperature. Most CO2 floods are
conducted at pressures greater than MMP to maximize oil interaction with CO2, maximizing oil
swelling, minimizing oil viscosity, and improving sweep efficiency.

MMP was determined for TBR reservoir oils using both statistical correlation and laboratory
analysis. These methods are described below and returned MMP values that are in strong
agreement.

Statistical Correlation

MMP for TBR crude oil was calculated using three statistical correlations developed by Valluri et
al. (2017), Cronquist (1978), and Yellig and Metcalfe (1980). This analysis required reservoir
temperature and the API gravity of the crude oil, which were obtained from a report written by
Brannigan et al. (1960) that contained information on three samples collected from the Albion,
Pulaski, and Scipio fields. Table 16 lists the properties of the three crude oil samples used for
the analysis and Table 17 shows the results of the statistical MMP analysis.

Table 16. Crude oil sample data used for MMP calculations (after Brannigan et. al., 1960).

 Sample Parameter Albion Sample Pulaski Sample Scipio Sample
          Date                                       5/1/1959     10/22/1959           8/18/1958
 Formation Trenton Black River Trenton
          Depth (feet)                                 3,390         4,100               3,800
          Bottomhole pressure (psi)                    1,737         1,732               1,542
 Bottomhole temperature (deg. 101 106 107
 F)
          Oil API gravity                              41.2           41.4               41.9

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                83

Table 17. Statistical MMP values for the three samples.

 MMP (psi)
 Oil Sample Yellig and Metcalfe
                               Valluri et. al. (2017)     Cronquist (1978)
 (1980)
     Albion                          1,255.0                  1,103.6                1,216.0
     Pulaski                         1,323.8                  1,159.2                1,309.8
     Scipio                          1,312.5                  1,148.5                1,309.8

Laboratory MMP Determination

Three different kinds of oils were used to determine MMP, with properties shown in Table 18.
These fluids were freshly sampled from active wells in the trend for laboratory analysis. The
sample termed “Michigan oil” was taken from the Travis 1-18 well in the Albion-Scipio field in
November of 2019; the “Napoleon oil” was taken from the West Bay 1-22 well in the Napoleon
field in mid-2021. Finally, the “McCann oil” was sampled from the McCann 1-20 well drilled
during project execution in early 2021, also in the Albion-Scipio field. Slim tube experiments
were conducted to determine MMP for these oils. The slim tube setup comprised a 20 ft coiled
slim tube made of stainless steel with an inner diameter of 0.26 inches and thickness of 0.063
inches which was packed with 100-150 mesh sand. System porosity was 37.4%, and tests were
run at pressures of 1,000 to 2,300 psi and temperature of 40°C, consistent with reservoir
environments. Full experimental setup is shown in Figure 76.

The effects of CO2 additives were also studied by slim tube experiments. Two additives from
Sasol Company, ME-1 and ME-2, were tested for lowering MMP. The slim tube experiments
were conducted with dead oil and the additives were mixed with CO2 separately prior to the
experiment.

Table 18. Properties of crude oils

Battelle | May 2026                                                                           84

Figure 76. Schematic of the set up for MMP determination apparatus using slim tube.

Results
 (1) Michigan Oil (Albion-Scipio Field)
Slim tube experiments were conducted with Michigan oil at seven different pressures. The 1.2
pore volumes injected (PVI) recovery factors are plotted as a function of pressure in Figure 77.
Oil recovery increased with pressure. The first five oil recoveries (1,000 to 2,000 psi) were lower
than 95%. However, the last two recoveries (2,100 and 2,300 psi) surpassed 95% with a slight
difference. Therefore, MMP was determined as 2,100 psi at 40°C.
 (2) Napoleon Oil (Napoleon Field)
Slim tube experiments were conducted with Napoleon oil at three different pressures, and the
corresponding oil recovery factors are presented in Figure 78. At 1,100 psi, the oil recovery was
78.3%, which is below 95%, indicating an immiscible flooding. At 1,300 psi, the oil recovery
notably improved to 93.4%. The increase of pressure to 1,500 psi resulted in an oil recovery of
94.6%, which was close to the previous one. Therefore, the MMP for Napoleon oil and CO2 is
approximately 1,300 psi at 40°C.
 (3) McCann Live Oil (Albion-Scipio Field)
Slim tube experiments were performed using McCann live oil, composed of McCann dead oil
combined with methane at a gas-oil ratio (GOR) of 220 scf/bbl. Results are shown in Figure 79.
At 1,100 psi, the oil recovery was 68%, indicating an immiscible flooding. At higher pressures
(1,300 psi, 1,500 psi, and 1,700 psi), the oil recovery was higher than 95%, indicating a shift to a
miscible flooding. Consequently, the MMP for McCann live oil and CO2 was determined as
1,300 psi at 40°C.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              85

Figure 77. The relationship between oil recovery and pressure (Michigan oil)

Battelle | May 2026                                                            86

Additive Results

Four separate slim tube experiments were conducted using ME-1, with the additive combined
with CO2 at between two weight percent and five weight percent, and then injected together into
the slim tube at 1,750 psi. Oil recovery results are shown in Figure 80. These results suggest
that the introduction of additive ME-1 did not yield an improvement in oil recovery, thus no
improvement in miscibility performance between oil and CO2.

A single slim tube experiment was performed using ME-2 as an additive. Five weight percent
ME-2 was combined with CO2 and injected into the slim tube to displace oil. The resulting oil
recovery, shown in Figure 81, was 84.7%. There is a 2.2% decrease compared to 86.9%
recovery achieved by CO2 flooding without any additives. Hence, the introduction of ME-2 as an
additive did not contribute to an increase in oil recovery at the tested pressure of 1,750 psi.

Slim tube experiments were conducted for reservoir oil with CO2. The following conclusions can
be drawn from the results.

 • The MMP for CO2 with Michigan oil was 2,100 psi at 40°C.
 • The MMP for CO2 with Napoleon oil was 1,300 psi at 40°C.
 • The MMP for CO2 with McCann live oil was 1,300 psi at 40°C.
 • Additives ME-1 and ME-2 cannot contribute to an improvement in miscibility
 performance between Michigan oil and CO2.

4.3 Subtask 3.3: CO2 Core Flood
The objective of this task was to assess the efficiency of CO2 flooding in EOR. To investigate the
effectiveness of CO2 flooding for EOR, core flooding experiments were conducted at a pressure
of 1,200 psi and a temperature of 40°C using the setup shown in Figure 82. Full

Battelle | May 2026                                                                            88

methodology is included in Appendix to be determined. Both continuous CO2 flooding and WAG
flooding were simulated.

Figure 82. Schematic of the set up for CO2 flooding experiments

Continuous CO2 Flooding

Figure 83 shows the oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop data versus pore volume
injected (PVI) for continuous CO2 flooding. The experimental procedure involved injection of four
pore volumes (PV) of simulated pore water (SPW), followed by 0.5 PV of CO2, and then another

1.5 PV of SPW injection. During the water flooding, breakthrough of water occurred at 0.16 PVI,
and a total of 36.5% of the original oil in place (OOIP) was recovered at the end of water
flooding. Subsequently, during the injection of CO2, breakthrough of CO2 was observed at 0.1
PVI of CO2 injection. At the end of the experiment, the incremental oil recovery was 14.4%
OOIP, which was attributed to the multi-contact miscible behavior of CO2 and oil. Not all of the
oil was recovered because of the viscous fingering and high-level heterogeneity of the core
sample. The relative pressure drop was calculated based on the pressure drip generated by
single-phase oil flow at the same flow rate of 1 ft/day. The relative pressure drop during CO2
flooding was found to be small, less than 1 psi, indicating that the CO2 tended to bypass the
smaller pores in the core samples.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                           89

Figure 83. Oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop for the continuous CO2 flooding experiment.

Water Alternating with Gas Flooding

Figure 84 shows the data on oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop versus PVI
during WAG flooding. The water flood was followed by a WAG flood with the same amount of
CO2. The water flood recovered 42.5% OOIP, higher than that in water alone. The tertiary WAG
flood yielded an additional 20.1% oil, indicating that the WAG flood could improve oil recovery
over the continuous CO2 flood in this core mount. Moreover, breakthrough of CO2 was observed
at 0.25 PVI of WAG injection, and the relative pressure drop during WAG injection was higher
than that of CO2 flooding, which indicated that WAG could help control CO2 mobility.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     90

Figure 84. Oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop for WAG flooding experiment

Continuous CO2 flooding with 0.5 PV CO2 injection could improve 14.4% OOIP over water
flooding. WAG injection could help control CO2 mobility and increase the oil recovery to 20.1%
OOIP.

4.4 Subtask 3.4: CO2 Foam Flood
The University of Texas developed and conducted several foam stability and rheology
formulation tests to determine the preferential alteration and wettability of rock to water-wet
conditions over oil-wet conditions for EOR for the Lee 26 field. Foam stability tests were
conducted to observe and determine foam performance, while rheology tests were used to
determine the steady state of foam and the apparent differential flow rate viscosity. The testing
parameters used included:

 • Reservoir Temperature = 40°
 • Pressure = 1,200 psi
 • Surfactant Concentration = 0.5 wt%
 • Foam Quality (gas volume fraction) = 80%
 • Foam Generation:
  Pre-flush surfactant solution into the system and increase the pressure to 1,200 psi
  Inject surfactant and CO2 simultaneously (qsurf = 0.4 cc/min, qCO2 =1.6 cc/min)

Foam stability surfactant tests focused on three surfactants at weight concentrations of 0.5%
(Soloterra843, CETAC-30, and LMDO). Each surfactant was tested under four conditions, 1)
saltwater density of 30,000 parts per million (ppm) (SWD1), 2) saltwater density 165,500 ppm

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              91

(SWD2), 3) SWD1 plus oil, and 4) SWD2 plus oil, to determine foam stability. Soloterra843
showed a breakdown of stability by more than 50% of foam volume at 10 minutes in a SWD1
and oil solution and complete breakdown at 40 minutes after solution injection (Figure 85). The
CETAC-30 surfactant had significant foam generation in all four solution scenarios (Figure 86).
The half-life results showed that foam breakdown of 50% was reached after 30 minutes and
80% after 90 minutes. In the higher salinity solution plus oil scenario, foam breakdown of >70%
occurred after 30 minutes and was >85% after 60 minutes (Figure 87). LMDO tests showed that
slightly less foam was generated than the CETAC-30 surfactant (Figure 88).

Figure 85. Soloterra843 (0.5wt%) surfactant, brine solution (30,000 ppm), and oil foam stability test. Foam
breakdown >50% occurs after 10 minutes.

Figure 86. CETAC-30 (0.5wt%) surfactant, brine solution, and oil foam stability tests.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     92

Figure 87. LMDO (0.5wt%) surfactant, brine solution, and oil foam stability tests.

Figure 88. Graph showing surfactant stability half-life results in brine only and brine with oil solutions.
Soloterra843 had the least foam stability in higher density brines. LMDO showed greater stability in higher
density brines. CETAC-30 is comparable to LMDO, however, there is less stability in higher density
brines.

CETAC-30 and LMDO showed the best foam generation in testing. However, CETAC-30
optimized pore surface wettability.

Data from the foam stability tests were used to compare pure CO2 flooding versus surfactant
and CO2 foam flooding to determine the most effective oil recovery scenario. Approximately
50% of the oil recovery resulted from a pure CO2 flood (Figure 89). However, the CETAC-30
surfactant with CO2 foam flood provided approximately 13% better oil recovery than pure CO2
(Figure 90).

Figure 90. A model of a CETAC-30 and CO2 flood to determine approximate oil recovery during a foam
flood scenario.

Methanol effect on foam stability was also tested at room conditions using CETAC-30 surfactant
(0.5 wt%). Foam stability of the surfactant in the presence (5 wt%) and absence of methanol
was investigated. Foam stability and foamability of the generated foam was investigated in each
scenario as the main criteria for evaluation. Foam stability was indicated by the foam half-life,
which is the time required for the foam to reach half of its original height. Foamability was
quantified by the initial foam height. Table 19 shows the material and conditions during this
project.

 Table 19. Material and conditions

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 94

Fifteen centimeters (cm) long and one centimeter diameter glass vials were filled with three
milliliters (ml) of the surfactant solution prepared in deionized (DI) water. 0.5 wt% CETAC
surfactant was utilized. Two cases were investigated. The first one without adding methanol and
the second one with adding 5 wt% methanol to the aqueous solution. Finally, foam was
generated by shaking the vials vigorously. Both foam stability and foamability were measured
for both cases.

Figure 91 shows the foam evolution versus time for both foam cases in the presence and
absence of methanol. Figure 92 further illustrates both cases showing a higher foamability (12.6
cm) attained in the absence of methanol. In addition, foam stability was much longer with a half-

of 9.3 cm. Moreover, the foam stability was significantly reduced with a half-life of only seven
minutes.

The conclusions from the investigation of methanol impact on foam stability are as follows:

 • In the absence of methanol, the generated foam had a high foamability of 12.6 cm and
 better foam stability with half-life of 58 minutes.
 • In the presence of 5 wt% methanol, the generated foam had a lower foamability of 9.3 cm
 with much lower stability with half-life of only seven minutes.
 • This demonstrates the adverse effect of methanol on the generated foam by disrupting
 the foam structure and accelerating its collapse.

and analyzed via:

 • Compositional analysis of separator oil/gas
 • Oil viscosity measurements at surface conditions
 • Recombination of separator oil and gas to reservoir conditions based on producing GOR
  Two recombinations were performed at two separate GOR values – 350 and 512
 scf/bbl
 • Constant composition expansion (CCE) analysis of each recombined fluid

Composition of the Hoffman stock tank oil is shown in Figure 94. The compositional analyses of
separator fluids and recombined fluids were used to create a compositional fluid model for the
Lee 26 reservoir. The CCE analyses provided bubble point pressures for each recombined fluid:
2,400 psia for the 512 scf/bbl fluid, and 1,750 psia for the 350 scf/bbl fluid. Discovery pressure
at the Lee Field was around 1,620 psia, meaning that the 350 scf/bbl fluid is a more accurate
representation of the saturated oil present at discovery within the reservoir. Using this fluid
composition in combination with gas samples from the Lee field, a compositional fluid model
was created for the reservoir at discovery. The full compositional analysis through C30 had to
be simplified through component lumping to make the fluid model computationally tractable. The
lumping scheme and final composition of the fluid model is shown in Table 22.

5.0 Task 4: Design and Simulations of Injection
 Scenarios
5.1 Subtask 4.1: Development of Static Earth Models

No SEM was developed for the McCann well area. This section focuses on SEM development
for the Lee 26 field.

Three formations were characterized for input into the SEM: the Utica, Trenton and Black River.
The Utica Shale is the primary caprock in the system, while the Trenton Limestone and Black
River Shale are both reservoir formations subject to dolomitization. The dolomitized zones,
present near fluid-transporting faults, typically contain the highest porosity and permeability
sectors. Therefore, facies and fault characterization are key factors influencing the development
of the SEM. Analysis of oil production across the four wells within the field (Beattie, Jacoby,
Fischhaber, and Marshell) is another indicator of the area with higher-quality reservoir
properties.

Well tops were picked in producing and non-producing wells in the area of the Lee 26 field for
the Utica (model top), Trenton, Black River, and Glenwood (model base) formations based on
known gamma ray log responses for each formation, selecting facies, and generating effective
porosity and permeability logs. Four primary facies comprise the three zones of interest: shale,
cap dolomite, limestone, and dolomite. The Utica Formation contains a uniform shale facies,
although more heterogeneity exists in the Trenton and Black River formations. Typically, cap
dolomite is present in the upper 5 to 50 ft of the Trenton formation and is believed to have
occurred due to dewatering of the Utica. This interval typically has lower porosity and
permeability than the conventionally dolomitized zones below it. Within the lower parts of the
Trenton and the Black River Formations, facies are divided between limestone and dolomite.
These facies were selected based on the neutron porosity and photoelectric (PeF) logs. Higher
porosity, lower PeF intervals indicate dolomite presence, whereas low porosity and high PeF
zones indicate limestone presence. The dolomite-rich zones are more commonly present in the
southeast portion of the field, as indicated by the Fischhaber well logs and overall production in
the Marshell well. These facies selections will guide the eventual property distributions that
indicate reservoir quality. Based on the trend of the field from northwest-southeast, the facies
major direction orientation (azimuth) is approximately -22o.

Effective porosity was calculated using neutron porosity and shale volume log from gamma ray.
The two permeability distributions were generated based on the Timur (1968) equation, which
relates effective porosity to irreducible water saturation in sandstones and carbonates. Varying
irreducible water saturation values of 0.15 and 0.5, derived in Task 2 “Advanced Field
Characterization and Machine Learning Based Data Integration” and based on knowledge of the
fields in the Albion-Scipio trend, were utilized due to there being no core data from the Lee 26
field.

Pressure buildup data provided from the Marshell (easternmost) well were also examined.
Buildup tests were conducted at the well on January 30, 2009 and April 28, 2009. Examining the
way in which pressures evolve throughout these tests provides insight on reservoir
characteristics and properties (including permeability). This information was invaluable in the
history matching process. All analysis was done within the IHS Markit WellTest software.

Screenshots of the buildup curves, along with fitted models, are shown in Figure 98.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             101

Results from the pressure buildup analysis are consistent with a dual-porosity system, affirming
the conceptual model of the field. Single porosity models were a poor fit to the pressure data.

Ranges for effective permeability, a composite of matrix and fracture (or in this case, moldic)
permeability were between 250 to 1,400 mD. Estimates for interporosity flow coefficient (the
effectiveness of flow between matrix and fracture) were between 10-5 and 10-6 which are
average values in dual porosity systems. Results on storativity ratio, the ratio of oil stored in the
network of vugs as compared to the matrix, were inconclusive, but ranged between 0.5% and
40%. Estimates based on the amount of dolomitization seen within the SEM are closer to this
40% mark. These ranges provide a starting point in history matching the SEM to fit field
production data.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                102

Figure 98. Pressure buildup analysis for the Marshell well on 1/30/2009 (left) and 4/28/2009 (right).
Shown are Horner plots (top) and typecurves (bottom) for each test, as well as the overlain model fit.

Seismic Inputs to Static Model

A 3D seismic volume provided by West Bay Explorations was used to characterize the
structures controlling reservoir facies distribution in the field area. Horizons were picked above
the target zones, within the sealing formation (Utica) and reservoir (Trenton-Black River), as
well as below the reservoir (Glenwood and basement). Twelve horizons were mapped in total.
Horizon-based and volume-based seismic attributes including RMS amplitude and average
instantaneous frequency were generated, as well as a series of isopach maps. These attributes
and isopachs maps were used to identify lineaments and ‘sag’ areas thought to be indicative of
the faulting that introduced dolomitizing fluids to the reservoir interval (Figure 99). The sag
features are clustered primarily in the vicinity of the reservoir and are oriented generally NNW-
SSE. The sags and lineaments, visible both on maps and in seismic cross-sections, guided the
placement of wrench-style faults throughout the seismic volume (Figure 100).

The Lee Field outline was determined was defined during West Bay’s initial seismic analysis of
the field using a dip of maximum similarity seismic attribute. This attribute identified the
presence of faults that bound and seal the field based on where there is a continuous similar dip
on a surface in the reservoir. The four producing wells in the Lee field fall within the bounding
feature and several dry holes, such as the JF Booten well penetrate the TBR just outside of the
bounding feature (Figure 101). The hotter colors bounding the Lee field wells in the figure
correspond to where there is a continuous dip of maximum similarity, which indicates the
presence of a fault providing lateral compartmentalized within this feature.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      103

Figure 99. Isopach map of the Trenton Formation showing thin ‘sag’ features (black dashed outlines) in
relation to the reservoir (white solid outline).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      104

Figure 100. A- Arbitrary line through 3D seismic volume showing interpreted wrench faults bounding the
sag features shown in the figure above.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  105

Figure 101. Dip of maximum similarity seismic attribute showing Lee field extent.

The Fischhaber well contains both bulk density and sonic logs, which were used to generate a
synthetic seismogram, allowing us to tie the well and formation tops to the seismic. Based on
this well tie, the horizons for the four model formations were picked. The structural model was
generated at a grid size to represent the vertical and lateral heterogeneity within the reservoir
zones. The model comprises three zones representing the three formations of interest: the
Utica, Trenton, and Black River. The depths and thicknesses of these zones were produced
based on the well top picks and tied to their corresponding seismic horizons to guide structure
(Figure 102). The Trenton formation is the key reservoir and therefore has the finest vertical
resolution, followed by the Black River and Utica formations. The faults that were picked in
seismic were integrated into the pillar grid. These faults bound the sag feature and are largely
linear features within the model domain.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             106

Figure 102. Cross-sectional view of model showing zones, layering, and faults bounding the dolomitized
sag feature.

In addition to the faults bounding the field and identified using the dip of maximum similarity
attribute, the faults transecting the Lee Field were incorporated into the model structure and
used to guide the facies and property distribution. These faults are perpendicular to the
bounding faults and in contrast are interpreted to serve as hydrothermal fluid conduits that
dolomitize the reservoir and develop secondary porosity and permeability. A normalized
distance to fault property was generated and co-kriged to the facies distribution, with dolomite
presence being tied to proximity to these faults (Figure 103). The higher porosity zones are
present within the dolomite, with the porosity distribution being guided by the facies.

The property model is based on the effective porosity and permeability distributions, with these
distributions to be guided by the facies and structural modeling. The well logs were upscaled to
fit within the grid model, and a random gaussian property distribution was utilized to populate
the grid cells. The facies and faults are intertwined, with the dolomite facies expected to be most
commonly present near fault features that transport high-temperature brine. Based on the initial
property model, the high porosity and permeability zones follow this trend (Figure 103).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  107

Figure 103. Cross-sectional view of initial porosity distribution in the Lee Field, with higher porosity zones
present to the SE near the Fischhaber well.

5.2 Subtask 4.2: Upscaling and Ranking of Static Earth Models.
SEMs generated in Subtask 4.1 were upscaled for use in CMG-GEM. The inability of the ML
and data analysis work in Task 2.4 to resolve the relationships between hydrothermal dolomite
facies and key geological properties caused the project to be unable to generate a broad and
simultaneously data-driven set of static model realizations. Instead, the project focused its
efforts on accurately representing geological property distributions based on the placement of
structural features in the Lee 26 seismic volume as part of Subtask 4.1.

5.3 Subtask 4.3: Material Balance Study

A series of material balance analyses (MBAs) were conducted using production and pressure
data from the Lee 26 field in order to estimate initial hydrocarbon in place at the time of
discovery –OOIP and original gas in place (OGIP). An accurate estimate of these values is
crucial for estimating field recovery and residual oil volumes, as well as constraining dynamic
reservoir simulations. MBAs are conducted using the material balance equation, which relates
initial fluid in place (OOIP and OGIP) to produced oil and gas by accounting for volume
gain/loss, fluid expansion/compression, solution gas evolution, and (where relevant) pore
volume expansion/compression. The concept of MBA is explained in detail in Ahmed (2019).
The Lee 26 field is a saturated oil reservoir with a gas cap showing a combination drive with
little to no aquifer support and fairly incompressible rock. As a result, the MBA focuses on the oil
and gas terms in the equation, ignoring pore volume compaction and water influx.

An initial MBA was conducted by project partner West Bay Exploration, the operator of the Lee
26 field, using data available for the field in 2019. Estimates of the OOIP were calculated at
various times throughout the production lifetime of the field using standard correlations for fluid
properties (Vasquez and Beggs, 1977; Standing and Katz, 1942) alongside field data. An initial
gas cap fraction of 20% was assumed, and solution GOR of 512 scf/bbl. The OOIP and OGIP
estimates obtained from this analysis at various stages of field depletion are shown in Figure

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         108

104. After the results of the PVT analysis conducted on Hoffman 1-2 oil in Task 3 were received
showing an initial solution GOR of 350 scf/bbl, a second MBA was conducted using the same
correlations with updated oil properties and final production volumes from the field. Using the
same assumed 16% free gas volume fraction, the updated MBA suggested OOIP and OGIP of
3.06 million stock tank barrels (mmstb) and 1.50 billion cubic feet (Bcf), respectively. The OGIP
is consistent with initial estimates. Given the level of depletion in the field, nearly all of the OGIP
will have been produced (i.e. OGIP roughly equals produced gas volume). OOIP increases in
this case by ~35% from the average of initial MBA calculations, suggesting a substantially lower
recovery fraction than initially calculated.

Figure 104. Material balance estimates of OOIP and OGIP for the Lee 26 reservoir.

This analysis technique inherently has some uncertainties. Some of these uncertainties are
relatively small, such as the assumption of no pore volume compaction. Any system with
connate water saturation, which the Lee 26 field is expected to have, will exhibit some degree of
pore volume compaction simply from the expansion of this connate water. Given the relative
incompressibility of water, this uncertainty was deemed inconsequential. The volume of
produced water in this system is also a source of small uncertainty. Given the lack of available
water production data, the MBA ignored this piece of information. The best available estimate of
between 175,000 and 200,000 stb of produced water serves to change the OOIP estimate by
about 0.3%.

Several significant sources of uncertainty do exist, however. One such uncertainty is the free
gas volume fraction in the reservoir at discovery. While it is known that a gas cap exists based
on observations during field development, the complex geometry of the Lee 26 field makes it
difficult to obtain a better estimate than the 20% value used. Another significant uncertainty is
the implicit assumption of MBA techniques that the system is a static tank. Pressure data
obtained during water injection operations at the Lee 26 field suggests a dynamic system, with a
high-conductivity fracture that closed at some point during depletion, decreasing connectivity to
certain sections of the reservoir. This would have changed the pressure and production profiles

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 109

of the system in a way that would be misrepresented by MBA calculations. Despite this
uncertainty, the MBA gives a reasonable estimate for total reservoir volume that can be used as
a starting point for dynamic modeling work.

5.4 Subtask 4.4: Dynamic Simulations and Analysis
Dynamic modeling work for the Lee 26 field began early in the project lifetime and evolved in
both complexity and scope over the course of the project. Initial modeling efforts consisted of
tank models built to estimate the volume of water needed to pressurize the depleted field to
MMP and estimate necessary CO2 volumes for a chemically-enhanced CO2-EOR flood. After
this, a history-matched model was built from the full-complexity SEM by incorporating lab
analysis results, multi-porosity systems and field production and pressure data. This history-
matched model was used to forecast expected production from the CO2 treatment. A series of
shoebox models was built and assessed based on this history-matched model to better assess
the efficacy of low-pressure CO2-EOR suggested by the history-matched model. Finally, a
specific set of forecasting scenarios were assessed on the history-matched model to inform
production strategy during field testing operations. The modeling efforts are presented
chronologically below to illustrate how the reservoir modeling evolved and built on existing work
throughout the project lifetime.

Tank Model – Early Repressurization Screening

Early modeling efforts focused on estimating water injection needed to facilitate a miscible or
pseudo-miscible flood at the Lee 26 field given the measured MMP of ~1,300 psi. A target
pressure of 1,000 psi was investigated as a tradeoff between lower recovery efficiency and
decreased repressurization time. A basic repressurization scenario in a homogeneous, layer
cake reservoir was modeled in the commercial simulator CMG-IMEX using reservoir property
data provided by project partner West Bay. Model dimensions were constrained by a material
balance study performed by West Bay that estimated OOIP as 2,294,000 bbls. Using a
formation volume factor of 1.26 (also from the material balance study) and the OOIP estimate,
estimated pore volume occupied by oil at discovery as 2,890,000 bbls at reservoir conditions.
The reservoir gas cap is estimated to take up roughly 16% of the volume occupied by oil, giving
us a final hydrocarbon pore volume (HCPV) estimate of 3,352,000 bbls at reservoir conditions.
Reservoir dimensions were then calculated using volumetric analysis techniques with the
equation:
 𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻 = 𝐴𝐴 ∗ ∆𝑧𝑧 ∗ 𝜙𝜙 ∗ 𝑆𝑆𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻

where A is the surface area spanned by the reservoir, ∆𝑧𝑧 is the thickness of the reservoir, 𝜙𝜙 is
porosity and 𝑆𝑆𝐻𝐻𝐻𝐻 is the initial saturation of hydrocarbons. An initially saturated reservoir with
connate water saturation of 0.15 was assumed. The surface area was found by examining the
footprint of wells within the reservoir and was estimated to be 0.6 mi2. Average reservoir
porosity was estimated from core samples to be ~3.9%. Using these values and the HCPV
estimate gives a reservoir thickness of 35 ft. These values were used to build the tank model
illustrated in Figure 105. The reservoir model has a gas cap in the top layer which accounts for
~14% of the total volume. Reservoir permeability was set to 50 mD with vertical to horizontal
anisotropy factor of 0.01.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                110

Figure 105. Depiction of the initial gas saturation in the Lee field tank model. Model dimensions are 4,030
ft x4,030 ft x35 ft.

Using production data provided by industry partners, a history match was attempted using the
simplified model with production data for the Lee field as shown in Table 23 below. Obtaining a
suitable history match was a difficult process with the tank model. The complexity of the Lee
field and its completions were difficult to replicate without a more sophisticated model. However,
a suitable match to the data was obtained by adjusting well locations and orientations and
modifying reservoir parameters. The match to field pressure data are shown in Figure 106, and
the matches to both oil and gas production are shown in Figure 107.
