Figure 107. Cumulative Oil (green) and Gas (maroon) production data and history matches.

Part of the difficulty in history matching stems from a change in production conditions in the Lee
field. Starting around mid-2013, oil production tapered off and gas production increased.

Several changes to the tank model were necessary to force additional gas production while
keeping oil production at a steady decrease. These changes included decreasing vertical
permeability, lowering oil mobility after June 2013 (by lowering the oil relative permeability), and
inserting a horizontal production well into the gas cap in June 2013. Figure 108 shows the
placement of this horizontal well within the reservoir model, as well as the gas saturation near
the end of production (November 2019).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               112

Figure 108. Placement of the horizontal well within the gas cap of the reservoir tank model. Coloring
corresponds to gas saturation in November 2019.

These artificial changes provide insight into the actual production processes that were likely
occurring in the field. Production data indicate that beginning sometime in 2013, production from
the field was primarily gas which likely originated from the gas cap. This high amount of gas
production would also explain the large drop in pressure from 2015 to 2019, as producing
primarily gas lowers pressure much faster than producing a more oil-heavy mixture. In addition,
the production rates necessary to match production data after 2013 caused a significant amount
of coning in the simulations. The presence of this coning coupled with the low amount of oil
production during this time is further evidence that much of the late-stage production was likely
from within the gas cap. These lessons learned were applied in all following model development
efforts.

Reservoir repressurization volume estimates were then calculated using the history matched
tank model by simulating water injection into the depleted reservoir until formation pressures
reached 1,000 psi (a conservative estimate of the miscibility pressure for CO2). Injecting water
at 50,000 bbls per month (~49 gpm), it took 28 months and an injection volume of 1.4 MMB of
water to reach 1,000 psi. The results of this simulation are shown in Figure 109. A scenario was
also modeled where water was injected with an injection well blowhole pressure maintained at
90% of the formation fracture pressure (assuming a fracture gradient of 0.8 psi/ft). In this
scenario, the reservoir took less time (~14 mos.) to repressurize, but the injection volume
remained consistent at 1.4 MMB.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     113

Figure 109. Pressure (orange) and injected water volume (blue) in the history matched tank model with
repressurization. In this scenario, water is injected at 50,000 bbls/mo. until pressure reaches 1,000 psi.

Given the high estimate of water volumes for repressurization, the team began exploring
scenarios where only a section of the reservoir was repressurized (around a single well or
across a specified well pair), theoretically allowing for chemical EOR on only the repressurized
zone. An additional layer of sophistication was built into the tank model to facilitate this effort. To
better represent the geometry of the Lee field, volumetric model dimensions of the tank model
were adapted to be significantly longer in one areal dimension than the other (more consistent
with field boundaries determined from seismic). Reservoir properties were maintained from the
initial tank model. The four wells in the system were added at locations consistent with the four
wells in the Lee field. The updated volumetric model is shown in Figure 110, with well traces of
each of the four new wells shown. The figure is oriented NW (left) to SE (right). The model was
history-matched to field production and pressure data, the results of which are shown in Figure
111.

Figure 110. Image of the updated volumetric model for the Lee field with approximate locations for the four
wells. Orientation is NW to SE.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                          114

Figure 111. Lee field history-matched model results (lines) with field data (points) for Cumulative Oil
(black), Cumulative Gas (green), and Reservoir Pressures (orange).

With a suitable match obtained, modeling efforts shifted focus toward pressurizing a sector of
the field that spanned across the identified injector-producer pair (Marshell and Fischhaber) to
at least 1,300 psi, the minimum miscibility pressure of the formation fluid. The location of this
sector is illustrated in Figure 112. Initial scenarios for repressurization were modeled in the
history-matched domain with different wells (and number of wells) used for injection and
variable injection rates. Jacoby was unable to be used for injection due to well conditions, and
modeling results showed that injection through Beattie provided minimal benefit to
repressurization of the eastern sector. Injecting water through Fischhaber, the designated
producer during CO2 EOR flooding, would lead to a high water cut during the production phase
of the project. Thus, the only viable scenario was to use Marshell to inject the water.

Figure 112. Image illustrating the sector to be repressurized (pink) in the volumetric model. The orientation
of the cross-section is NW to SE.

Various injection rates and lengths of injection were simulated for a single-well repressurization
scenario at the Marshell, the results of which are shown in Figure 113. Three different scenarios

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       115

were able to attain an average sector pressure of 1,300 psi – injecting at 80,000 bbls/mo. for
either 7 or 10 months or injecting at 50,000 bbls/mo. for 10 months. The models were also
limited to a well BHP that is 90% of the fracture gradient, causing injection rates to drop off in
later months. The injection volumes for these scenarios ranged from about 450,000 bbls to
580,000 bbls of water. These estimates were deemed more logistically feasible than the
calculated 1.4 million barrels of water necessary to repressurize the entire field, and a strategy
of sector repressurization was pursued.

Figure 113. Average pressures in the sector shown in Figure 112 for a variety of injection scenarios.
Target pressure is shown by the pink line, and three scenarios manage to attain this pressure.

Full-Complexity Model – CO2 Volume Scoping

Upon completion of the detailed SEM in Petrel, the model was imported into CMG. Shown in
Figure 114 is the porosity distribution of the model in three dimensions. The Utica is the caprock
at the top, followed by the Trenton, Black River-1 and Black River-2 in increasing depth. The oil-
water contact was initialized at 3,735 ft true vertical depths, subsea (TVDSS), consistent with
geologic interpretation, and a connate water saturation of 20% was applied across the initial
model. Given the increases in the pricing of CO2 at this stage of the project, the first modeling
goal was to obtain an estimate of the volume of CO2 necessary for a half-pore volume (PV)
flood of the southeast sector of the field. Based on well log data, the majority of the Lee field
reservoir is in the top section of the Black River-2, with some parts of the reservoir lying in Black
River-1 and the bottom portion of the Trenton. To remove the PV above this region, null values
were applied to blocks above the reservoir interval, as shown in the top panel of Figure 115. PV
was then calculated in CMG for the field as a whole and for the sector of interest (bottom panel
of Figure 115).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     116

Figure 114. Three-dimensional initialization of Lee Field static model in CMG-GEM. Image shows porosity
throughout the model.

Figure 115. Portions of the Lee field used in the CO2 volume estimation: (top) lower region of the field
within which the Lee field reservoir lies; (bottom) SE sector of the field, highlighted in pink.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        117

The HCPV estimate of the full field was in alignment with values obtained from the production-
based material balance analysis on the field – 3.35 million reservoir barrels. The HCPV of the
sector of interest was 7.6 million cubic feet (at reservoir conditions). Based on this volume and
the density of CO2 at 1,200 psi – a sub-optimal repressurization target – the CO2 mass
requirement for a 0.5 PV flood of this section of the reservoir was calculated to be 29,850 metric
tonnes.

Full Complexity Model – Multi-Porosity Integration and Production History Matching

Initial dynamic modeling was conducted on the single porosity model used in CO2 volume
estimation. Given the large gap between early results and field data, along with the dual porosity
pressure signatures observed in pressure transient analysis, the decision to implement a full
dual porosity model was made. Although many depositional and diagenetic facies are
identifiable within TBR reservoirs, the reservoir can be effectively represented for modeling
purposes with three primary ‘producing facies’: tight limestone, matrix dolomite, and
vugs/cavernous dolomite. In addition, fractures are expected to be present in the system; these
are grouped with the high-conductivity vug/cavernous dolomite facies (referred to hereafter as
vug/fracture).

Facies were initially split within the SEM into limestone and dolomite. Laboratory and field data
shared by industry partners suggest that the limestone facies are not pressure connected to the
reservoir and do not contribute to fluid flow. As such, model blocks defined as limestone were
eliminated fom the dynamic model by setting those cells as null. The dolomite facies was further
divided by using a porosity cutoff. Within the dolomitic facies blocks, the dual porosity system
was identified as:
 1. Matrix porosity – small pores and generally lower porosity and permeability
 2. Vug/fracture porosity – higher permeability and generally higher porosity

In the dynamic model, porosity values of <10% were assigned as matrix dolomite, and values
>10% assigned as vug/fracture. The matrix and fracture (really vug/fracture) porosity values
were calculated from total porosity (ΦT) as defined in the SEM for each block using the following
equations (for Χ=0.1):

Implicit in this assignment of values is that the vug/fracture system is present in every block
within the model. Blocks assigned as matrix dolomite are assumed to have a very small
contribution from the vug/fracture system. Permeability for each system was calculated using
the effective permeability from the SEM, volume fraction and dual porosity factors using the
approach of Warren and Root, 1963. Grid blocks are modeled as each containing a single
vertical fracture in each of the x and y directions. After some algebraic manipulation based on
this geometric assumption and the use of shape factors defined by Warren and Root, the final
equations used for permeability calculation are as follows:

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               118

The resulting model porosities are shown in Figure 116. The dual porosity option was used in
CMG-IMEX to determine matrix and fracture grid connectivity.

Figure 116. Three-dimensional rendering of the dynamic model, viewed from the south. The upper
render indicates the distribution of matrix porosity; the lower render indicates the distribution of fracture
and vuggy porosity. White/blank space indicates limestone matrix that is not pressure connected to the
reservoir.

Once the porosity and permeability distribution were populated, fluid saturations were assigned
to the model. The hydrocarbon fluids were represented with a black oil fluid model for the
Napoleon oil assessed in Subtask 3.2 (Section 4.2). Fluid contact depths were assigned based
on data provided by the field operators. A 15% minimum connate water saturation was used

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                             119

throughout the reservoir, and the top boundary of the reservoir was determined using OOIP,
OGIP and free gas fraction constraints from the material balance analysis. The resulting fluid
saturations are shown in Figure 117.

Figure 117. Dynamic reservoir model filled with reservoir fluids. The model indicates a reservoir filled with
gas-saturated oil, with an overlying gas cap. The lower portion of the reservoir is a water leg, although
there are no indications it provided any water drive.

Initial history matching to production data was done on this model using the original 520 scf/bbl
solution GOR assumption. A fairly strong match was obtained by varying local pore volumes,
local permeability, and grid connectivity in specific locations. Once PVT analysis was completed
on the analogous Hoffman oil sample, suggesting a lower solution GOR, the existing model was
history matched again to field production data using the new black oil model for Hoffman oil (see
Subtask 3.1, Section 4.1). The prior model provided a good starting point for history matching
efforts, and a match was obtained by modifying local-scale pore volumes, permeabilities and
relative permeabilities throughout the reservoir. In addition, during history matching the gas cap
over the Marshell well was removed from the model, a modification supported by revisiting log
analyses. A good quality match was obtained for the complete field history as shown in Figure
118. The well-by-well history match, shown in Figure 119, was also fairly consistent. The
Fischhaber and Jacoby wells showed the largest variability from observed production data.
Cumulative volumes show a good match at each well (including Jacoby and Fischhaber), but
production rates are not always well matched.

Figure 118. Field-scale production history match showing comparison between gas/oil production and
pressure of the simulation and field data.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       120

 Figure 119. Well-by-well history match at the Lee 26 field, showing gas (blue) and oil (black)
 production at each well in the simulation (solid line) vs. field data (open circles).

Full Complexity Model – Injection History Match and Forecasting Models

Using the production history-matched model, further simulations were run to attempt to match
pressure data obtained during water injection operations. Initial results showed that pressure
buildup at the Marshell well was significantly higher than observed in the field while pressures at
the Fischhaber were much lower than observed. This is illustrated in Figure 120. In an attempt
to increase pressure transmission between Marshell and Fischhaber within the model,
permeability was increased across the entire model. This had the result of reducing total
pressure buildup in the field, with hardly any pressure increase observed at either well. This
suggested a more complex explanation for the lack of a match in injection data despite a good
production history match.

Figure 120. Pressure response at the Fischhaber (left) and Marshell (right) wells due to water injection.
The model response does not match observations.

One possible explanation for the model pressure responses was gleaned from analysis of the
fluid saturations and pressure distributions after water injection. Figure 121 shows these
properties in the reservoir after 15 months of injection following the field injection schedule.
Pressure primarily builds up in a compartment surrounding the Marshell well (eastern side of the
field), and high pressures do not propagate very far outside of this region. Despite this, injected
water does manage to travel throughout the entire reservoir, reaching the far western end of the
field. This is consistent with sample analysis and observations of chemical tracers in all three
offset wells. There is a clear discrepancy in inter-well conductivity between the Marshell and
Fischhaber (and to a lesser extent, the western portion of the field).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         121

Figure 121. Pressure distribution (top) and fluid saturations (bottom) in the Lee field model after 15 months
of water injection.

A solution that was identified was the presence of a high conductivity channel between Marshell
and Fischhaber that continued deeper into the field. A high conductivity channel that spans the
entire field from E-W was added to the model at a depth of ~3,705 ft TVDSS (4,653 ft TVD at
Fischhaber). This depth was chosen based on image logs at the Fischhaber well, which
indicated the presence of vugs, and porosity logs at the Marshell well showing high porosity
around this depth. The throughgoing channel was given a constant horizontal permeability of
1,000 mD and vertical permeability of 500 mD. The porosity was set to 0.5% in blocks that did
not already show the existence of vugs/fractures and unchanged in those that did.

In addition, a vertical connection was established at the Fischhaber well between the deeper
and shallower sections of the reservoir. This connection was expected to exist based on the
lack of agreement in pressure data from the previous history-matched (HM) model. A 3x3
section of the model was added surrounding the Fischhaber well trace with high vertical
permeability (100 mD) and low horizontal permeability (1 mD). Porosity was calculated in the
same way as the throughgoing channel. This vertical connection was modeled in the immediate
wellbore vicinity to approximate the impact of an acid treatment done on the well shortly after
completion.

The addition of these two features, as well as further modifications to local pore volumes (both
fracture and matrix) and permeabilities, resulted in a new field history match. The production
data match was worse than the prior model, but injection pressure data match was significantly
better. The team chose to weigh the injection data more heavily due to the high resolution
nature of the data and its proximity in time to planned CO2 injection operations. The results of
the final history-matched model are shown in Figure 122 (field-wide production history match),
Figure 123 (well-by-well production match), and Figure 124 (injection pressure data match). The
overall match is fair, but the lack of agreement particularly in production data suggests the

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      122

subsurface interpretation shown in the model may not fully capture the complexity present in the
Lee 26 reservoir.

Figure 122. Field-wide history match of current best-fit Lee 26 model. Match quality is slightly decreased
from prior models in favor of injection data match.

Figure 123. Well-by-well history match of current best-fit Lee 26 model.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      123

Figure 124. Injection pressure data match of current best-fit Lee 26 model. Downhole gauge data (points)
are coarsened from the high resolution output of the gauges.

The saturation distribution within this history-matched model did not quite agree with the team’s
mental model of the reservoir. The expectation was that the vug/fracture network within the
reservoir would be entirely gas-saturated at field depletion, as supported by the very high GOR
in the later years of production. Matrix oil saturations were expected to be high within the oil leg,
accounting for practically all of the residual oil in the field. However, the numerical model
suggests a substantial column of oil in the secondary porosity network below the perforations in
the wellbores. Figure 125 (top) shows this column (highlighted with an orange box) in the model.
Also shown in the figure are matrix saturations, which are essentially unchanged from
discovery. This mostly agrees with the team’s understanding of the reservoir. Despite the
disconnect between the team’s mental model and the numerical model, this match was deemed
satisfactory in balancing field operation timelines, resource allocation, and diminishing returns
from further modifications to the history-matched model.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                   124

 Figure 125. Saturation distributions in the vug/fracture network (top) and matrix (bottom) at field
 depletion in the Lee 26 model. The orange box highlights a column of oil in the “fracture” network at
 field depletion.

In order to assess the utility of further field repressurization operations prior to CO2 injection
(i.e., more water injection), a series of huff and puff simulations was assessed on the best-fit
field history model. Five scenarios were run in which the field was pressurized to various target
pressures, after which the anticipated field CO2 injection operations were modeled. The
scenarios were designed as follows:
 1. Inject water through Marshell at rates consistent with field data (1,800 bbl/d afterward)
 2. Shut-in field for two weeks, assess average reservoir pressure
  This is the representative pressure for scenario definition
         Five target pressures: 550, 750, 950, 1,150, 1,350 (psia)
 3. Inject CO2 through Fischhaber in three 1,000 tonne slugs
  Inject at 1,000 tonnes/d
  Follow CO2 with a wellbore volume of water
  Soak for 12 days following an injection phase (including the final phase)
 4. Produce Fischhaber well at 600 bpd liquid target rate

CO2 injection was added to the model using the pseudomiscible option in CMG-IMEX (keyword
MISNCG). The input solvent PVT and miscibility table was generated using results of the MMP
lab testing results. The average field pressures for all five scenarios (color-coded) are shown in
Figure 126. Oil recovery for each of the scenarios with the same color coding is shown in Figure
127.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         125

Figure 126. Average reservoir pressure in each of the 5 huff and puff scenarios during pressurization, CO2
injection, and production. The colors range from low to high pressure as follows: orange, blue ,black,
green, purple.

Figure 127. Cumulative oil (top) and oil production rate (bottom) for each of the five huff and puff
scenarios. Curves are synchronized for comparison purposes. Colors correspond to the low-high pressure
colors described in Figure 118.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    126

The results of these simulations show only small differences between the performance of low-
and high-pressure huff and puffs, an unexpected observation based on CO2 huff and puff
operations in more conventional settings. Moreover, the lower pressure scenarios show higher
total oil recovery (at least within the first 6 months of production) than the higher-pressure
scenarios. This result initially seemed to contradict the team’s understanding of CO2-EOR
operations. Reaching MMP, or at least getting close to it, by repressurizing the field with water
was a high priority in order to optimize the volume of swelled oil resulting from dissolved CO2. It
appears in this case that the benefit from a higher volume of CO2 at reservoir conditions (i.e.,
the lower pressure scenarios) – which allows for more reservoir volume to be touched by the
injected CO2 – outweighs the benefits of miscibility in total recovery.

Motivated by the unexpected result, a literature review was conducted to identify previous work
on the impacts of miscibility in CO2 huff and puff operations in fractured reservoirs. One such
study was discovered with results that largely agree with the modeling results discussed above.
The paper, Ding et al. (2019), describes bench-scale experiments of CO2 floods conducted on
manufactured core with various fracture parameters and pressure regimes. The overarching
result suggests that low/medium pressure (pseudomiscible) floods perform as well as or better
than high pressure (fully miscible) floods in fractured settings.

Shoebox Model – Huff and Puff Operational and Reservoir Parameter Sensitivity

Additional simulation work was conducted to validate the findings that a low-pressure huff and
puff operation would perform as well or better than a high-pressure huff and puff. The first step
toward validating the low-pressure injection results was to build a simplified model, which was
termed the “shoebox model,” to enable rapid sensitivity analyses. A simulation matrix was
developed to determine which variables would be assessed in the sensitivity analyses, as
shown in Table 24.

Table 24. Scenario Matrix for Shoebox Model Sensitivity Analysis

The shoebox models were set up as a rectangular prism (longer than it is wide, similar to the
real field and prior rectangular tank model) with pore volume and original fluids in place (OFIP)

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                127

similar to the full complexity reservoir model. Porosity and permeability in each porosity system
were homogeneous across the model domain. Since porosity varied across the different
scenarios, specific scenarios were fine-tuned with different volume multipliers for each unique
porosity combination to match OFIP. The fluid contacts were kept at the same depths as in the
complex model. The wells were in roughly the same locations as in the complex model, with
some slight variation due to the lower resolution of the shoebox model. Relative permeability
curves and black oil fluid model were identical to the full complexity model.

In order to ensure each simplified model was representative of the depletion history of the Lee
field, a full-field history matching exercise was conducted for each unique porosity combination.
Proxy production wells located strategically throughout the different fluid columns (oil/gas) were
used to generate the full-field matches since a well-by-well production match would be
infeasible with this simplistic model. This resulted in a series of depleted reservoir models that
were sufficiently representative of the true depleted reservoir state given the level of simplicity.
The history matching results for each scenario are co-plotted in Figure 128. The dots show field
data for production volumes and pressures, while the various curves show different scenarios
(unlabeled due to the high number of scenarios). Green curves/dots show cumulative oil
volume, red curves/dots show cumulative gas volume, and orange shows average reservoir
pressure. All of the gas and pressure curves show a reasonably close match. The oil curves are
not as well matched in some cases, indicating that specific fracture/matrix porosity combination
is likely not perfectly representative of the reservoir. The scenario corresponding to the oil curve
that drastically underestimates oil production corresponds to the unfractured scenario. It is
understandable that this does not match well, since the reservoir is dual porosity, but it was
important to have an unfractured scenario in the sensitivity analysis for purpose of comparison.

Figure 128. History matching results for the various shoebox model cases assessed in the sensitivity
analysis.

Next, the repressurization period was simulated on each of the history-matched models.
Depending on the scenario, water was either injected into just the Marshell well or into all three
offset wells (Marshell, Jacoby, Beattie) at a field injection rate of 3,000 barrels per day (bpd).
Water injection continued until the average reservoir pressure reached the value cited in Table
24 for a given scenario. Once the model reached target pressure, a CO2 slug was injected into

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    128

the Fischhaber well – either 100 tonnes or 1,000 tonnes – at a rate of 200 tonnes per day (tpd).
After a four-week soak period, the Fischhaber well was turned over to production with either (1)
a bottom hole pressure constraint corresponding to absolute open flow (AOF – zero gauge
pressure at bottom hole) or (2) a rate constraint of 600 bpd, with a secondary bottom-hole
pressure (BHP) constraint corresponding to AOF. In the second case, the well would produce at
600 bpd until the BHP reached 0 psig, at which point the constraint would flip to BHP. A total of
46 unique scenarios were run in the sensitivity assessment.
From the results of this sensitivity assessment, several conclusions are draw, and addressed in
turn below:

 1) In a single porosity reservoir, higher pressure leads to higher recovery.

Figures 129 and 130 and show the results of models run on a single porosity model with porosity
8% and permeability 10 mD. Across all of these scenarios, attaining a higher reservoir pressure
prior to CO2 injection leads to a higher cumulative recovery. This is true regardless of the
volume of CO2 injected or production strategy (AOF versus rate-limited) used. These results
align with the conventional understanding of CO2-EOR – higher pressures yield better results
due to the impact of CO2-oil miscibility and the fact that a higher pressure reservoir has more
energy to move fluid into the production wellbore. This indicates that the dual-porosity nature of
the Lee field contributes to the efficacy of low-pressure huff and puff scenarios.

Figure 129. Cumulative recovery in the unfractured shoebox model for AOF production scenarios

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             129

Figure 130. Cumulative recovery in the unfractured shoebox model for rate-limited (600 bpd) production
scenarios.

 2) In a dual porosity reservoir producing at AOF, higher pressure leads to higher
 recovery.

The trend observed for the unfractured scenarios remains true in the fractured scenarios where
an AOF production strategy is used. Figure 131 below shows the results from a fractured model
(matrix poro/perm same as above; fracture porosity = 1%, fracture permeability = 1,000 mD)
where the field was repressurized using all three offset wells. Figure 132 shows the same, but
for cases repressurized through only the Marshell (1,000 t CO2 injection). The observed trend is
true regardless of the volume of CO2 injected. In contrast to the single porosity cases, however,
oil volume plateaus after a point as water cut approaches 100%. The high permeability
fractures/vugs allow for injected water to bypass oil remaining in the matrix system.

Figure 131. Cumulative recovery in fractured shoebox model for AOF production strategy. Water re-
injection was done through all three offset wells.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  130

Figure 132. Cumulative recovery in fractured shoebox model for AOF production strategy. Water re-
injection was done through only the Marshell well.

 3) In a dual porosity reservoir, a rate-limited production strategy increases
 hydrocarbon yields and performs similarly at both low and high
 pressures.

Figure 133 shows the results for the 1000t injection, three-well repressurization, fractured
scenarios from above, but with a rate-limited (600 bpd) production strategy. Unlike the AOF
case, similar performance is seen across different pressure cases.

Production in the first 200 days or so does follow the established trend, where higher pressures
correlate with substantially higher recovery. However, over the course of the first 1.5 years,
recovery is approximately the same across the four scenarios pictured, varying by ~1,000 bbls
between the lowest and highest recovery scenarios at the 1.5 year mark. The same qualitative
behavior is observed in scenarios with 100 tonnes of CO2 injected, and in scenarios where
water is injected exclusively at the Marshell well (not pictured). In addition, the yields in the rate-
limited case are substantially higher than in the AOF case. The high water cuts observed at later
times in the AOF case do not appear to be an issue in the rate-limited case, so oil continues to
be produced.

Figure 133. Cumulative recovery in the fractured shoebox model for rate-limited (600 bpd) production
strategy. Water re-injection was done through all three offset wells.

 4) The heterogeneity of the full complexity model contributes to the efficacy of
 low-pressure huff and puff scenarios.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    131

It should be noted that while the distinction between low- and high-pressure scenarios is less
apparent (i.e., similar performance) in the rate-limited, fractured scenarios, better performance
is seen from high pressure than from low pressure cases. In the simulations discussed that
were run on the full complexity model (see Figure 129), low pressure cases outperformed the
high-pressure cases. This would suggest that the heterogeneity present in the full complexity
model is at least partially responsible for the efficacy of a lower-pressure huff and puff. The
contrast in reservoir parameters between the two porosity networks is also a factor, as was
discovered within this sensitivity analysis.

Figure 134 shows the results of a low porosity, low permeability case for both the fracture and
matrix networks (matrix: 4%, 1 mD; fracture: 0.1%, 100 mD). The left panel shows results from
an AOF production strategy, while the right panel shows results from a rate-limited strategy. In
the AOF case, the high-pressure scenario (1,150 psi) outperformed the low pressure scenario
(450 psi) as with the first porosity/permeability combination tested. However, oil production does
not plateau as it did in the other case. It is unclear why this is the case, but it could be due to the
lower degree of contrast between matrix and fracture permeabilities. In the rate-limited case, the
low pressure treatment does indeed outperform the high pressure treatment. Qualitatively
similar results are seen for a high matrix poro/perm (12%, 100 mD), low fracture poro/perm
(0.1%, 200 mD) case (not pictured). While it is hard to draw exact quantitative relationships
based on these results, this does show that for certain reservoir parameter distributions – even
homogeneous ones – low pressure CO2 huff and puffs can outperform their high pressure
counterparts.

In summary, a combination of reservoir heterogeneity (both the multi-porosity nature of the
reservoir and heterogeneity within each porosity network) and a rate-limited production strategy
appear to be responsible for the efficacy of low-pressure huff and puff scenarios suggested from
full-complexity models. These findings helped to further inform the results of additional
simulations run on the full complexity reservoir model.

Figure 134. Sensitivity results for low matrix poro/perm and low fracture poro/perm cases at two different
pressure levels. The left panel shows an AOF production strategy and right shows rate-limited

Full Complexity Model – Operational Sensitivities

Additional full-complexity models were run to assess the impact on recovery of various
operational considerations – specifically CO2 injection rate and volume, recycling of non-oil well
stream components, and production rate – to further investigate the low-pressure huff and puff

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      132

scenarios and to help inform field operations. The first set of simulations was run to assess the
uptick from CO2 over a pure water injection. To compare with the five scenarios illustrated in
Figure 126 (3,000 tonne huff and puff at 550, 750, 950, 1,150, 1,350 psi target pressures), five
additional scenarios were run where the CO2 injection phase was skipped. The results of these
10 scenarios are co-plotted in Figure . The addition of CO2 provides a substantial recovery
uptick at early times over water-only scenarios. This observation is true at all modeled
pressures, indicating that even in the absence of full miscibility, CO2 treatment is effective at
mobilizing oil. In the 550 psi scenario, the closest to actual field implementation, the difference
in cumulative oil over the first year with CO2 vs. only water is about 6,000 barrels, a recovery
increase of ~60%.

The second set of simulations was run to investigate the contribution from miscible behavior of
CO2 and oil in the reservoir. In addition to the five huff and puff scenarios already reported on,
two additional runs were made with higher average reservoir pressures prior to CO2 injection
(1,650 psi and 1,900 psi). The reservoir pressures during the repressurization (water injection)
phase are shown in Figure , with the lab-obtained minimum miscibility pressure of 1,300 psi co-
plotted. The times at which miscible phase behavior is contributing to oil mobility correspond to
the time the average reservoir pressure spends above the horizontal minimum miscibility
pressure (MMP) line, so the degree to which miscibility impacts overall recovery can be
understood by comparing the higher pressure scenarios (green), with the lower pressure
scenarios (blue). The oil rate corresponding to each of these scenarios is shown in Figure . The
vertical axis is cutoff to better show the differences in the qualitative behaviors of each scenario.
Initial production (first ~day) in the two highest pressure scenarios exceed 100 bpd, with the
1,900 psi scenario showing oil rates as high as 175 bpd. Cumulative recovery is shown in
Figure 138. Cumulative production versus production time (top) and versus date (bottom)
across the seven repressurization scenarios from Figure 136., with the top panel showing the
cumulative curves synchronized and the bottom panel showing them in real time.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               133

Figure 135. Oil production rate (top) and cumulative production (bottom) for CO2 treatment vs. water-only
treatment across five different repressurization scenarios.

Figure 136. Average reservoir pressures in each of the seven different repressurization scenarios run to
test the impact of miscibility. Colors are consistent across scenarios in Figure 129 and Figure 130.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    134

Figure 137. Oil production rate in the first 250 days for the 7 repressurization scenarios in Figure 128.

Figure 138. Cumulative production versus production time (top) and versus date (bottom) across the
seven repressurization scenarios from Figure 136.

As can be seen in Figure 137, the rate curves appear to have two distinct features – a “miscible
displacement” peak and an “immiscible displacement” peak. The miscible displacement peak
occurs at early times in the scenarios where miscibility is reached and maintained for at least
some portion of production (green curves – see Figure 136). This period of production shows
very high oil rates until the reservoir depletes past MMP (1,300 psi), at which point production
falls off. The miscible displacement period in the highest pressure scenario (dark green curve)
lasts around 50 days, while this period in the 1,650 psi and 1,350 psi scenarios lasts a shorter
amount of time. The blue curves, which correspond to scenarios that never exceed MMP at any
point, do not have this miscible displacement period.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         135

In scenarios with a miscible displacement period of production, an immiscible displacement
period follows. In Figure 137, this presents as a second peak in the oil rate. Immiscible
production is the only peak in scenarios where MMP is not reached. The local maximum in this
peak corresponds to the point at which the production well switches from a rate constraint (600
bpd) to a BHP constraint (AOF) – in other words, the point at which the near wellbore region is
depleted to the point that the reservoir can no longer deliver 600 bpd to the producing well.

Based on the figure, for higher pressure scenarios, the immiscible displacement peak appears to
both have a lower local maximum and to occur later than observed in the lower pressure
scenarios. The 550 psi scenario peaks around 60 bpd on day 55, while the 1,900 psi scenario
peaks around 48 bpd on day 195.

One might expect, then, that based on the high recovery seen during the miscible displacement
period, the higher pressure scenarios would see greater cumulative recovery. However, as
shown in the top panel of Figure 138, this is not the case. The scenarios with miscible
displacement do have a higher cumulative recovery after the first 50 days or so, but the
immiscible displacement in the lower pressure scenarios still results in a higher cumulative
recovery over the first year of production. Starting with the lowest pressure scenario (dark blue),
increasing pressure serves to decrease cumulative recovery, likely due to the higher volume of
gas (and therefore greater reservoir volume touched) in the lowest pressure scenario. This trend
reverses itself once miscibility comes into play (between the 1,150 psi and 1,350 psi scenarios –
light blue to light green). From 1,300 psi and higher, increasing pressure serves to increase total
recovery, causing the 1,900 psi scenario to have the second-highest total recovery of all of the
models run after one year. Presumably, if the project could continue increasing the average
reservoir pressure prior to CO2 injection, a high pressure scenario could overtake the 550 psi
case as the optimal recovery scenario. However, given the expected formation fracture pressure
within the reservoir, obtaining an average reservoir pressure higher than 1,900 psi would be
difficult, leaving the 550 psi case as the optimal EOR strategy for the field based on modeling
results.

The time component of these different treatment scenarios is also an important piece of the
puzzle. Until now, synchronized versions of the recovery curves have been the focus, looking
only at time since initial production. However, when considering the additional time spent on
repressurization (water injection) for the higher pressure scenarios, the 550 psi case becomes a
clear-cut optimal scenario. The bottom panel of Figure 130 shows the cumulative recovery
curves plotted in real time, where the timeline to repressurization is considered. Since lower
pressure scenarios begin production at an earlier time, the operator is able to realize production
revenue earlier. At the beginning of 2026, when the highest pressure scenario would just be
turning over to production, the low pressure case (550 psi) has already netted 12,000 barrels of
oil. Not only does this case result in the highest cumulative production, but that production
occurs significantly earlier than the higher pressure cases, providing significantly higher value to
the operator.

It is clear, however, that in this dual-porosity model, miscible CO2 behavior causes a significant
recovery uptick. The periods of miscible displacement in Figure 136 correspond to oil production
rates that are as high as or higher than the peak rates seen in immiscible displacement. If
pressures above MMP could be maintained, that scenario would almost certainly see the
highest incremental recovery. One possible avenue for maintaining high reservoir pressure is
recycling the non-oil components of the production stream – that is, pumping all produced fluid
(except for oil) back into the reservoir to slow the rate of pressure depletion. Four additional

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              136

simulations were conducted to analyze the impact of a recycled well-stream, with pre-CO2
injection reservoir pressures of 550, 750, 950 and 1,900 psi. The results of these simulations,
co-plotted with results without recycling, are shown in Figure 139.

Figure 139. Oil rate (top) and cumulative recovery (bottom) for 4 repressurization cases, both with and
without well stream recycling. Top panel is plotted in real time, while bottom panel is synchronized to start
of production.

As can be seen in the figure, reinjection of the well stream (dashed lines) leads to a significant
decrease in oil recovery compared to scenarios without recycling (solid lines). Oil rates are
reduced by as much as 50% in matching pressure scenarios, and cumulative volume is reduced
by as much as 8,000 barrels over the first year – twice that much over two years. This is true
regardless of reservoir pressure – all four of the scenarios show a substantial decline in
production. The primary reason for this is the high conductivity of the fracture/vug network. Re-
injected fluid flows into this high-permeability system and, because the well creates a pressure
sink during production, that fluid channels back to the production well, bypassing oil volumes in
the matrix system. In a pure huff and puff scenario, as the fracture/vug system continues to
deplete, fluids flow out of the matrix and into the high conductivity channels where they can
easily flow into the production wellbore. Figure 140, which shows the water cut in the production
stream for the same scenarios, illustrates this point. In the scenarios without recycling, water cut
gradually decreases as matrix oil continues to be produced. In the scenarios with recycling,
water cut holds steady around 96-98% throughout the entire production cycle. This suggests
that any sort of flood scenario without significant mobility control is likely a sub-optimal recovery
strategy. Further simulations investigating the efficacy of a waterflood without mobility control
confirm this finding. Figure 141 shows cumulative recovery for four water-only scenarios with
and without well stream recycling, and the same conclusion can be reached from these results.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       137

Figure 140. Water cut in four production scenarios with and without well stream recycling (reinjection and
no reinjection, respectively).

Figure 141. Cumulative recovery in water-only scenarios with and without water reinjection (orange and
blue respectively).

Full Complexity Model – Simulations to Inform Field Operations

A final set of simulations was run on the history-matched model to help inform injection and
production strategies during field testing. The first set of simulations was motivated by a loss in
available CO2 volumes due to logistical challenges encountered during field testing. Three
scenarios were run to assess the impact on recovery due to lower injected CO2 volumes. Figure
142 shows the oil rate (top) and cumulative oil (bottom) comparison of a huff and puff conducted
with 3,000, 2,000, 1,000 and 0 tonnes of CO2 at an expected reservoir pressure of 550 psi
(3,000 and 0 tonne scenarios correspond to the dark blue, black curves in Figure 143). As one
might expect, a lower CO2 volume does lead to less incremental recovery than a higher CO2
volume. However, even the 1,000 tonne scenario shows an increase in recovery compared to
the baseline case with no CO2, indicating the potential for project success even with a lower
volume of CO2.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     138

Figure 142. Oil rate (top) and cumulative recovery (bottom) for huff and puff scenarios with four different
CO2 volumes (0, 1,000, 2,000, 3,000 tonnes).

Once the final procured volume of CO2 (2,000 tonnes) was determined, additional simulations
were conducted to determine the optimum rate at which the CO2 should be injected into the
reservoir. Figure 143 shows the oil rate resulting from a 2,000 tonne huff and puff where the
CO2 was injected at 1,000 tonnes per day (tpd, black, solid line), 500 tpd (gray, dashed line) and
250 tpd (light gray, dotted line). A higher injection rate during the “huff” phase does appear to
slightly increase peak oil rates during the “puff” due to slightly higher oil cuts (likely pushing
more water away from the wellbore during gas injection). The difference between the scenarios
was minimal, and it was determined that CO2 injection should proceed at as high a rate as
reasonably practicable while staying well below estimated reservoir fracture pressure.

Figure 143. Oil rate comparison of 2000t huff and puff with three different injection rates (1,000 tpd, 500
tpd, 250 tpd).

A final suite of simulations was conducted to inform an optimal production strategy. Using the
2,000 tonne, 550 psi huff and puff scenario, four different production target rates were assessed
– 600 bpd, 400 bpd, 300 bpd, and 200 bpd. These rates correspond to the anticipated pump

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       139

operations, with the overall constraint on production rates still being the AOF condition (i.e.,
once the reservoir can no longer deliver the target rate, well constraint becomes BHP of 0 psig).
Figure 144 shows the oil rate (top), cumulative oil (middle) and water cut (bottom) of these four
production scenarios. The results indicate that a higher production rate produces more
cumulative oil. The 400 bpd and 600 bpd perform somewhat similarly, but lower production
rates perform much worse over the first 1.5 years. In some cases, a lower rate constraint is able
to reduce water cut at a production well, but that does not appear to be the case within the Lee
field. Water cut is actually lowest for the highest production rate scenario. Additional model
outputs were assessed to help plan for impending field operations. The gas production rate over
time for each case was assessed to inform surface compression unit procurement and
operation, and the operating BHP was assessed to inform pump operation at the production well
(since artificial lift was needed).

Figure 144. Oil rate (top), cumulative oil (middle) and water cut (bottom) for four different production
scenarios of a 2000t huff and puff.

Modeling efforts accomplished in Task 4 provided useful results to help inform field operations.
The sensitivities assessed ensured that operational planning proceeded with minimal delays
and functional changes when necessary. Key uncertainties that remain include the exact impact
of compositional simulation and surfactant inclusion on the results. Relative permeability,
capillary pressure curves and vertical to horizontal permeability anisotropy could all be better

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        140

constrained by field data. In addition, the impact of dynamic fracture systems was not
investigated and may serve to play a critical role in accurately modeling these systems. Future
modeling efforts would do well to assess these remaining uncertainties.

6.0 Task 5: Field Injection Tests
Reporting on this task, which focused on implementation of the CO2-EOR design to inject
chemically enabled CO2 into the TBR, collect advanced characterization data from the injection
well, and establish baseline measurements, will focus exclusively on activities at the Lee 26 field
since no injection was attempted at the McCann 1-20 well.

6.1 Subtask 5.1: Site Preparation at the Marshell 1-35, Lee 26 Field,
 Calhoun County
Details of field selection method, stimulation method, and CO2 procurement are described in
Section 1.0 of this document. This section will focus on work completed to prepare the Lee 26
field for surfactant-enhanced CO2 stimulation.

Production Facility Design

Surface production equipment required to produce the Fischhaber 2-35 following CO2 injection
was sited at the adjacent Marshell 1-35 well pad. This pad already housed oil and gas
processing facilities from the primary producing phase of the field, and much of the equipment
already in place was re-purposed for project needs.

The purpose of the production facility was to control the rate and pressure of flowback and to
separate the well stream into produced oil (for sale), produced water (for reinjection or disposal)
and produced gas (for reinjection). Additionally, the facility was designed to control and capture
gas emissions resulting from pressure let-down as oil reaches the stock tanks. Mainly, the
facility consists of the following process units: choke valve, mainline compressor, heater treater,
stock tanks, and vapor recovery compressor. The specification of each process unit was based
on its operating pressure and flow capacity. The notional process flow diagram of the facility is
given in Figure 145.

The production facility controls the rate of production and reduces pressure using a choke valve
near the wellhead. The choke valve has a variable orifice that can be adjusted manually or
automatically, based on feedback from a flow or pressure measurement device. The well stream
directly enters the heater treater, which was already located on the Marshell pad. The heater-
treater operates similarly to the high pressure separator, but includes a heated compartment to
drive volatile components out of the liquid stream, with such volatiles mixing into the gas phase
exiting the top of the vessel. Produced gas effluent from the top of the vessel is sent to a
multistage compressor for re-injection directly into the Marshell well. The heater-treater is fitted
with float operated liquid dump valves for both water and oil, and the resulting liquid streams are
sent to their respective stock tanks. From the stock tanks, the water may be transferred by
pump for re-injection into the Beattie 1-27 and/or Marshell 1-35, and the oil may be trucked to
market. Tank vapors, if combustible, are routed to a flare stack with a standing pilot for safety.

This approach maximizes the amount of flash gas that can be recovered from the well and all
but eliminates venting and flaring losses.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              142

Figure 145. Process Flow Diagram of proposed Production Facility

For the most part, the facility is conventional in design and operation philosophy and presents
low technical risk. Because the well requires artificial lift to bring the produced oil to surface, the
initial rate of production was estimated based on reservoir dynamic model simulations and all
downstream equipment was sized based on that initial production rate. Additionally, the
production facility located at the Marshell pad rather than the Fischhaber pad, which will result in
a flowline of 0.5 miles in length. The well was expected to produce paraffin waxes, which may
occlude the flowline and require periodic maintenance (flushing) to clear. These issues are
mostly operational and not out of the ordinary, but the paraffin mitigation plan required special
attention to avoid plugging the underground flowline, which is not easily serviceable. The most
prevalent unknown during facility design was the behavior of CO2 throughout the process, and
how the complex phase behavior of CO2 will influence the performance of lift, separation, and
compression equipment. Ultimately, since relatively low volumes of water and CO2 were
produced, CO2 phase behavior was not an issue during the production period.
The Marshell production facility was largely completed in the first quarter of 2025. The facility
includes all the elements of the process flow diagram of Figure 145.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 143

Figure 146. Marshell Production Facility as of March 4, 2025

Facility Design Outcomes and Lessons Learned

Because the Fischhaber 2-35 well did not produce measurable oil, there was limited data
available to analyze the performance of the facility implemented at the Marshell 1-35 pad.
However, the discussion below reflects lessons learned during this study that can be applied to
future CO2-EOR development.

For pipeline and facility designs at scales from individual pads to full-field systems, a process
simulation should be initiated prior to construction, preferably using a Pressure-Volume-
Temperature (PVT) study from offset production when available. In the absence of such data,
an estimated fluid composition and anticipated production rates should be developed. The
primary objective of the process is the effective separation of gas, oil, and water through the
application of gravity, residence time, and heat. Gas is routed to the pipeline system, while oil
and water are directed to storage tanks. For oils with higher API gravity, the final oil–water
separation stage typically requires heat application to remove components that would otherwise
vaporize at atmospheric pressure. Vapors generated from treaters, towers, and tanks must be
captured and sent either to a flare system or to flash compression, depending on economic
considerations.

The design and installation of production facilities requires extensive experience, as proficiency
is generally achieved only after multiple iterations and sustained field exposure, so facility
designs that have worked well in the TBR trend in the past should be emulated, but updated
and optimized for safe CO2 handling. Foundational concepts associated with effective facility
design are widely documented and remain universally applicable across production operations.
Within industry, many individuals with production backgrounds possess familiarity with the
established methods; however, organizations lacking seasoned expertise may require additional
guidance from engineers and facility operators with experience in the TBR as well as engineers
and operators with experience handling CO2.

Carbon dioxide introduces unique challenges due to limited industry-wide experience with its
production and reinjection. In the near term, flow assurance represents the primary concern.
Carbon dioxide, water, and methane have a tendency to form hydrates under elevated pressure

Battelle | May 2026                                                                            144

and moderately low temperature conditions, particularly near control valves or in downhole
environments where CO₂ may undergo a phase change from liquid to vapor, resulting in
temperature drops below hydrate formation thresholds. Common mitigation strategies include
gas dehydration (up to the critical point), methanol injection, heat application, and staged
pressure reduction. Over longer periods, corrosion emerges as a key issue when CO₂ and water
coexist. The choice between a materials-based solution and a monitoring-based solution should
be driven by equipment age, evaluation of safety thresholds, and project economics.

Carbon dioxide also presents additional hazards due to its non-combustible nature and its ability
to displace oxygen. Vapors typically managed safely through combustion can transition between
combustible and non-combustible states as CO₂ concentration increases, potentially
extinguishing pilot flames and allowing dangerous accumulations of flammable gases. To
address this risk, facility designs should incorporate methods for removing CO₂ from the
solution prior to emissions control systems. Indirect heat application within an oil heater-treater
and the installation of a low-pressure vapor recovery tower, upstream of storage tanks, can be
effective approaches to ensure tank vapors remain combustible and suitable for flaring.

Because CO₂ is an asphyxiant, sufficient air quality monitoring is required to protect personnel.

When present as a cryogenic liquid, CO₂ will generate significant pressure increases if warmed
while confined, reaching pressures of approximately 750 psi. Therefore, relief devices must be
properly installed to safeguard equipment. Additionally, liquid CO₂ released to ambient
conditions can rapidly form dry ice, which may temporarily block a line break before violently
discharging as internal pressure increases. Carbon dioxide equipment must be depressured to
gas phase and warmed prior to maintenance activities. Operational personnel should receive
appropriate training to understand and manage these hazards.

From a safety perspective, the most common risks for CO2-EOR operations involve loss of
containment and the accumulation of flammable gas. API Recommended Practice 14C provides
comprehensive guidance on these hazards for typical installations and represents a durable
reference for ensuring the safe operation of production facilities.

6.2 Subtask 5.2: Baseline Site Characterization and Monitoring

Spectral Pulsed Neutron Logging: Baseline data collection, January 2023

SPN logging was planned to evaluate two aspects of the Lee field to evaluate pre-injection
reservoir saturations of oil, gas and water, to be assessed using Baker-Hughes' SPN tool, with
the intention of making repeated runs with the tool during water and CO2 injection periods to
understand change in reservoir saturation over time.

Logging commenced during the week of January 23, 2023. Baker’s logging crew mobilized to
Fischhaber 2-35 and ran in hole with the deep shear wave tool (Figure 147). During down-
logging (running into the tool with sensors on to provide tool calibration), the tool encountered
an unexpected plug at 4,600 ft measured depth, 18 ft above the expected plug-back total depth.

A fluid column was expected in the Fischhaber 2-35, but no fluid was present upon
commencement of logging operations. Both the SPN and deep shear wave tools require a fluid
column to gather data, so logging of the Fischhaber well was put on hold, and the logging crew
mobilized to the Marshell 1-35.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             145

Similar conditions were encountered at the Marshell 1-35, with insufficient fluid column to
acquire logs. An attempt was made to trickle water down the hole to establish a temporary fluid
column, but this method was unsuccessful.

Figure 147. Baker Hughes crew preparing to run wireline logs at the Fischhaber 2-35 well.

Baker-Hughes was able to run the SPN tool in a gas environment with a proprietary sleeve to
run the tool inside. That sleeve was shipped to Michigan, and SPN logs were acquired in a gas-
filled Marshell 1-35 wellbore on February 7, 2023. Calibrated logs were delivered to Battelle and
West Bay on March 2, 2023, and revealed a lack of cement behind casing across most of the
logged interval. That lack of cement created a fluid gap behind casing, thwarting the SPN tool’s
ability to accurately assess reservoir saturations. With this well condition, Battelle and West Bay
concluded that running repeat logs with the SPN tool over the course of repressurization and
EOR would not yield useful data in the Marshell 1-35, so no further logs were run in this well.
However, SPN logs were collected in the Fischhaber 2-35 both before and after CO2 injection,
as discussed later in Section 6.2.

Lee 26 Field Baseline Pressure Testing
Baseline pressure monitoring was conducted at the Lee field in early 2023 to establish baseline
conditions prior to the start of water injection. This was done through the use of fluid shots to
test fluid levels in the wells and dip-in pressure readings with wireline gauges. Fluid shots taken
at the Marshell well on January 6 and January 26, 2023, showed consistent fluid levels of 4,812
ft and 4,843 ft TVD. Assuming a freshwater gradient, this corresponds to a bottomhole pressure
of between 30 and 52 psig, suggesting pressure did not decrease substantially since the
readings taken in 2018. Fluid level was also measured at Jacoby on March 15, 2023, at 4,018 ft
TVD. Assuming freshwater gradient, this corresponds to a bottomhole pressure of 350 psig.
This measurement was fairly consistent with fluid levels taken at the well in 2020. At the time,
these values were regarded as anomalous, likely due to wellbore completion elements skewing
the fluid shot response. While this still may be the case, the low density fluid column observed in
the Fischhaber well during gradient runs later in the project (see Section 5.3) suggests these
fluid shots could have been seeing a column of this same low density condensate.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             146

Dip-in pressure measurements were taken at the Marshell, Beattie, and Jacoby wells on June 1,
2023. These measurements are much more accurate than pressures interpreted from fluid
levels. Pressures of 3 psig, 107 psig, and 40 psig were measured at the Beattie, Jacoby and
Marshell wells, respectively. The low pressure reading at Beattie was taken at a depth much
shallower than the reservoir top and was likely not submerged due to low reservoir pressures.
The reading at Marshell was consistent with the fluid level interpretations taken in January.
Pressure at the Jacoby well was still anomalously high relative to the rest of the field, though not
nearly as high as suggested by fluid level data. This elevated pressure may have been due to
constrictions in the well from completion elements, or it may have been caused by a lack of
connection to the rest of the field after closure of a high conductivity pathway that served as the
primary connection to the rest of the reservoir. Existence of this dynamic fracture pathway is
suggested by pressure data gathered during water injection operations, as discussed in the next
section.

6.3 Subtask 5.3: Injection Operations
No injection operations were attempted at the McCann 1-20 well. At the Lee 26 field, injection of
CO2 was completed in March and April of 2025, preceded by approximately17 months of water
injection to repressurize the field prior to CO2-EOR operations. The Lee field injection operations
are detailed below.

Lee 26 Water Injection

Due to the low pressures present in the depleted Lee 26 reservoir, water injection was
conducted to repressurize the field prior to CO2 injection. The goal of this water injection was to
raise average pressure of a sector of the field (between Marshell and Fischhaber, the proposed
injector-producer pair) above MMP to facilitate miscible CO2 treatment of the residual oil. Water
injection began at the Marshell well at the end of September 2023, using water sourced from a
nearby remediation well. Injection operations were conducted only through the Marshell to
minimize watering out of residual oil in the field. Water injection concluded in February 2025, in
preparation for CO2 injection. Daily water injection data from the Marshell 1-35 is shown in
Figure 148. Injection was maintained at a rate between 1,500-2,500 bpd; a total of 957,870
barrels of water were injected over 17 months.

Figure 148. Water injection rate data from the Marshell 1-35 water injection well. Note rate decrease
during the second half of 2024. Injection rate subsequently increased after repair of purge water pump at
water source.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    147

Real time wellhead pressure (WHP) monitoring data was collected via a supervisory control and
data acquisition (SCADA) network at each of the wells. The SCADA system came online in
September 2023, concurrent with the start of water injection. Tubing pressure data collected
during water injection is shown in Figure 149. During the first six months of injection (9/2023
through 3/2024), while all wells besides Marshell were shut in, the WHP data provided an
excellent view of pressure front migration through the reservoir. The first marked increase in
pressure was observed at the Beattie well in early October 2023, indicating the presence of a
high-conductivity channel between the Marshell and Beattie wells. WHP at the Fischhaber
began rising slowly in mid-late October 2023, and pressures at the Jacoby never responded to
water injection during this period. In March 2024, field operators began the sampling campaign
for water samples from offset wells, requiring well blowdowns and other manipulations, at which
point wellhead data became an unreliable proxy for subsurface pressures. Pressures at
Marshell were negative for the entirety of water injection, as it was continually injecting on a
vacuum. Casing pressures were also monitored during this period to ensure well integrity –
nothing of note was observed in this data.

Figure 149. Wellhead pressure data collected during water injection phase. Spikes in Jacoby data
correspond to well work operations.

Downhole pressure monitoring was also conducted at the Lee 26 field from September 2023
through the end of the production testing in November 2025. Bottomhole gauges were installed
in each of the four wells at different times and were pulled periodically throughout injection
operations to retrieve data. In addition, the ESPs installed in the Fischhaber 2-35 well during
production testing (see Section 6.2) provided pressure data during the post-treatment
production period. The final realized schedule of gauge installation and retrieval is shown in
Table 25.

Raw pressure data from the downhole gauges during water injection are shown in Figure 150,
and a datum corrected version of the same data (flattened on the pressure prior to production
startup on August 28, 2025) is shown in Figure 151. Pressure steadily increased at the injecting
Marshell well from the beginning of data collection. A marked response was not observed at the
Fischhaber well until March of 2024, at which point the Marshell and Fischhaber pressure
curves increased in parallel (likely indicative of boundary-dominated flow). Large drops in
pressures at various times during injection correlate with operations on surface infrastructure.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                   149

Figure 151. Datum-corrected bottom-hole pressure data from the Fischhaber (orange), Marshell (blue),
Jacoby (black), and Beattie (red) wells during water injection.

A significant change occurred in the pressure response across the field in early January 2025.
Prior to this date, pressures at the Jacoby well are substantially lower than elsewhere in the field
(including at the Beattie well). The direction of pressure gradients within the field suggests that
fluid was flowing through a channel from Marshell and Fischhaber toward the Beattie well, then
flowing back to the Jacoby. The combination of lower pressures and muted pressure responses
(i.e. slower rates of change) at Jacoby before January 2025 suggests lower conductivity
connection with the reservoir, as compared to the rest of the wells. From January 8-10, 2025, a
250 psi increase was observed at the Jacoby well, correlating with a slope change in pressure
increases at the other three wells. This likely corresponds to the opening of a high conductivity
fracture between the Jacoby section of the reservoir and the area around Fischhaber. After this
date, pressures at Jacoby and Fischhaber were nearly identical, pressure changes were nearly
instantaneous with changes at Marshell, and fluid flows from east to west through the field,
according to pressure gradients. Pressure fell off substantially in the weeks preceding CO2
injection, while the field was shutin, indicating a >200 psi difference pressures while injecting
and static reservoir pressures. This is likely due to fluid flow from the high conductivity fractures
and vugs into the matrix dolomite porosity network.

Bottomhole temperature was also monitored using the downhole gauges in each well.
Temperature data from the water injection period is shown in Figure 152 below. Temperatures
at both Beattie and Jacoby were roughly constant for the period when gauges were installed
and show the impact of installation depth in their temperature values (i.e. the shallowest gauge,
Beattie, shows the lowest temperatures of all non-injection wells). Temperatures at Marshell
were much lower than elsewhere in the field (due to water injected from surface) and increased
rapidly during periods of low/no injection. These temperatures also cycle seasonally from the
injected water. Fischhaber temperatures fluctuated slightly throughout injection but mostly
remained around 100-102 degrees Fahrenheit.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    150

 Figure 152. Bottomhole temperature data across the Lee field during water injection.

Fluid level shots were also collected from the three non-injection wells during the second half of
2024 and are shown in Figure 153. Fluid levels at the Fischhaber 2-35 well and Jacoby 1-26
well were elevated relative to the fluid level at the Beattie 1-27 at the beginning of the fluid shot
campaign in July, but the Beattie level rapidly rose to meet the level of the other two wells in
August. The three wells, more or less, followed the same trend from this point forward. The
lagging fluid level in the Beattie 1-27 in July 2024 may be the result of a closed fracture
restricting fluid flow to that region of the reservoir, or the result of fluid preferentially flowing
toward the Fischhaber well at that point during repressurization. Interestingly, this timeline does
not correlate with the fracture opening in early January 2025 observed in bottomhole pressure
data. This could indicate that there was more than one fracture opening and closing in the
system based on different pressure states in the reservoir.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                151

Figure 153. Fluid level shots from non-repressurization wells in the Lee 26 field, July 2024 through
December 2024.

Given the failure of baseline SPN logging to provide context on reservoir saturation changes, the
team deployed water tracers during the repressurization period. These water-soluble tracers,
provided by ProTechnics, are designed to provide data around reservoir connectivity and the
transient time of water from the injection well to the non-injection wells in the field.

Figure 154 illustrates the geographic relation of the wells to each other and shows the
conceptualized flow pathway from the Marshell injection well to the other wells in the field.

Twenty-four gallons of tracer was injected into the Marshell well on September 29, 2023, upon
commencement of repressurization operations. After five months of injection, water levels rose
sufficiently to allow fluid samples to be captured at the Fischhaber well in March 2024, and
sampling was initiated in the Jacoby 1-26 well a month later in April 2024. The first successful
water samples to be collected at the Beattie well occurred in September 2024. Fluid samples
were collected bi-weekly. The resulting tracer concentrations (in parts per trillion) and salinity
concentrations (in parts per million) are shown in Figures 155 and 156, respectively.

Tracers were present in every sample from all three wells, with the highest concentrations of
both tracer and salinity at the Jacoby well over the 10-month sampling period. These results
indicate strong transmissibility across the reservoir and likely also indicate that the main tracer
slug had already passed through the Fischhaber well before the wellbore built a sufficient water
column to be sampled. Likewise, with fluid salinity concentrations significantly lower at the
Fischhaber well than at Jacoby, it is believed that the fresh repressurization water injected at the
Marshell well had reached and diluted the near wellbore brines in the Fischhaber reservoir
before fluid sampling occurred. Concentrations of both tracer and salinity at the Beattie well
were lower than the Jacoby and higher than the Fischhaber until the final sample collection,

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    152

indicating that injection fluids moved past the Beattie well and were beginning to dilute with
continued injection. It may also indicate that the Beattie and Fischhaber wells are in more direct
hydraulic communication with one another, and that the Jacoby well received an isolated pulse
of tracer-laden fluid early on during repressurization, but that the conduit that had previously
been open has now been closed or blocked, isolating the Jacoby wellbore from continued
freshwater flow. Tracer concentration at the Jacoby well dropped by two orders of magnitude
from October 15 to November 5, while salinity increased substantially at both Jacoby and
Beattie over the same time period. Salinities varied substantially at Jacoby from that time
through the end of sampling.

Figure 154. Fluid tracer injection and sampling diagram, Lee 26 field. Tracers were injected into the
Marshell well, and sampling will occur at the remaining 3 wells to understand flow pathways of injected
water across the field.

Figure 155. Tracer concentration (parts per trillion) in bi-weekly samples from the Fischhaber 2-35, Jacoby
1-26 HD2, and Beattie 1-27 HD1 wells. Note the logarithmic scale on the y-axis.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       153

Figure 156. Salinity data (parts per million) captured from the Fischhaber 2-35, Jacoby 1-26 HD2, and
Beattie 1-27 HD1 wells.

Tracer concentrations and salinity variations in these wells over the course of the injection
period lend further weight to the idea that the Lee 26 field (and likely TBR fields generally) have
highly complex, fracture- and vug-dominated plumbing. The fact that water moved across the
field before effective sample collection could be completed also indicates that highly permeable
pathways extend across the field. It is also possible that the large shifts in both salinity and
tracer concentration over the course of the repressurization period (particularly the major salinity
shifts from September 2024 to January 2025, Figure 156) were caused by pressure-dependent
opening and closing of fractures within the reservoir and/or other complex flow pathways
(spillpoints, etc.) throughout the system.

and pressure monitoring (Figure 157), and an afternoon visit to the Fischhaber 2-35 pad to walk
down the CO2 huff & puff site and the Marshell 2-35 pad to walk down the production and CO2
reinjection site (Figure 158).
