Stage 1 Surfactant and CO2 Injection Operations

Injection operations (the ‘huff’ portion of huff and puff) commenced on March 3, 2025, with
injection of 422 barrels of surfactant laden water into the Fischhaber 2-35 well. Injection was
completed at 2.3 barrels per minute (bpm), with the well taking the entire volume on vacuum
(Figure 163). Messer then pressure tested the CO2 injection lines and initiated CO2 injection,
ramping up from one bpm to two bpm. An issue was identified with the injection flow meter, and
operations were shut down for the day to address the issue, which was found to be a dry ice
blockage. Injection resumed on March 4, 2025 and proceeded with no further operational
pauses (Figures 164 and 165). Injection was completed March 5, 2025 (Figure 166), with
injection pressure below 200 psi for the majority of the injection period.

Stage 2 Surfactant and CO2 Injection Operations

Stage 2 CO2 delivery and storage operations were initiated on April 7, 2025 and proceeded
through April 13, 2025, with a total of 1,011 tons of CO2 delivered and stored (Figure 167). A
short standby period was needed before the team was able to pull bottom hole gauges from the
Fischhaber (Figure 168). Stage 2 injection operations commenced on April 16, 2025, with
removal of the downhole gauges. On April 17, 2025, 422 barrels of surfactant laden water into
the Fischhaber 2-35 well, followed by CO2 injection. Initial injection was completed at about
three bmp, with the well taking the entire volume at between 250 to 300 psi discharge pressure,
well within the expected range based on performance during Stage 1 (Figure 169). Metering on
the injection spread failed after about 550 tons of CO2 had been injected, and the Messer team
manually monitored tank pressure and volume to complete the remainder of injection at three to
four bmp . Injection was completed April 18, 2025.

Pressure Monitoring

Pressure monitoring continued through CO2 injection using both the installed downhole gauges
and SCADA WHP monitoring system. Datum-corrected BHP data (using the same datum
correction as the water injection data) is shown in Figure 170. Fischhaber bottomhole data is
missing during the actual injection as the gauge had to be removed during this period. BHP at
the Fischhaber well increased to as high as 1,050 psi during the first phase of CO2 injection,
with a long pressure falloff observed during the soak periods. Pressures at the other three wells
displayed very similar qualitative behavior, indicating high connectivity across the formation.
Pressures at the Marshell increased further than at Jacoby, likely due to the Marshell’s proximity
to the boundary of the field. Pressures at Beattie decreased by the lowest amount due to being
furthest from the injector. Pressures remained elevated at the Fischhaber well relative to the
rest of the field during the falloff period until approximately one month after injection, at which
point pressure decreases to a level more consistent with the other three wells. This may be due
to the presence of foam near the Fischhaber well and subsequent foam breakage one month
after injection. Beneath the pressure signatures due to CO2 injection, a background pressure
decrease is observed in the data from continued falloff from water injection and fluid flow
between the vug/fracture and matrix systems. For this reason, pressures during the second
injection period were not as high as those observed during the first injection.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              162

Figure 170. Datum-corrected bottomhole pressures at all four wells during the CO2 injection and soak
periods.

Wellhead pressure data from the same period is shown in Figure 171. Note that the Fischhaber
data is shown on a separate y-axis from the rest of the wells. WHP at the Fischhaber reached
650 psi during the first phase of CO2 injection and roughly 600 psi during the second phase.
The high variability at the Fischhaber during the first injection period is due to poor signal
connectivity with the sensors. Pressure at the Jacoby well is increasing from the start of CO2
injection, likely indicating gas influx into the wellbore. A sharp increase is seen coincident with
the injection operations, which falls back off toward the background increasing trend. At the
Beattie well, a similar (though smaller) response was observed coincident with the first injection
phase, but no substantial response is seen during the second injection phase. No significant
pressure response is observed at Marshell.

data is plotted on a different axis.

Pressure Transient Analysis

Using gathered pressure data, a pressure transient analysis (PTA) of two falloff periods
following CO₂ injection into the Fischhaber well was conducted. The analysis focused on
identifying the dominant flow regime observed during each falloff period using rapid pressure-
derivative diagnostics. Emphasis was placed on flow-geometry interpretation (e.g., linear versus
radial behavior) and discussion of plausible flow scenarios consistent with the observed
response, based solely on the pressure data, in order to characterize the pressure-transient
behavior associated with CO₂ injection at this well. The PTA analysis was performed using IHS
WellTestTM 2024.1.

The primary input for this analysis was downhole pressure data acquired from the gauge
installed in the Fischhaber well. Figure 172 shows the pressure data used in both falloff periods
for analysis. Pressure measurements were recorded at one-minute intervals during the falloff
periods. CO₂ injection rates were used as input in units of tonnes per minute. Pressure data
during the active injection periods were not available; therefore, as an assumption, the first
recorded pressure at the start of each falloff period was used to represent the pressure at
injection shut-in. Additional input data included reservoir properties such as temperature,
thickness, post-waterflood fluid saturations, and oil properties, which are summarized in Table
26. These properties were derived from a combination of field data and modeling results from
Task 4. Fluid and reservoir properties were assumed to be constant over the analyzed falloff
periods.

Figure 172. bottom hole gauge pressure data provided

Battelle | May 2026                                                                            164

Table 26. Input parameters into model
 Input Value/Range
 Reservoir Temperature 98 F
 Gas Saturation 7%
 Oil Saturation 30%
 Water Saturation 63%
 Oil API 40
 Bubble Point Pressure 1620 psi
 Gas Oil Solution 350 scf/bbl
 Thickness 523 ft
 Porosity 10%

Figure 173 shows the pressure-derivative and type-curve match for the first CO₂ injection falloff
period. Pressure-derivative and type-curve plots are diagnostic tools used in pressure transient
analysis to identify flow regimes based on the slope and shape of the pressure response as a
function of time. The slope of the pressure derivative is particularly important because it reflects
the underlying flow geometry controlling pressure diffusion in the reservoir. During the main
portion of the falloff period, the pressure derivative exhibits a 1/2-slope behavior, consistent with
a linear-flow regime, indicating flow toward a high-conductivity planar feature rather than matrix-
dominated radial flow. At late time, the derivative trend steepens toward a slope approaching
unity, which may suggest the influence of a boundary or transition to a different flow regime.
However, due to data scatter and limited late-time stabilization, this interpretation should be
considered indicative rather than conclusive.

Figure 173. Derivative plot for first falloff cycle

While a zero slope in the pressure-derivative plot is diagnostic of matrix-dominated radial flow,
such behavior is not observed in this dataset. Instead, the derivative response is dominated by
a sustained 1/2-slope, indicating linear flow geometry throughout the main portion of the falloff
period. A 1/2-slope linear-flow response may be associated with flow toward a planar fracture or
a linear channel system, and PTA alone cannot uniquely distinguish between these two
scenarios. In linear channel flow, a transitional radial-flow period is often expected prior to the
onset of linear behavior; however, no clear zero-slope radial-flow transition is observed here.
This suggests either the presence of a very narrow channel that suppresses observable radial
flow or flow toward a dominant planar high-conductivity feature, such as a pre-existing or

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               165

injection-induced fracture.

Battelle | May 2026           166

A similar pressure-derivative response is observed during the second CO₂ injection falloff cycle
(Figure 174), confirming the observations from the first falloff period. Early-time data are
affected by noise and do not exhibit a stable diagnostic trend. Similar to the first cycle, a zero-
slope derivative indicative of matrix-dominated radial flow is not observed. Instead, the
derivative response is dominated by a sustained 1/2-slope, consistent with a linear-flow regime
throughout the main portion of the falloff period. Both linear fracture and linear channel type-
curve models provide reasonable matches to the data, reflecting the inherent non-uniqueness of
linear-flow interpretation using PTA alone. The absence of a clearly identifiable transitional
radial-flow regime suggests either a narrow channel geometry that suppresses observable
radial flow or flow toward a planar high-conductivity feature, such as a pre-existing or injection-
induced fracture. The repeatability of this response across both falloff cycles supports the
conclusion that linear flow dominates the pressure-transient behavior at the Fischhaber well.

Figure 174. Derivative plot for second falloff cycle

In conclusion, the pressure transient analysis of both CO₂ injection falloff periods at the
Fischhaber well consistently indicates a dominant linear-flow regime, as evidenced by a 1/2-
slope pressure-derivative response. The absence of a zero-slope radial-flow signature suggests
that matrix-dominated flow does not control the pressure behavior during the analyzed periods.
The repeatability of the linear-flow response across both falloff cycles increases confidence that
linear flow is the governing flow geometry during CO₂ injection and falloff at this well. The
sustained 1/2-slope linear-flow response observed in both falloff cycles indicates flow toward a
high-conductivity planar or elongated feature. Such behavior may be explained by either a
planar fracture or a narrow linear channel system, as PTA cannot uniquely distinguish between
these geometries. However, linear channel flow typically exhibits a transitional radial-flow
regime prior to the onset of linear behavior, which is not observed in the analyzed data. The
absence of a discernible radial-flow transition suggests either a very narrow channel geometry
that suppresses observable radial flow or flow toward a dominant planar fracture. Both linear
fracture flow and narrow channel flow reflect the presence of a high-permeability, high-
transmissibility pathway that dominates pressure diffusion relative to the surrounding matrix.

The analysis of transient pressure data following CO2 injection supports the subsurface
interpretation of the Lee reservoir and other TBR fields. In a reservoir with a system of
interconnected, high-conductivity vugs and fractures flowing through low permeability matrix, the
1/2-slope behavior could be created by these high conductivity channels. The feature that is
connected to the Fischhaber appears to be one of these high conductivity channels, within
which matrix-dominated flow does not appear in the month of data analyzed. If the residual oil is

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             167

all or mostly located within the matrix porosity system, as is suspected, the lack of matrix flow
could help explain why no oil was produced from the Fischhaber well. While the observed 1/2-
slope response clearly supports linear-flow behavior, PTA alone cannot uniquely distinguish
between a planar fracture and a linear channel system as the underlying high-transmissibility
feature. This uncertainty could be reduced by acquiring continuous downhole pressure data
during the injection periods, which would enable direct analysis of injection period and improve
early-time diagnostic interpretation. Additional improvement could be achieved by applying
similar high-resolution pressure data and PTA workflows to other wells in the field to assess the
consistency of flow regimes across the reservoir and better constrain the dominant flow
mechanisms. Also, PTA analysis during falloff period of waterflooding (prior to CO2 injection)
could provide additional information.

Temperature Monitoring

Bottomhole temperature data from the CO2 injection period are shown in Figure 175. The data
illustrates the impact of CO2 injection operations on formation temperature. The Beattie and
Jacoby wells show very little variation in temperature because the gauges are located within the
vertical section of those wellbores. As a result, any temperature impacts seen at bottomhole
must diffuse through the fluid column in the wellbore before they are seen by the gauges. There
are small increases in temperature observed at these wells coincident with the observed
pressure increases from injection. This is due to warmer fluid moving up the column in response
to the increased bottom-hole pressure.

Temperatures at Fischhaber decrease sharply coincident with cold liquid CO2 injection and
warm rapidly back to initial formation temperatures (low temperatures in mid-February 2025 are
from a wellbore gradient survey and are not representative of bottom-hole conditions).

Temperatures at the Marshell well show a significant response to CO2 injection. Temperatures
decrease coincident with observed pressure gain, indicating arrival of cold CO2, and remain low
for approximately one week before beginning to warm back toward geothermal temperatures.

These temperature responses likely indicate that most of the injected CO2 flowed to the
Marshell well and either stayed there or continued to transit elsewhere in the formation. There is
also an overall trend of warming in the temperature data that is a return to subsurface
temperature conditions after water injection.

 Figure 175. Bottomhole temperature data during CO2 injection and soak periods.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                            168

Wellbore Gradient Surveys

Downhole pressure and temperature gauges were pulled from the Fischhaber 2-35 on April 16,
2025. On the way out of the hole, five-minute-long stops were made every 100 ft to measure
pressure and temperature within the tubing, allowing the team to assess CO2 density and phase
behavior in the wellbore (Figure 176). The pressure curve appears to be a straight line, which
would suggest a constant density fluid column. However, upon further inspection, this was not
the case. Figure 177 shows the density of fluid calculated using NIST RefProp with measured
pressure and temperature as inputs. Also plotted is the slope of the best fit line to the pressure
data. This shows that the density is, in fact, variable throughout the column.

The temperature data shows variable temperature gradients from the top to the bottom of the
well. These inflection points correlate with changes in lithology (and more importantly, thermal
conductivity) within the stratigraphic column near the wellbore. The temperature anomaly from
4,000 to 4,500 ft is due to a measurement error in the gauge. The gauge had been pulled to the
surface prior to this gradient run, then dropped back to the bottom of the well. As a result, the
thermometer within the gauge had not yet equilibrated to downhole conditions at the start of
measurement.

Figure 178 shows this pressure and temperature data overlaid on a CO2 PVT diagram. The
colormap shows the fluid density at a given pressure and temperature, and the red line and dot
show the vapor pressure line and critical point, respectively. The white dots are the measured
data from the wellbore gradient run. As shown on the plot, the data is entirely within the vapor
phase section of the diagram (below the vapor pressure line), indicating that the entire column
of CO2 is in the vapor phase from the top to the bottom of the well.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                            169

Figure 176. Wellbore pressure and temperature gradients in the Fischhaber 2-35 on April 16th, 2025.

Figure 177. Fluid density as a function of depth within the Fischhaber 2-35 on April 16, 2025.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                   170

Figure 178. Pressure and temperature data from the Fischhaber 2-35 on April 16, 2025, overlaid on a CO2
PVT diagram.

On April 18, 2025, after the completion of CO2 Injection Stage 2, the downhole pressure and
temperature gauges were run back into the Fischhaber 2-35 well. On the way into the hole, five-
minute-long stops were made every 100 ft to measure pressure and temperature within the
tubing (opposite direction of the gradient run on April 16, 2025). The pressure and temperature
data from this gradient run are shown in Figure 179. Compared to the previous gradient run, this
version shows a much higher pressure at the bottom of the well. Temperatures at the wellhead
are substantially lower due to recent injection of cold liquid CO2. There is also a temperature
anomaly near the top of the well, which arises from slow thermal equilibration of the gauge
when moving from ambient temperature to the cold tubing.

The inflection point in both the temperature and pressure data around 3,500 ft appears to
correspond to a phase change (given the rapid change in fluid column weight). Since the
average pressure gradient below 3,500 ft corresponds to a fluid density of around 40 lb/ft3,
which is lower than that of fresh water (62.4 lb/ft3), this is most likely a transition from gaseous
CO2 to liquid CO2. Figure 180 shows the pressure and temperature data overlaid on the same
PVT diagram as Figure 178. All of the gauge data (excluding the outliers from the shallow
temperature anomaly) lies almost exactly on the vapor pressure line, pointing to a dynamic
system that is actively undergoing phase change. The interpretation of this data is that the
wellbore was filled with CO2, with a column of liquid CO2 at the bottom of the well that was
actively boiling off into gaseous CO2. In other words, the liquid level encountered was likely
dropping with time as more phase transition occurred, and the gauges crossed through this
dynamic liquid level on the way to the bottom of the well.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    171

Figure 179. Wellbore pressure and temperature gradients in the Fischhaber 2-35 on April 18, 2025.

Figure 180. Pressure and temperature data from the Fischhaber 2-35 on April 18, 2025, overlaid on a CO2
PVT diagram.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 172

7.0 Task 6: Production Monitoring
At project inception, Task 6 focused on improving technologies for monitoring flood fronts and
evaluating parameters affecting breakthrough times and CO2 sweep efficiency during and after
CO2 injection. In the final huff and puff iteration of the project, monitoring was intended to
capture placement of the injected CO2 plume within the reservoir, and efficiency of residual oil
mobilization by CO2 and surfactant. Density, temperature, pressure, and gas composition
measurements were used to inform models, understand fluid behavior within the reservoir, and
revise reservoir architecture concepts.

7.1 Subtask 6.1: Fluid Sampling
Fluid sampling during the production test was accomplished via multiple techniques including:
 1. In-line gas chromatography at the Marshell 1-35 facility.
 2. Water sampling at the separator to analyze density and salinity.
 3. Planned wellhead oil sampling (no oil was produced, so no samples were gathered)

The in-line gas chromatograph enabled the team to accurately monitor changes in CO2 and
natural gas production in real time. An example of the chromatograph output taken on
September 16, 2025 is shown in Figure 181. The CO2 fraction in the produced gas stayed
around 97-98% for the entirety of the production period.

Figure 181. Example gas chromatograph output during production (9/16/25).

Water at the separator was consistently weighed to track density and salinity of the produced
fluid. Water weight fluctuated between 8.33 (fresh) and 8.8 ppg (mix of injected fresh water and
production water) for the entirety of the test, averaging around 8.7 ppg (roughly 65,000 ppm
TDS). No clear correlation between water weight and production rate was observed. The fact
that water weight was changing indicates non-uniform mixing of the injected fluid within the
formation.

Additional sampling was conducted during swabbing efforts at the Beattie well after the
conclusion of the production test. Gas was sampled at the wellhead on November 22, 2025,
prior to any blowdown of the well. This gas underwent compositional analysis, identifying it as
an extremely wet gas (~25% methane, 70% C2-C5) with a specific gravity of 1.28. This is
substantially heavier than gas sampled during primary production in the field and could correlate
with a light condensate observed during wellbore gradient surveys conducted as part of this
project (see Section 6.3).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             173

Swabbing operations also returned a small volume of oil (refer to Section 6.2 for further details).
This oil was sampled and lab analysis consisting of compositional analysis, density
measurement and API gravity. The composition of the sample (by mole percent) is shown in
Figure 182. The API gravity of the sample was 36.9, making it the heaviest sample from the
Southern Michigan Trenton-Black River trend analyzed as part of this project.

Figure 182. Composition of Beattie oil sample.

By comparing the composition of this sample with the Hoffman oil that is analogous to in situ
fluid at the Lee 26 field at discovery, changes between live reservoir oil and the residual oil
sampled post-CO2 treatment can be ascertained. One significant difference is in the makeup of
the heavy hydrocarbons (C30+) between the samples. The average molecular weight of the
C30+ components in the Hoffman oil is 570 g/mol, while in the Beattie oil is heavier at 600
g/mol. Figure 183 shows the relative difference in mole percent for each carbon number
between the Hoffman and Beattie oils. A positive ratio indicates that a given component is more
present in the Beattie oil than the Hoffman oil, while a negative ratio indicates the opposite. The
light hydrocarbons are substantially depleted in the Beattie oil, which is not surprising – being a
residual oil from an ultra-depleted reservoir, almost all of the light hydrocarbons would have
evaporated and been produced as gas. The C4-C10 components are moderately depleted, with
the exception of C5. This could be indicative of further mobilization of light-medium
hydrocarbons during primary production, differences in the analog oil from Lee reservoir fluids,
or some combination of both.

Figure 183. Relative difference in mole percent by component between Beattie oil and Hoffman oil.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 174

From C12-C29, components are generally far more prevalent in the residual oil, with C24-C26
showing similar to slightly lower levels. Some level of increase is expected here given the fact
that the Beattie oil is depleted of lighter hydrocarbons. However, the consistent level of increase
observed across a wide range of molecular weight hydrocarbons appears to be indicative of
preferential mobilization. The fact that C30+ components are much less prevalent in the Beattie
oil further supports this theory (since CO2 floods tend to cause heavy ends to precipitate;
Hartono et al, 2024), as does the fact that CO2 tends to mobilize these medium-heavy
hydrocarbons left behind during primary production. This analysis supports the fact that CO2
operations in the field did, in fact, mobilize residual oil, though not within the anticipated regions
of the reservoir.

7.2 Subtask 6.2: Fluid Flow

Initial Production Tests

On May 28, 2025, the Fischhaber well was opened to flow with no artificial lift in the well. The
goal of this operation was to examine how long tubing pressures would stay elevated while
flowing and to ensure surface gas recycling infrastructure was operating properly. The well free-
flowed for one week, during which time the well produced CO2 that was reinjected in the
Marshell. The well was then shut-in again in preparation for swab testing. No liquid reached the
surface during these operations.

On June 10, 2025, swab testing commenced at the Fischhaber well. These operations ran from
June 10 – June 14, during which time a small volume of liquid was produced – mostly 8.4-8.5
ppg water with trace amounts of oil. The swab was run to 4,500 ft and was consistently bringing
back around 4 bbls of liquid per run. On the first run-in, operators reported a liquid level around
2,700 ft but returning no liquid to surface. After this, liquid levels were consistently found around
3,500 ft to 3,700 ft from surface. At the conclusion of swab testing, the bottomhole gauge was
retrieved from the Fischhaber well in preparation for pump installation.

Electric Submersible Pump Installation, Troubleshooting, and Replacement

At the start of the intended production test period, an ESP was installed in the Fischhaber 2-35
to lift reservoir fluids to surface. West Bay Exploration worked to design this pump with Summit
ESPs (a Halliburton company). The pump was designed for flowrates up to 600 bbl/day of
combined liquids, with a downhole gas separator to promote efficient pump operations (Figure
184). The pump was expected to consume 20 to 50 horsepower and powered by a diesel
generating set on the Fischhaber pad and was controlled by a variable speed drive (VSD) that
adjusted the pump operating speed to provide consistent flow and pressure to the Marshell
production facility.

West Bay installed the summit pump and drive equipment and found that the pump needed to
be operated at maximum frequency (pump speed) in order to bring liquid to surface, well outside
the intended operating window (Figure 185). This discrepancy may in part have been a result of
the reservoir pressure being approximately 400 psi lower than expected due to the extended
shut-in period. Summit attempted to troubleshoot the pump in position but were unable to
identify a solution. It was hypothesized that insufficient liquid was entering the pump due to its
location above the lowest perforations in the well. To improve liquid intake, the pump was
lowered below the perforations and Summit attempted to operate the pump again. The pump
ran successfully for about three days prior to repeatedly faulting out on either low or high
frequency. Summit again attempted to troubleshoot the issue, but was unable to re-start the

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                175

ESP. The Fischhaber was shut in on July 10, and the pump was removed from the well on July
17.

On removal, the ESP was inspected and found to have failed radial bearings, which had caused
the pump to mechanically lock and cease functioning. Summit initially concluded that sediment
ingestion triggered the failure, but full disassembly of the pump identified several assembly
defects that left the pump out-of-tolerance, destroying the bearings and subsequently the
impellers (Figure 186).

Figure 184. Design schematic for original Electric Submersible Pump.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                       176

Figure 185. ESP production data indicating inconsistent performance of the first ESP. Gas lock may have
been an issue, however, assembly error was identified as the primary failure mode.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  177

Figure 186. (A) Fractured radial bushing in one ESP stage and (B) impeller hub with excessive wear and
tip damage (lower).

The team leveraged the downtime caused by the pump failure to work with Summit to find a
pump design that was optimized for the real-time reservoir conditions. The pump was re-
designed to handle a wider range of fluid rates, as well as changes in fluid column density and
percentage of gas content. Impeller shape and stage count were selected to control fluid density
changes within the ESP due to condensation of CO2 as it moved upward through the pump.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 178

This replacement pump was intended to be placed below the perforations, so a shroud was
added to shield the intake ports from sediment ingestion. A detailed schematic is provided in
Figure 187.

Figure 187. Detailed schematic of optimized replacement ESP. Note shroud (gray cone) over intake ports.

The replacement ESP was delivered on August 26, 2025, installed on August 27, and started up
on August 28, 2025. The pump ran within design tolerances and produced fluids consistently
within the targeted production ranges for the entirety of the production test.

Production Test Results

Fluid flow rates were monitored by the field operators, project partner West Bay Exploration,
throughout the production testing cycle. Swabbing tests to assess flowback operations began at
the Fischhaber well on August 28, 2025 after installation of the second ESP and ceased on

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 179

October 30, 2025. All produced gas was reinjected at the Marshell well, and produced water
was diverted to an offsite disposal well. A total of ~42,000 bbls of water and ~3,300 mcf of gas
(98% CO2) were produced during the 62-day production test period. This amounted to ~170
tonnes of CO2, or 9% of the total injected mass. No oil was produced from the Fischhaber
during this period. The water and gas rates, along with cumulative rates, during the production
test period are shown in Figure 188.

Figure 188. Fluid rates (top) and cumulative volumes (bottom) observed during the production test period.

After the Fischhaber well was shut in and the ESP removed, the Jacoby and Beattie wells were
swabbed to investigate potential fluid production. The Jacoby did not produce any fluid after
several days of swabbing due to formation infiltration downhole. Swabbing at the Beattie well
produced 31 bbls of oil and five bbls of 8.8 ppg water (mix of production water and injected fresh
water) over three days of swabbing. Another two days of swabbing was conducted after the
release of packer elements in the well, during which time production fluid was all water and skim
oil. The oil produced during initial swabbing runs was sampled and sent for laboratory analysis.

7.3 Subtask 6.3: Pressure Analysis: Identifying the Placement of
 CO2 in the Reservoir
Pressure data from late May 2025 to the end of production testing in November 2025 shows the
impact of production operations on field pressures. Datum-corrected pressure data from the
production period is shown in Figure 189. The period from May 28 to June 15 shows decreased

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    180

pressures at the Fischhaber well during swabbing and flowing wellbore conditions. Pressures at
Marshell are elevated in response to the reinjection of gas produced from the Fischhaber well.
During initial flowback, bottomhole pressure dropped initially around 30 psi, continuing a much
slower drop for the remainder of that production period. Pressures fluctuated across a four to
five psi range, likely indicating a dynamic system at the bottom of the well. The team’s
interpretation of this data is that percolation of CO2 at the bottom of the well through a fluid
column within the wellbore is being observed. During the shut-in period, these fluctuations
gradually decrease in magnitude. The difference in producing BHP and shut-in BHP is around
10 psi.

During swabbing operations much larger fluctuations are observed due to the production and
subsequent backfilling of liquid volumes in the wellbore. The overall background trend of
pressure drop during swabbing and production phases is roughly consistent with one another.
During shut-in periods, the BHP drops much slower than during production phases, illustrating
the impact of fluid production on reservoir pressure. Over the course of the approximately two
weeks of production and swabbing, BHP at the Fischhaber well dropped by approximately 50
psi. Pressures from June 26-July 9, corresponding to the operation of the first ESP, are skewed
due to the failed pump, but do show a pressure decrease from pump operations. This first
production period ended with the failure of the installed ESP, at which point the field was shut-in
until a new ESP could be installed. Bottomhole pressures at the Marshell dropped at this point,
as injection ceased.

Figure 189. Datum-corrected bottom-hole pressure data from the Fischhaber (orange), Marshell (blue),
Jacoby (black), and Beattie (red) wells from May 28, 2025 through the end of available data.

Production was restarted with the new pump on August 28, 2025. At this point, pressure at the
Marshell increased (again due to gas reinjection), and pressures elsewhere in the field
decreased. The spike in pressures across the field on September 20-21, 2025 corresponds to a
brief downtime in the ESP. Pressures appeared to decrease much slower at the Jacoby well
than at the other three wells, indicating that the fracture that opened in early January 2025 was
likely closed, and the lower conductivity flow pathways between the Jacoby portion of the
reservoir and the rest of the reservoir were again dominant. In early October 2025, the Jacoby
pressures stabilized and remained relatively constant until the end of data collection. This is
likely when the gauges were no longer submerged due to fluid levels dropping in the well as
reservoir pressures decreased. Pressures at the other three wells continued to decrease until

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    181

the reservoir was shut-in on October 30, at which point buildup behavior in the Fischhaber
(consistent with end of production), falloff behavior in the Marshell (consistent with ceasing gas
re-injection), and stable, constant pressures at the Beattie well were observed.

WHP data during production testing is shown in Figure 190. Marshell wellhead pressures were
elevated during periods of injection but fell off as pressures declined in the field. During shut in
periods, WHP at the Marshell was constant, corresponding to the backpressure control at the
injection facility. Fischhaber WHP was extremely variable over the course of production. Jacoby
WHP gradually increased for the entire production test, increasing faster in early October 2025
before leveling off and staying constant through the remainder of available data. This increase
likely corresponds to continuous CO2 influx throughout the production period. The average
column density in the Jacoby well at the end of production correlates to vapor-phase CO2, which
further supports this theory. Finally, pressures at the Beattie well remained stable at zero psi
until the end of September 2025, at which point it increased by about five psi over the next
month and a half. These low pressures likely indicate a lack of gas in the Beattie well.

Figure 190. Wellhead pressure data at all four wells during production testing.

Temperature Monitoring

Bottom-hole fluid temperature data was also collected during this period from the bottomhole
gauges and ESP. These data are shown in Figure 191. Temperatures stayed mostly constant
throughout this period at the Beattie and Jacoby wells. Temperatures at Fischhaber were
slightly elevated as the water flowing past the motor of the ESP and was absorbing heat from
the pump. Temperatures at Marshell also increased slightly during periods of injection, likely
due to gas heating during compression at the surface.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              182

Figure 191. Downhole temperature data from the production testing period.

Wellbore Gradient Survey

On June 9, 2025 – prior to commencement of swabbing activities – a third wellbore gradient run
was conducted with downhole pressure/temperature gauges at the Fischhaber well. The
resulting pressure and temperature data are shown in Figure 192. As with prior gradient runs,
there appears to be a temperature anomaly in the shallow portion of the well. However, given
the high ambient temperatures at the time of measurement and the observed increase in
pressure over the same interval, these could be real data. The most striking feature of the data
is the inflection point in pressure around 2,700 ft, seemingly indicating a liquid level at that point.
Shallower than 2,700 ft, pressure is roughly constant around 20-30 psi. Deeper than this point,
there is a relatively linear pressure gradient to the bottom of the well. The slope of this line
(0.226 psi/ft) corresponds to a 4.3 ppg fluid. This is substantially lighter than any water that
would be seen in the reservoir and substantially heavier than a column of CO2 at these
pressure/temperature conditions. Hydrocarbon fluid at this density would correspond to a hot
condensate (liquid at depth, vapor at surface).

These data are consistent with what was observed in the first swabbing run. The fluid level
detected by the operators corresponds to the same depth seen in this data, and the resulting
production of zero fluid could correspond to a fluid that evaporated on the way up the wellbore.
Future swabbing runs that consistently found liquid around 3,500-3,700 ft indicate a column
density consistent with that of fresh to lightly saline water, which is what was produced during
those runs.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 183

Figure 192. Wellbore pressure and temperature gradients in the Fischhaber 2-35 on June 9, 2025.

7.4 Subtask 6.4: Additional Monitoring Analyses
In addition to the monitoring described above, the project team evaluated a number of additional
technologies to monitor and evaluate the surfactant-enhanced CO2 stimulation. Technologies
evaluated include:
 • CO2 and oil tracers: Intended to be injected along with CO2, these tracers have the
 potential to shed light on oil and CO2 mobility and placement within the reservoir.
 The project team worked with Baker-Hughes ProTechnics to understand applications
 within the scope of the study, but tracers were found to be too costly for this
 application.
 • One-spot tracer testing: Single-well chemical tracer testing (also known as one-
 spot tracer testing) is a method for in-situ evaluation of reservoir fluid saturation.
 During the test, a small amount of ester is injected into the reservoir, where it reacts
 with oil to generate alcohol. Because the ester reacts with the oil and is partitioned
 when the well is later produced back, the ester lags behind the alcohol (Figure 193),
 generating two distinct production profiles (Figure 194). The difference in time
 between the peaks of the two profiles is indicative of residual oil saturation. This test
 would have been conducted twice: once to determine native residual oil saturation,
 and a second time after a small injection of surfactant-enhanced CO2. The second
 test would reveal how much oil had been mobilized by the CO2, allowing the project
 team to very accurately determine the oil production uplift generated by this
 stimulation strategy. However, due to high cost, operational complexity related to
 reservoir pressure, and scheduling conflicts with test providers, the project team
 chose not to proceed with this method of surveillance.
 • Time-lapse seismic monitoring: The project team worked with Dr. Shuvajit
 Bhattacharya at UT BEG to scope a time-lapse seismic monitoring program for the
 Lee field. The team evaluated an integrated workflow developed by Bhattacharya et
 al. (2024) to conduct a pre-feasibility assessment of the potential for 4D seismic

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 184

 detection of CO2 injected into the Lee 26 field in Calhoun County, Michigan, using
 existing 3D seismic, well logs, and a CMG dynamic flow simulation model of the field
 to generate a predicted seismic amplitude anomaly generated by the injected CO2
 plume. After injection, the team would shoot serial 2D seismic lines across the field,
 process them, and complete attribute analysis to identify the plume in the new
 seismic lines. However, based on dynamic modeling results, the project team
 determined that the induced anomaly generated by the relatively small 2,000 ton
 injection would likely be undetectable given the reservoir depth and thickness.

Figure 193. Conceptual diagram showing the reactions occurring during a single-well partitioning tracer
test; diagram is from tracer test provider Tracerco.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       185

Figure 194. Example of production curves used to determine residual oil saturation by single well tracer
test, diagram is from tracer test provider Tracerco.

7.5 Subtask 6.5: Integration of Monitoring Technologies
The integration of selected monitoring technologies (surface and bottom-hole pressure and
temperature gauges; water tracers; SPN logging; fluid sampling & analysis) is demonstrated in
Subtask 6.3 and in Task 7. This section of the report deals with the effectiveness of the selected
monitoring technologies and recommendations for monitoring of future CO2-EOR efforts in the
TBR trend.

 • Water Tracers (and water salinity): During the repressurization phase of the project,
 water tracers were found to be relatively effective at characterizing movement across the
 reservoir. By pairing tracer response and water salinity measurement, the team was able
 to identify slugs of relatively fresh, tracer-laden water arriving at wells in the field. Adding
 this context to bottom-hole pressure measurements allowed for the identification of
 distinct events in the reservoir which is believed to be related to fracture opening/closing
 or the initiation of a spill point within the vug systems in the reservoir. This gave the team
 a view into the extreme reservoir heterogeneity in the Lee 26 field and provides context
 for future TBR CO2-EOR. One area of improvement is that the tracers were injected into
 the field before water samples from the wells were accessible. This caused the team to
 fail at identifying the first arrival of tracers at each well, which would have been very
 useful data. In future projects, operators should wait until they are certain they will be
 able to acquire water samples before implementing their tracer study and should plan to
 sample for tracers at a high frequency (every day if possible) during the first few weeks
 after tracer injection.
 • Spectral Pulsed Neutron Logging (SPN): SPN was not effective in this application. A
 combination of poor cement condition (in the Marshell 1-35), lack of sufficient porosity
 and pay zone thickness (at the Fischhaber 2-35), and changes in wellbore configuration
 between the baseline and follow-up logging runs at the Fischhaber 2-35 resulted in the
 project acquiring no useful data from these logging operations. The project does not
 recommend

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                        186

SPN for reservoir saturation evaluation in depleted TBR fields. Instead, developers should
utilize reservoir engineering techniques such as history matching and material balance analysis
to determine which areas of their fields have sufficient residual oil saturations to be appropriate
for CO2-EOR. Single-well partitioning tracer studies may also help answer this question, but it is
possible that the high reservoir heterogeneity in the TBR could cause this technique to fail.
 • Downhole Pressure Gauges: This is the key technology for tracking reservoir behavior.
 Downhole pressure gauges are by far the most cost-effective method for directly
 monitoring reservoir fluid movement and tracking inter-well connectivity. The downhole
 pressure data gathered during the project provided the basis for the field history match
 during repressurization and production, meaningfully influencing the assessment of
 porosity distribution, fracture and vug connectivity, and heterogeneity. The downhole
 gauges also provided temperature sensing, which proved useful during CO2 injection as
 first arrival of the cold CO2 was easily observable.
 • Because the gauges were on hand (reducing cost) and were able to be rapidly deployed,
 the decision was made to use gauges with built-in memory rather than gauges that were
 connected to the surface and could be monitored in real time. In retrospect, this resulted
 in higher operating costs than expected since payment was needed for slickline removal
 of the gauges on a regular basis to collect stored data. Wired gauges will likely be
 selected in the future.
 • Surface Pressure Gauges: Surface tubing and annular pressure gauges are a
 requirement for every operating wellbore, therefore are strongly recommend for future
 projects. These gauges also proved useful for approximate fluid column density tracking,
 giving the project insight into wellbore contents. They also provided a real-time,
 continuous proxy for downhole pressure data, allowing for a method to observe some
 pressure differences during repressurization, CO2 injection, and production.
 • Gas Chromatography: In-line gas chromatography was built into the production facility
 to track changes in gas composition during production. This data would have been crucial
 for identifying changes in mobilized constituents between initial flowback and later
 sustained production, providing insight into the utility of both CO2 and CETAC-30
 surfactant, as well as providing valuable CO2 concentration data for facility operation and
 optimization. Because no hydrocarbons were produced (outside of trace methane), the
 gas chromatograph was not useful. However, it is still recommended to include gas
 chromatography in facilities that CO2-EOR operations flow through.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             187

8.0 Task 7: Performance Assessment
At project inception, Task 7 was designed with a focus on ongoing performance and
assessment of the efficiency of the CO2-EOR injection. As initially planned, the period from the
end of injection to project closeout was on the order of one year. Given the shortened timeline
as the project played out, as well as the lack of production and subsequent field shutin after CO2
treatment, assessment of the sustained performance of the reservoir is no longer relevant. As a
result, discussion surrounding the performance assessment (Subtask 7.1) focuses on the
team’s overall evaluation of the efficacy of CO2 treatment in the Lee reservoir and explanations
for production trends (or lack thereof) observed. Subtask 7.2 focuses on integration of field data
to the existing reservoir model in the form of a sensitivity assessment, as well as simplified
modeling to assess scale-up across the Southern Michigan trend and potential recovery in the
TBR.

8.1 Subtask 7.1: Performance Assessment

Despite challenges related to timeline, winter weather, wireline logging, and artificial lift
malfunctions, field testing at the Lee 26 field was an operational success. Approximately 2,000
tons of CO2 were injected into the reservoir, and then the reservoir was production tested for
two months with no injuries, no unintended release of hydrocarbons of CO2, and no harm to the
environment. During these operations, the team was able to gather valuable data about
reservoir performance that inform the economic potential of future chemically enabled CO2-EOR
tests within the TBR trend.

That being said, oil production (and therefore economic productivity) resulting from chemically
enhanced CO2 treatment did not reach the intended target. Over the 63 days of production
testing, several basic trends were revealed:

 1. Bottom hole pressure declined predictably. Some portion of the pressure decline is
 likely related to continued bleed-off of pressure into the matrix, but the majority of
 pressure decline appears to be caused by production.
 2. Water rate varied predictably based on pump frequency. This indicates that the
 replacement ESP functioned as intended, and that permeability is sufficiently high
 that pump rate is the limiting factor on deliverability, not reservoir conditions.
 3. Produced CO2 to natural gas ratio was stable, at about 98% CO2 and 2% natural gas.
 4. Water to total gas ratio remained stable. Minor increases in pump rate and water
 production are accompanied by similar increases in gas production. Since the
 perforations in the Fischhaber 2-35 are in the water leg of the reservoir, this provides
 at least some evidence that there is not a purely stratified fluid column in the
 reservoir-there is at least some gas that exists within the water column.
 5. Water weight varied between 8.3 and 8.8 pounds per gallon, with no obvious trend.
 Water used for repressurization was approximately 8.4 pounds per gallon, while in-
 situ reservoir water is approximately 9.7 pounds per gallon, so the produced water
 indicates heterogeneous mixing between injected and in-situ waters.

Forecasting simulations predicted a much larger return of residual fluid (>1,000 bbls of oil over
the production period). There are a number of possible causes for the lack of oil production
observed at the Fischhaber 2-35. These include:

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 188

 1. Carbon Dioxide and surfactant did not effectively penetrate into the matrix, where
 modeling indicates most residual oil should be.
 a. It is possible that the capillary entry pressure needed to move useful volumes of
 gas-phase CO2 into the matrix was not achieved. The apparent movement of
 injected water into the matrix does not conflict with this notion: if water exists as
 a continuous phase in the matrix, it does not need to overcome capillary entry
 pressure, whereas CO2 would have to disrupt the water phase in order to gain
 entry to the matrix.
 b. If this is the case, it is possible that gas-phase CO2 could have been effectively
 injected into the matrix if higher pressure had been achieved. However, it is
 likely that liquid or supercritical CO2 pressure would be necessary to generate
 sufficient entry force.
 2. Carbon Dioxide and surfactant did penetrate into the matrix during high-pressure
 injection but because the vugs and fractures returned to a water-saturated state during
 the soak period, a relative permeability block exists, keeping CO2 and oil from flowing
 out of the matrix.
 a. It is possible that if the reservoir were to continue to be drawn down, matrix
 production might turn on, increasing CO2 production and initiating oil production.
 3. Oil was successfully mobilized and the protracted shut-in period between injection and
 production has caused oil to buoyantly move upward. With perforations currently
 positioned in the water column, access to oil is precluded. Continued water withdrawal
 will cause water level to drop, ultimately providing oil production.
 a. A related scenario is that oil has moved buoyantly up-dip, away from the
 Fischhaber and towards the Jacoby and Beattie. Continued water withdrawal at
 the Fischhaber may draw oil back toward the well, providing production.
 4. Carbon Dioxide injection swept CO2 and oil away from the Fischhaber and towards other
parts of the reservoir, reducing the effectiveness of the ‘puff’ production phase.
 a. Related to this, the injection of water may have swept oil away from the
 Fischhaber well. Pressure gradients from bottom-hole gauge data suggest that
 fluids were flowing away from the Fischhaber well towards the Beattie and
 Jacoby wells for the majority of water injection.
 5. Local pore volume around the Fischhaber well is smaller than initially predicted.
 Revisiting of log-interpreted dolomite presence, seismic interpretations, and recently
 acquired pressure/temperature data suggest that the majority of reservoir pore volume,
 at least in the eastern portion of the field, exists around the Marshell well. A smaller
 local pore volume would necessitate a smaller volume of residual oil around the
 Fischhaber well.
 a. If this hypothesis is true, then the large volume of injected CO2 would have
 pushed any residual oil away from the Fischhaber well (see scenario 4).
 6. Primary production was so efficient that there is minimal residual oil.
 a. The technical team does not believe that this scenario is likely; it implies that all
 primary production was from fractures and vuggy porosity, which is inconsistent
 with the team’s understanding of production in the remainder of the trend.

Based on interpretations of field data from the entire lifetime of the field, the most likely

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 189

explanation for the disparity between model predictions and operational data is a combination of
hypotheses four and five above. The intent of the surfactant in huff and puff operations, in
addition to providing wettability changes within the reservoir to enhance oil mobilization, was to
provide mobility control of the injected CO2 to allow it to act on residual fluids surrounding the
Fischhaber well. If local pore volume was limited in this area of the reservoir (as the team
believes it was), the large injected volume of CO2 would have flown away from the well,
regardless of any mobility control, simply due to a lack of available void space. The temperature
signals at the Marshell well, coincident with CO2 injection, support the idea that CO2, and
therefore any mobilized oil, was swept away from the Fischhaber well. Fluid column stratification
that would have happened in the high conductivity vug/fracture network during the soak phase
of the huff and puff may then have caused fluid reorganization in such a way to prevent oil
migration back toward the Fischhaber well during the “puff” phase.

Based on laboratory analysis of the oil recovered from the Lee field during swabbing operations
(see Section 6.1), it does appear that oil was a residual oil within the reservoir. Since sampling
was conducted at the Beattie well throughout the entirety of water injection and no oil was
detected in any of the samples, it appears that this residual oil was, in fact, mobilized by the
chemical CO2 treatment. In essence – the chemical CO2 mixture succeeded in mobilizing
residual oil, but the injection and production plan proved unsuccessful in predictably controlling
the migration and recovery of this mobilized fluid. The solution to this problem is not clear – huff
and puff operations appear to be ineffective, but the high conductivity pathways in TBR systems
make effective flooding a challenge. It is possible that, if a concentrated zone of high pore
volume could be identified, a huff and puff recovery strategy could be more effective. Even then,
injected fluid will be prone to migrate away from the near-wellbore area through high
conductivity paths. Substantial mobility control will be required to successfully implement any
huff and puff or flooding tertiary EOR scheme.

Another possibility involves a field implementation design that is agnostic to reservoir geometry.
This may include something like a “full field huff and puff” where the entire field is repressurized
with CO2, allowed to soak, and then turned to production. The practicality of such a scenario is
unclear, especially given the delay in realized revenues from oil production. However, given that
operations and data collected in this project support that mobilization of the residual fluid is
possible, and that a substantial amount of residual resource exists in these fields, additional
exploration and optimization of recovery strategies is worthwhile.

8.2 Subtask 7.2: Modeling Update and Scale-Up
As part of this task, additional dynamic simulations were conducted that integrated pressure and
fluid flow data obtained during field testing operations. Ideally, this exercise would have involved
a full integration of the gathered data, including detailed pressure transient and inter-well flow
analysis, updates to the SEM, and a new history match in the dynamic model. However, a major
takeaway of the CO2 huff and puff operations was the large impact that small scale
heterogeneities present in the reservoir have on fluid flow patterns. The level of complexity
present in the reservoir severely inhibits effective correlations between wells, even with the
relatively small well spacing present in the Lee field. As a result, the geostatistical property
modeling techniques used in SEM construction and property manipulations used in history
matching efforts struggle to effectively converge on a representative model.

The disagreement between forecasting simulations and field observations – particularly in oil
production rates – motivated the team to focus model update activities on gaining a better
understanding of what parameters most contribute to the migration of fluids within these types of

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              190

reservoirs. Rather than seeking a single history match to this extremely complex field, which
may have little application to other fields in the trend, the goal was to delineate key subsurface
factors which could lend some level of insight to operations in other HTD fields. A sensitivity
assessment was conducted that examined large variability in key subsurface parameters and
the resulting impact on performance metrics in the field – specifically pressure response and
production volumes. With this approach, the utility of the existing reservoir model could be
maximized despite a poor match to field test data.

To facilitate rapid modeling of a large set of scenarios, the existing model was simplified into
various sectors corresponding to sections of the reservoir associated with a specific well
penetration. The divisions in the reservoir are depicted in Figure 195, corresponding to the
oil/water legs near each well (Beattie, Jacoby, Fischhaber, Marshell) and the gas caps located
above the Jacoby and Fischhaber wells. Within each block, homogeneous porosity and
permeability distributions were assigned based on average values from the history-matched

reservoir model. Fluid properties and contacts, fluid models, and relative permeability models
were left unchanged. Matrix permeability was set to a single value (0.6 mD) across the entire
model due to the low variability in this parameter in the history matched model.

Figure 195. Reservoir zones of the simplified reservoir model used in sensitivity analysis.

A list of key subsurface variables were identified for analysis based on their impacts in initial
history matching, interpretations from field pressure and production data, and the level of
uncertainty in existing data. The list of variables and their corresponding values are shown in
Table 27. These variables primarily cover the distribution of pore volumes across the field and
conductivity of the connected fractures and vugs across the field. The base variable values
correspond to the average values for each zone in Figure 195, while the low and high values
were chosen to assess the full range of variability expected within the parameters. Relative pore
volumes were modified by changing pore volume modifiers across specific sections, rather than
by changing porosity. The porosity of matrix dolomite is fairly well constrained from the geologic
assessments, but the amount of dolomitization in each part of the reservoir (particularly between
wells) is an unknown of interest. As such, matrix porosity was set to a constant value of 7.5%
across the entire model. Similarly, fracture porosity was set to a constant 1.6%, corresponding
to the 21% storativity ratio used in the history matched model. Total pore volume was held
constant in the relative pore volume scenarios. In addition, the Beattie and Jacoby reservoir
zones were combined for certain sensitivities due to the level of uncertainty in this section of the

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              191

reservoir (specifically around the Jacoby completions).

A total of 23 scenarios were run to assess the sensitivity of these variables – a base case run
with the average property values, and 22 scenarios where a single variable was changed to
either its high or low value. Contrary to the history-matched model, produced CO2 was
reinjected into the Marshell to better reflect field setup. The scenario matrix for the sensitivity
analysis is shown in Table 28. In addition, a single scenario (Scenario 0.1 in Table 28) was run
with average property values where the fracture presumed to exist between the Jacoby and
Fischhaber wells was dynamically opened and closed at a corresponding fracture closure
pressure (FCP). This was done by applying transmissivity multipliers of zero across the gas and
oil legs between these sections, while maintaining connectivity in the water leg.

The results of each scenario were then compared to field datasets for 22 different metrics of field
history and performance. The metrics were as follows:

 • Field cumulative production of oil and gas (2)
 • Field average pressure during primary production (1)
 • Oil and gas cumulative production at each well – including initial Jacoby
 completions – during primary production (12)
 • Bottomhole pressure response at each well for duration of injection operations (4)
 • Oil, water and gas production at Fischhaber post-treatment (3)
As a proxy for goodness of fit, the root mean square error (RMSE) was calculated for each
scenario across each of these metrics using the equation

 ∑𝑁𝑁 (𝑦𝑦𝑖𝑖 − 𝑦𝑦𝑦𝑖𝑖)2
 √  𝑖𝑖=1
 𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅𝑅 =
 𝑁𝑁
Where 𝑦𝑦𝑦𝑖𝑖 are the measured data points, 𝑦𝑦𝑖𝑖 are the synchronized model outputs corresponding to

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              193

measured data, and N is the number of data points for a given metric. In the case of bottomhole
pressure data, where minute resolution data was available, data were upscaled to daily
resolution. RMSE values for each metric and each scenario are given in Table 29. The
RMSE values for the history matched model (HM) are also included for comparison. Note that
the “Fischhaber Gas Post-Inj” metric is significantly smaller in the HM case than in most of the
other cases because of the lack of gas reinjection.
In addition, RMSE values for the sensitivity scenarios were compared to the base case RMSE
for each metric to assess whether a given variable changed improved or worsened the fit to field
data and by what magnitude. The values in the table are color coded according to the following:

 • White – RMSE changed by <1%
 • Light Red/Green – RMSE increased/decreased by 1-5%
 • Medium Red/Green – RMSE increased/decreased by 5-20%
 • Dark Red/Green – RMSE increased/decreased by >20%

Several of the metrics show little to no variation across scenarios (e.g., gas at vertical Jacoby
completion during primary production). These metrics typically represent primary well
constraints for a given well within the model, and as such the production values are identical
across scenarios as long as the deliverability of the reservoir near that well is sufficiently high.

While the raw RMSE values allow for effective comparison across scenarios within a given
metric, they do not allow for comparison across metrics because of the large difference in raw
values (e.g. comparing gas production, which is in billions of scf, to pressures in tens to
hundreds of psi). To facilitate such a comparison, the relative change in RMSE for each metric
(relative to base case RMSE, as a decimal) is presented in Table 30. The degree of change
observed in each metric for a given variable can help inform what aggregate changes would
drive better model performance.

Visual inspection of the modeling results, relative to field data, can provide additional context
around the RMSE values and improvements (or lack thereof) in goodness of fit across metrics.
Figures 196 through 199 show visual comparison of model results co-plotted with field data for
all 22 metrics for the base scenario (top panels) and the aggregated results of all sensitivity
scenarios (bottom panel). The dashed lines in the bottom panels show the best fit scenario to a
given metric (not necessarily the same scenario across metrics), while the shaded region shows
the range of all sensitivity results. For all metrics (except those used as primary constraints), at
least one case exists where RMSE has decreased. However, there is not a single scenario that
improves fits across the board. This is illustrative of the complexity of the system, indicating that
a combination of these factors is responsible for the pressure and fluid migration observed in
the Lee field.
