A large amount of information can be gleaned from the RMSE and relative RMSE tables. A
discussion of a few key observations is provided here to illustrate the utility of the information
contained. Figure 200 displays the relative change in RMSE for all variables across a selection
of the key metrics – primary gas production, pressure at three of the wells during injection
operations, and hydrocarbon production post-CO2 treatment – with the white bar showing
change in the “high” case for that variable, and orange showing the same for the “low”. The
plots are truncated at both a 100% increase and decrease in RMSE, though some of the RMSE
changes are greater than this. The charts convey the largest contributing variables to each of
these metrics, both in raw change and in terms of improving RMSE.

A large improvement in RMSE across all of these metrics is observed when permeability of the
throughgoing fracture/vug train is increased (the “high” case of full-field path variable). This
pathway is clearly an important piece of the puzzle in determining subsurface behavior in this
reservoir, but it does not tell the whole story. The presence of this high conductivity channel
substantially decreases gas production (thereby increasing RMSE of primary gas production),
which causes the depleted state of the reservoir to be far too high. The degree of observed
improvements does seem to indicate a high conductivity pathway connects the entire feature to
some extent. The presence – or lack thereof – of this throughgoing pathway is responsible for
some of the largest raw changes to RMSE observed across metrics, both positive and negative.
Clearly, identification and understanding of any such feature in other TBR fields will be crucial in
controlling fluid migration for any future EOR operations.

Figure 200. Tornado chart showing sensitivity of Fischhaber oil production post-CO2 treatment to various
variables.

Another key driver appears to be the size of the gas cap within the field. As with the
throughgoing fracture, changes to the amount of free gas present at field discovery cause

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                   198

substantial changes across all metrics. For production of gas and CO2 at the Fischhaber well
post-treatment, these changes are pronounced. The “high” case for this variable, which
increases the relative pore volume of the gas cap, significantly improves RMSE for this metric
by decreasing the volume of gas/CO2 produced. This makes sense, as the presence of a large
amount of pore volume shallower than the location CO2 was injected provides more space for
the CO2 to migrate into during both huff and puff operations and gas recycling. Any free gas in
the system would then be further from the production well when it is turned on, thereby
decreasing the volumes observed at the well. A decrease in free gas fraction has the opposite
impact, and the change in RMSE resulting from this is even more pronounced (>100% increase
in RMSE). Accurate representation of the gas cap in these saturated reservoirs is clearly
important, indicating the gas leg of any TBR reservoirs should be adequately characterized to
ensure success of CO2-EOR operations.

One major observation was noted in this modeling process that is not captured simply by
looking at the RMSE tables. The inclusion of dynamic fracture behavior in the system appears
to explain some of the behavior observed post-treatment at the Lee field. Having the fracture
open in early January, corresponding to a closure pressure of ~800 psi, does cause a marked
increase in pressure at the Jacoby well. Figure 201 shows the model data co-plotted with field
observations for downhole pressures while gauges were installed at this well. The qualitative
similarity in the data in January 2025 – specifically the noted increase – provides strong
evidence for the substantial increase in pressures being due to opening of a fracture. The fit
itself is poor, indicating that the permeability of this fracture is likely too low. Some combination
of changes in the other independent variables is necessary to truly improve the fit. This serves
to indicate that these dynamic flow pathways are critical to understanding subsurface flow in
these systems. Should any future CO2-EOR operations be conducted at other TBR fields,
pressures should be closely monitored during initial injection operations to identify the presence
of any dynamics in the system.

Figure 201. Jacoby downhole gauge data (points) and model data from Scenario 0.1 with a dynamic
opening and closing fracture. Note the pressure increase in early January in both datasets.

This approach has its limitations. Sensitivities to some key variables in dual porosity systems,
namely storativity ratio and interporosity flow coefficient, are not inherently assessed. The ratio
of fracture to matrix porosity and permeability does implicitly depend on these variables, so
there is some degree of analysis, though it is not explicit. Parameters like fracture density and

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                199

fracture aperture size are not assessed. However, given the assumptions on geometry for the
system (single fracture plane in each lateral dimension), any changes in fracture density or
aperture would be reflected as a change to the ratio of matrix to fracture porosity in a given grid
block. Sensitivity to relative permeability models is also not assessed due to lack of data. Core
flooding experiments on field-specific cores would provide useful information on the relative
permeability behaviors of these systems.

Capillary entry pressure to the matrix system was not assessed in any of the modeling efforts
and could potentially play an important role in determining to what extent CO2 penetrates the
matrix system. This was also not assessed due to lack of data. It is also possible that non-Darcy
flow impacts play a key role in fluid flow within these systems. Given that some of the cavernous
vugs observed in image logs within certain TBR reservoirs are on the order of mm-cm in
diameter, there is opportunity for laminar or turbulent flows to occur within the reservoir. Both in
initial modeling and sensitivity assessments, these limitations were acknowledged and
maintained in an effort to avoid overcomplicating a model that was built on sparse data.

In addition, regarding the sensitivity results specifically, there is no guarantee when a
combination of factors are changed within the model, that the change in RMSE will follow the
same trends observed in each variable individually. However, these results provide some insight
into what may be missing from the existing reservoir model, what controls the effectiveness of
CO2 treatments in these HTD reservoirs, and what geologic characterization will be most
important to screen candidate reservoirs.

Scale Up to other TBR Reservoirs

Given the lack of oil production observed at the Lee 26 field after CO2 treatment, alternative
modeling approaches were pursued to estimate the feasibility of CO2-EOR in other TBR
reservoirs. A series of simplified reservoir performance models were developed for the two
largest fields in the TBR trend – Napoleon and Albion-Scipio – resolving volumetric parameters
such as the potential incremental oil production (stock tank barrels [STB], one thousand STB
[MSTB], or million STB [MMSTB]), associated water production (STB, MSTB, or MMSTB), and
CO2 production and recycling (tonnes or millions tonnes [MMt]). A substantial amount of residual
oil remains in these fields, and the field testing has shown that mobilization of the residual oil in
this trend is possible. All that is needed is an appropriate field optimization scheme to tap into
these resources. Due to the remaining uncertainty in how effectively such a field program might
perform, a range of production scenarios were considered to explore the variety of possible
outcomes for trend-scale implementation. The primary variables of interest across the various
production scenarios are defined as follows:
 1. Recovery Factor (RF) during primary production
 o Percentage of OOIP produced to date
 2. Incremental RF due to CO2-EOR
 o Percentage of OOIP produced during hypothetical CO2-EOR operations
 3. Time to peak oil production
 o Time from start of CO2 injection to peak oil production rate
 4. CO2 utilization ratio
 o Barrels of oil produced per tonne of CO2 injected
The high and low values for each parameter are shown in Table 31. Primary constraints used for
scenario generation are denoted in the table. The range of values for the primary recovery factor

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               200

was constrained using existing literature (Verma, 2017; Hurley and Budros, 1990) and
material balance modeling conducted as part of this project for the Lee 26 and Napoleon fields.
Incremental recovery factors were based on literature (Verma, 2017) and core flooding
experiments conducted in Task 3. Time to peak production values were based on trends
observed in literature (Verma, 2017) and collaboration with domain experts. Finally, CO2
utilization values were constrained by literature (Verma, 2017; Miller et al., 1994; Thomas, 1989;
Haskin and Alston, 1989; Yang et al., 2010; Mohammed-Singh et al., 2006). For all parameters,
the results of field testing at the Lee 26 field were weighed against compiled values to determine
final ranges of values. Production scenarios were generated using a decline curve forecasting
method loosely following the analysis done in Verma (2017), Chapter C for existing CO2-EOR
floods. The scenario generation process is outlined step-by-step below.

Table 31. Parameters used for production scenario generation

 Parameter Low Value High Value Primary
 constraint?
 Primary RF 15% 40% X
 Incremental RF 5% 15% X
 Time to Peak 1 yr 5 yrs X
 CO2 Utilization 2 stb/ton 12 stb/ton

 1. Fit decline curves to monthly production data

For each field, a decline curve was fit to monthly oil production data pulled from the Michigan
EGLE Oil and Gas database (MI EGLE, 2026) using least-squares regression. Two parallel
decline curve analysis methods are used to describe recent production trends – one fitting oil
production rate versus time (assuming exponential decline), and another linear fit to production
rate versus cumulative production. For clarity, these are referred to as the “time decline” curve
and the “q versus Q” curve, respectively. The fitted curves for the Albion-Scipio production data
are shown in Figure 202 (note that only data since 1982 is used for the fits). The time decline
method is useful for estimating how long a particular field will be economically viable, but cannot
provide an ultimate production number due to the asymptotic behavior of the exponential fit. The
time decline curve for each field gives a baseline production case to which the forecasting
scenarios can be compared. Conversely, the q versus Q curve provides an estimate of ultimate
recovery but cannot describe the time-dependent behavior of the production rate. Ultimate
cumulative recovery is calculated for each field using the q versus Q curves (the x-intercept of
the fit) and used as an input to the next calculation step.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             201

Figure 202. Fitted time decline (left) and q vs. Q (right) curves for Albion-Scipio field using EGLE data.

 2. Calculate OOIP and incremental oil production

Using the ultimate cumulative recovery volumes from step 1, the OOIP for each field is
calculated by dividing ultimate recovery by the assumed primary RF. Incremental recovery is
then calculated by multiplying the OOIP value by assumed incremental RF. The incremental
recovery volume is used to constrain the CO2-EOR decline curves.
 3. Model forecasted decline curves for CO2-EOR

The sum of the incremental and ultimate primary recovery gives a zero-rate point on the q
versus Q graph, assumed to be the final cumulative volume after EOR operations. The slope of
the CO2-EOR decline curve is estimated from field data reported in Verma (2017), Chapter C by
examining the slope ratio of the CO2-EOR curve to the primary/secondary decline curve.

Average values for this ratio range 0.53-0.65; to keep estimates conservative, the lower slope
ratio is chosen. The CO2-EOR q versus Q decline curve is then generated by multiplying the
slope of the primary production q versus Q curve by the slope ratio (0.53), and using the zero
rate, final cumulative volume point to constrain the line.

Using sampled points on this q versus Q decline curve, time series data (production rate versus
time) can be calculated by dividing change in cumulative volume between two points by the
higher of the two rates – essentially a left Riemann approximation. An exponential decline is
then fitted to the calculated time series data, resulting in a bounding decline curve for a
particular pair of primary RF and incremental RF. In order to vary the time to peak production
across scenarios, a straight line is drawn (in rate versus time space) from the last date of
production data to the bounding curve. This straight line represents a period of defined duration
(1-5 years) where production rate increases to a peak rate, after which exponential decline
begins. Figure 203 shows a subset of the scenarios generated via this method for the Albion-
Scipio field. The red line shows the primary production decline curve. Black lines show the
bounding exponential models for two separate primary recovery factors (15%, 40%), and the
dashed lines show the six scenarios (three for each bounding curve) generated through
variation in time to peak production.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                          202

 Figure 203. Subset of production rate scenarios (dashed lines) generated for the Albion-Scipio field.

 4. Calculate CO2 demand

Rather than using an assumed CO2 utilization ratio to calculate demand, the necessary mass of
CO2 is derived from assuming a voidage replacement flood strategy (volume in = volume out) in
the hydrocarbon column. The mass of injected CO2 is based on the peak production rate
observed for each scenario and held constant throughout the entire project lifetime. Miscible
flooding is assumed; hence the reservoir volume associated with peak production (assumed oil
formation volume factor of 1.0 for simplicity) is converted to CO2 mass using a density value
associated with the minimum miscibility pressure of Napoleon oil measured in Task 3. A
subsurface temperature of 100oF is assumed, consistent with measured bottom hole
temperatures at both the Lee field and the Napoleon field. Prior to peak production, while rates
are ramping up, the volume of injected CO2 exceeds production volumes to approximate
pressurization of the field. After peak production is reached and production rates begin to
decrease, the makeup volume of the production stream is assumed to be made up of CO2. This
results in an increasing volume of CO2 produced as the project continues. The time-series of oil
production rate, CO2 injection rate and production rate for a specific scenario at the Albion-
Scipio field is shown in Figure 204.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         203

Figure 204. Oil produced, CO2 injected and CO2 produced for a single scenario at the Albion-Scipio field.

An implicit assumption of subsurface dynamics consistent with a CO2 flood constrains this
approach. Initial injection of CO2 generates pressure in the field, pushing some residual oil
toward the production well(s). As CO2 spreads further into the reservoir, multi-contact miscibility
mobilizes, increasing volumes of residual oil which are pushed toward the production well.

Immediately following peak production, the CO2 front arrives at the production well, after which
the production well stream is a mix of oil and CO2. Despite the pressure maintenance of a
voidage replacement flood strategy, CO2 is only able to mobilize a finite volume of oil. The
production decline period is a result of decreasing CO2 utilization ratio over time as this finite
volume is extracted. The overall CO2 utilization for these scenarios fell between 2.5 and 5.3
stb/tonne which is consistent with the range of utilization ratios observed in literature.

Some additional assumptions concerning fluid volumes are used in this economic model. All of
the produced CO2 is assumed to be recycled back into the injection stream. Water production is
assumed to occur at a 2:1 ratio with produced oil. This is somewhat lower than observed water
cut in other CO2-EOR operations; however, most of these projects are preceded by a water
flood, which is not the case here. In addition, these Trenton-Black River fields have minimal
water drive (Hurley and Budros, 1990), meaning the volume of water in the system is relatively
finite. This 2:1 water-oil ratio is consistent with the Napoleon and Albion-Scipio fields based on
EGLE production data (MI EGLE, 2026). Produced water is assumed to be sent offsite for
disposal.

A total of 24 modeling realizations were generated – 12 for each field – by varying the primary
constraints of Table 30 (a time to peak production of 3 years was also considered). A subset of
these results were used to constrain the economic analysis of trend-wide deployment.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                     204

9.0 Task 8: Development Plan
The work completed over the course of this study has yielded significant new information about
the behavior of the Trenton-Black River hydrothermal dolomite play, including new insights into
reservoir architecture, seismic evaluation of reservoir presence and quality, reservoir response
to stimulation with surfactant-enhanced CO2, and operational lessons learned before, during,
and after CO2 injection. These learnings are synthesized below into a development plan that
can be used by operators to optimize CO2-EOR operations in the TBR and other hydrothermal
dolomite plays.

9.1 Subtask 8.1: Economic Analysis

This section presents the economic assessment of the Albion-Scipio and Napoleon Oilfields.
The cost-benefit analysis uses results from the reservoir model, described in Section 8.1, in
conjunction with a revenue and cost model to determine the net present value, internal rate of
return, and CO2 break-even cost (Figure 205). This analysis framework was used in previous
work by Battelle described by Fukai et al. (2015) and relies on work conducted by Advanced
Resources International (ARI) and researchers at Carnegie Mellon University (CMU) in
collaboration with the DOE’s NETL (ARI, 2006; McCoy and Rubin, 2006; McCoy, 2009; McCoy
and Rubin, 2009; U.S. DOE-NETL, 2011, 2014a). Calculation schemes for the revenue and cost
models were selected from these studies and verified using project-specific data and internal
expertise. Reservoir data was derived from a dynamic reservoir model based on a SEM
developed using site-specific data and analysis.

CO2-EOR performance results from the reservoir model, described in Section 8.1, are integrated
into separate revenue and cost models that calculate net revenue and total project costs,
respectively. The resulting net cash flow is then discounted to derive the net present value
(NPV) and internal rate of return (IRR) of the project. The break-even cost of CO2 is calculated
to represent the maximum price a CO2-EOR operator could pay for CO2 without incurring loss
on the project, and is calculated as a function of oil price to help determine a viable market price
for captured CO2 based on the prevailing price of oil.

CO2 storage is treated as a byproduct of EOR operations and is not directly associated with
incurrence of project costs. As such, injection wells considered in this assessment can be
categorized as Class II EOR wells, rather than Class VI under the United States Environmental
Protection Agency (U.S. EPA) underground injection control (UIC) program (U.S. EPA, 2015).
The value of 45Q tax credits was considered in all the scenarios to determine if claiming the
credits could improve the economics in scenarios where the value of oil production is insufficient
to generate a positive NPV.

The costs of CO2 site monitoring and verification were ignored, as this could be reasonably
assumed to be available from initial development and production of the oilfield. Liability was also
not included in the analysis. The cost model assumes the CO2 initially delivered to the site via
trunkline will be of sufficient purity and pressure for miscible injection. A per tonne cost of CO2 is
assigned based on the maximum amount of CO2 required for field development. The costs and
revenue associated with the recovery of natural gas and natural gas liquids were not included in
the analysis.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                205

Figure 205. Schematic of the cost-benefit analysis framework used in this work (adapted from Fukai et al.,
2015).

Cost-Benefit Analysis Methodology

Reservoir Performance Model
Reservoirs of Interest
In order to approximate scale-up across the entire TBR trend in southern Michigan, the two
largest fields in the trend – Albion-Scipio and Napoleon – were considered for economic
analysis. These two fields account for roughly 86% of oil production to date within the trend,
hence providing a reasonable metric for full trend scale-up. Field development history, including
historical and current well completions, are used to characterize the Napoleon and Albion-Scipio
field geometries for flood design. Specifically, this includes: the number of active and total wells
in the field; and the number of injection wells, production wells, and injection patterns needed for
a full-field development scenario. These values were generated using data from the Michigan
Department of the Environment, Great Lakes and Energy (MI EGLE, 2025) and are shown for
each field in Table 32.

Model Realizations
As described in Task 3.18.2, a series of simplified reservoir performance models were
developed for the Napoleon and Albion-Scipio fields, resolving volumetric parameters such as
the potential incremental oil production (stock tank barrels [STB], one thousand STB [MSTB], or
million STB [MMSTB]), associated water production (STB, MSTB, or MMSTB), and CO2
production and recycling (tonnes or millions tonnes [MMt]). Modeling results from the scale-up
analysis were used as input to the economic assessment. Four of the twelve model realizations
at each field, covering the full range of performance outcomes, were selected for economic
modeling. These scenarios are:

 • Medium-Low Scenario: 15% Primary RF, 5 year to peak production, 5% Incremental
 RF
 • Low Scenario: 40% Primary RF, 5 year to peak production, 5% Incremental RF
 • High Scenario: 15% Primary RF, 1 year to peak production, 15% Incremental RF
 • Medium-High Scenario: 40% Primary RF, 1 year to peak production, 15% Incremental
 RF
The results of these four modeling scenarios are summarized in Table 33. The oil production
rates for these scenarios at the Albion Scipio field are shown in Figure 206, along with field
production data since 1980 and modeled decline curve to this production data (similar curves
were produced for the Napoleon field). It should be noted that the Albion-Scipio field was mostly
depleted by 1980, and peak production rates at the field were more in line with the modeled
rates post-CO2 treatment.

Revenue Model
The revenue model calculates net revenue from oil recovered and sold at a specified oil price,
with relevant tax deductions (Table 34). Royalties comprising 15% of the gross revenue were
included in the calculation, derived from the average royalty percentage employed in the ARI
and CMU models. The current Michigan severance tax on conventional crude oil production of
6.6% and an oil and gas fee of 1% was applied (Michigan Department of the Treasury, 2026).
An Ad Valorem tax of 3.3% was determined, based on the average tax percentage levied on
mineral properties in Lee Township of Calhoun County, Michigan (Transnation Title Agency,
2024). Severance and Ad Valorem taxes were deducted from the remaining revenue after
royalty taxes. The revenue model calculation scheme produces a time series of incoming net
cash flows, where the resulting net revenue of the project can be evaluated incrementally during
specific periods of operation, and cumulatively at the end of the project life.

Table 34. Inputs and definitions of parameters included in the revenue model.

 Market price of oil. Simulations are for the market price of oil to be $40/bbl,
 Oil Price (USD/STB) $60/bbl, and $90/bbl
 Gross Revenue Revenue from oil recovered and sold at the specified market price; oil
 (USD) recovered (STB) x oil price ($/STB)
 Royalty (%) 15% of gross revenue; 0.15 x gross revenue ($)
 Severance Tax (%) 6.6% of revenue after royalty tax; 0.066 x gross revenue ($)
 Oil and Gas Fee (%) 1% of gross cash market value; 0.01 x gross revenue ($)

Cost Models
Cost models were used to determine the price of an integrated CO2-EOR project. The
processes for determining the costs of capture, transport, CO2 injection, and oil production are
detailed in this section.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    208

Capture Cost Model
The model assumes that CO2 is provided at a price per tonne from an outside entity. In this way
CO2 capture and transport costs are assumed to be a fee for service and the costs are incurred
during the operational period only. This approach assumes that CO2 will be available from
industrial and/or electric power facilities with CO2 capture facilities. As such, an additional 15%
is added to the reported capture cost to simulate the profit collected by the capture entity.

Injection/Storage Cost Model

The cost model calculated total costs for the CO2-EOR project and are broken out by capital
costs and operating costs. Capital costs include well-related costs and CO2 distribution and

recycling equipment. Capital costs are accrued in Year 1 of the project. Operating costs are
accrued for the remainder of the project. Total Operations and Maintenance (O&M) costs
account for consumables, services, and labor associated with ongoing site operation and
maintenance (surface and subsurface), liquid lifting/handling, general and administrative (G&A)
expenses, and plugging and abandonment for all wells and site restoration for all equipment and
piping.

Capital Cost calculations used to estimate injection and production well equipment costs and
CO2 related costs (i.e. CO2 distribution and recycling plant capital costs) are outlined in Table
37. This process has been used by Battelle before in a study funded by the Ohio Coal
Development Office (OCDO) as described by Fukai et al. (2015). Equations established by the
previous studies were used to calculate costs. All capital and operating costs were adjusted to
represent constant 2023 U.S. dollars (USD) using S&P Global Upstream Capital Cost Index
(UCCI) (S&P Global, 2024). These values were then escalated to 2026$ equivalents (the year
the project is assumed to start) using an escalation rate of 3.3%.

Well-related costs account for drilling and completion (D&C), equipment, services, labor, and
site-related capital costs associated with new injection well equipment and existing well
conversion. Well D&C costs were calculated for all newly installed wells using Equation 1 in
Table 38. New producing equipment for all newly installed production wells and a percentage of
the existing wells (see Other Cost Items, below) using Equation 2. Injection well equipment is
calculated for all injection wells (i.e., newly drilled injection wells and converted existing
production wells) using Equation 3. 20% of existing wells are assumed to be converted to
injection wells in all scenarios. These costs are calculated using Equation 4.

CO2 costs include the capital costs associated with on-site CO2 distribution and CO2 recycling.
Onsite distribution costs are based on the maximum amount of CO2 to be distributed per day
multiplied by the length of the lines (i.e., the length of the field). These costs are calculated
using Equation 6. The CO2 recycling rate in standard cubic feet (scf; 60°F, 1 atm) per day was
used to estimate the capital costs of the CO2 recycling facility (Equation 4). CO2 recycling,
including on-site separation, processing, and recompression as shown in Figure 207.

costs escalated from 2006$ to 2023$ using a factor of 1.317 based on the S&P Global Upstream
Capital Cost Index (UCCI). Capital costs escalated from 2009$ to 2023$ using a factor of 1.095
based on the S&P Global UCCI. (S&P Global, 2024).

Other Capital Cost Items: Additional cost items that are not directly related to well drilling, well
equipment, CO2 distribution, or CO2 recycling were also considered in the analysis (presented
as 2023$ in Table 38). The model predicts costs for well testing and rehabilitation of existing
wells. All existing wells will be tested with a four-armed caliper and mechanical integrity test
(MIT). An estimated 25% of existing wells are assumed to need well rehabilitation. The same
percentage of wells are assumed to need replacement production well equipment. Newly drilled
wells have additional costs beyond drilling and completion and production or injection well
equipment, calculated above. Because the Albion-Scipio and Napoleon Fields Characterization
of new wells is assumed to be $100,000 per well and includes a triple combo log and cement
bond log (CBL).

Notes: 1. A price of $40 per tonne was used for the Napoleon Field because the CO2 requirements could be satisfied from a nearby

Total O&M costs: Total O&M costs for the site include periodic O&M costs, liquid lifting costs,
and G&A expenses. All operating costs data were escalated to 2023$ equivalents using the
Upstream Operation Cost Index (UOCI) (S&P Global, 2024). These values were then escalated
to 2026$ equivalents (the year the project is assumed to start) using an escalation rate of 3.3%.
Periodic O&M costs reflect ongoing expenses associated with the operation and maintenance of
the CO2-EOR site at the required capacity over a given time, including electricity, consumables,
periodic well work over, surface and subsurface repairs (Equation 8, Table 37). Periodic O&M
costs are also dependent on the number and depth of wells used in the project. Liquid lifting
costs are estimated as $0.27 per volume of oil and water produced (Equation 9, Table 37).
CO2 operating costs include CO2 purchased for injection (see Table 39) and operations of the
CO2 recycling plant. Ongoing O&M costs for the CO2 recycling plant are determined by the
volume of CO2 recycled (scf) per time series set at 1% of the price of oil per STB to reflect the
cost of on-site generation of the energy required for operation (Equation 7, Table 38). Non-
injection or production operational costs include off-site water disposal, well plugging, and site
restoration. Water disposal costs were calculated on a per barrel basis and assumed to be $1
per STB (or $1,000 per MSTB) of water. Well plugging and abandonment costs are per well
costs obtained from the DOE/NETL Saline Storage Cost Model (DOE/NETL, 2023). Equipment
removal and site restoration are based on estimates and are single lump-sum cost. Plugging
and abandonment and equipment removal and site restorage area incurred at the end of the
project. G&A costs are calculated as 20% of the sum of periodic O&M and liquid lifting costs
(Equation 10).

Financial Metrics
Financial metrics used in the model are outlined in Table 39. All costs are reported in 2026$. A
nominal discount factor of 15% (and associated real discount factor of 11.3%) and an escalation
factor of 3.3% was used. The current 45Q a rate of $85 per tonne for CO2 utilization was used
and escalated from the base year of 2026 using the escalation rate of 3.3%. The credit is only
claimed for the first 12 years of the project, and only 90% of the total CO2 stored for each of
these 12 years is assumed to be eligible. This reduction simulates the lifecycle CO2-equivalent
emissions from the project, which would reduce the value of the credit.

Analysis Scenarios and Evaluation Metrics

Price Scenarios
The market scenarios examined for the cost-benefit analysis of potential CO2-EOR co-storage
projects in the oilfields of interest reflected an oil price of $60 per barrel. A CO2 price was
determined by the nearby emitters and annual CO2 demand. The analysis assumes that the
$60/STB of oil (current oil price) was defined as the lower price scenario and $80/STB of oil as
the higher price scenario in the NPV calculated in the analysis. The effect of discount rate on
CO2-EOR project economics was also evaluated. The discount rate represents the percentage
difference between the present and future value of an expected cash flow, accounting for the
time-dependent value of money and risk associated with a future investment. A discount rate of
15% was used. The analysis considers two operating periods, 12 years and 20 years, for all
scenarios.

Net Present Value
The value of future cash flows accrued incrementally and cumulatively over the duration of the
CO2-EOR project is represented by the NPV. The NPV is calculated as the sum of cash inflows
and outflows, discounted to account for the time value of money and the risk/uncertainty
associated with future cash flows. The net revenues (incoming cash flows) from the revenue
model were integrated with total costs (outgoing cash flows) calculated by the cost model to
derive a time series (years) of NPV and IRR estimates. The cumulative NPV calculated at the
end of the operation was then used to evaluate the profitability of the CO2-EOR operation, such
that positive values represent scenarios of profit and negative values represent financial losses
incurred by investors in the project.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                      214

CO2 Break-Even Cost
The break-even cost of CO2 is the CO2 purchase price that results in a NPV equal to zero, and
can be interpreted as the maximum price an EOR operator could pay for CO2 without incurring a
loss on the project (McCoy, 2009; McCoy and Rubin, 2009). The break-even cost of CO2 was
calculated as a function of various oil prices to define the specific price conditions at which CO2
becomes a viable purchase for EOR, and a marketable asset for capture facilities. The overall
trend observed in the CO2 break-even cost calculated over the range of oil prices was used to
derive the unit value of CO2 relative to oil for the oil field of interest. It should be noted that CO2-
EOR requires higher upfront capital and operational investments, such that the break-even cost
represents a much different timeline compared to primary oil production.

Effect of 45Q Tax Credits
The effect of the 45Q tax credits on project economics was determined for all scenarios. The tax
credits were assumed to be $85/tonne based on recent changes enacted in the One Big
Beautiful Bill. The tax credits are assumed to be claimed for 12 years, are escalated 3.3% a
year from a base year of 2026, and are claimed for only 90% of the CO2 stored, according to the
model, to simulate losses during the CO2 lifecycle analysis.

Effect of Existing Infrastructure
For each scenario of the Albion-Scipio Field, two possible simulation groups were modeled. The
first considers the full field development, which requires the drilling of additional injection and
production wells to match the number of active and plugged and abandoned wells in the field
and, thus, the reservoir modeling domain. The second considers only the wells in the field that
are currently active. For this scenario, only 37 of the possible 135 injection well patterns were
available (approximately 27.41%). For the scenarios that consider existing infrastructure only,
the reservoir model inputs (e.g. CO2 injection, oil production, and CO2 recycling rate) will be
multiplied by a factor of 27.41% to account for the smaller field area covered by these
scenarios. This process was not completed for the Napoleon Field because nearly all of the
wells in the field are still active.

Application of Methodology to the Oilfields of Interest

The economic assessment focused on two oilfields of interest, the Albion-Scipio and the
Napoleon Oilfields. This section presents each oilfield separately and considers their
development over a 12-year project and a 20-year project. For each scenario, the revenue was
calculated at an oil price of $60 per STB (approximate current price) and $80 per STB (last
reached in 2023) (macrotrends, 2026). All costs and revenue are presented as real
costs/revenue in 2026$.

Four scenarios were presented for each Simulation Group. They are bound by a scenario with
low recovery factor (Low RF) and high recovery factor (High RF) to show the pessimistic and
optimistic case, respectively, and are presented as Scenarios 6 and 7, respectively. Two
scenarios with moderate recovery factors were also considered, one with a longer time to
achieve peak oil production (Mod RF, long peak) and another with a shorter time to achieve
peak oil production (Mod RF, short peak). These scenarios are presented as Scenarios 3 and
10, respectively.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  215

Albion-Scipio
Economic model results for the Albion-Scipio Oilfield are presented for a 12-year project and a
20-year project in Table 40 and Table 41, respectively. This section discusses the results of the
12-year projects followed a discussion of the results of the 20-year projects. As of 2023, the
Albion-Scipio Field has 186 active wells and 487 abandoned wells (673 wells total) that
produced oil. Because of the number of legacy wells, two different simulation groups were
completed for the field. The first considers the economics of redrilling several hundred wells to
support 135 injection patterns of four producing wells to every injection well (675 wells total).
This set of simulations, labeled Group 1, most closely matches the results of reservoir modeling
because the entirety of the oilfield is produceable by the 135 injection patterns. The second set
of simulations, referred to as Group 2, considers only the 186 active wells in the field, yielding
37 injection patterns of four producers to every injection well. Because there is no evidence that
the existing wells are in a part of the reservoir that produces either a higher or lower than the
average amount of oil, the analysis was simplified by assuming that the proportion of oil the
wells produce is equal to the ratio of injection patterns in Group 2 compared to Group 1 (i.e.,
27.41%).

Twelve-Year Projects

Capital Expenses: Total capital expenses (CAPEX) are similar across all four simulations for
Group 1 (Figure 208). This is because the costs that are consistent across all these simulations
(e.g., drilling and completion [D&C] costs and new wells characterization, production well
equipment costs, and injection well equipment costs) account for 80% or more of the costs for
each simulation, meaning that capital costs that vary across the simulations (CO2 recycling plant
and CO2 distribution network) are less important for Group 1. Capital costs were more variable
for the simulations for Group 2. Capital expenses in these simulations varied by more than two
times, from $53.8 million to $135.5 million.

CO2 Costs and OPEX: CO2 costs, which are considered operational expenses, vary by 6.5
times for simulations with Group 1 (Table 41). CO2-related expenses were dominated by the
purchase of CO2, which outstrips CO2 recycling expenses by eight to more than 20 times. CO2
expenses for Group 2 (Table 41) vary by the same amounts; however, the overall CO2
expenses are more than three times lower for these scenarios compared to the simulations for
Group 1.

Total non-CO2 OPEX vary by more than 1.5 times for simulations within Groups 1 and
simulations within Group 2. Well operations and maintenance (O&M) expenses account for
around half to more than three-quarters of the non-CO2 OPEX in Group 1, depending on the
simulation, and two-fifths to nearly two-thirds of the non-CO2 OPEX in Group 2, depending on
the simulation. G&A costs are 16.7% of all non-CO2 OPEX costs in all simulations. The
remainder of the non-CO2 OPEX is spent on Water Disposal (4 to 23% for simulations of Group
1 and 3 to 20% for simulations of Group 2), well plugging and abandonment and site restoration
(11 to 16% for simulations of Group 1 and 10 to 14% for simulations of Group 2), and liquid
lifting (less than 10% for all simulations).

Twenty-Year Projects

Capital Expenses: CAPEX are similar across all four simulations for the Group 1 20-year
project. The CAPEX for the 20-year project is also similar to the CAPEX the 12-year project

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                                                                                          219

(Figure 212). The difference is limited to the CAPEX for the CO2 plant, which are higher for the
20-year project compared to the 12-year project. This is because the maximum CO2 recycle rate
is higher in years 13 through 20 compared to years one through 12, requiring a higher capacity
recycling plant. This increases the CAPEX for the project by 4% or less.

CO2 Costs and OPEX: CO2 costs, which are considered operational expenses, vary by 6.5
times for simulations with Group 1 (Figure 212). Like the 12-year project, CO2-related expenses
for the 20-year project were dominated by the purchase of CO2, which outstrips CO2 recycling
expenses by eight to more than 20 times. CO2 expenses for Group 2 vary by the same amounts
(Figure 213); however, the overall CO2 expenses are more than three times lower for these
scenarios compared to the simulations for Group 1. The total costs for CO2 for the 20-year
project are higher than the 12-year project because of the additional CO2 either purchased or
recycled in years 13 to 20 of the project. Total non-CO2 OPEX vary by more than 1.4 times for
simulations within Group 1 and for simulations within Group 2. Well O&M expenses account for
around half to 70% of the non-CO2 OPEX in Group 1, depending on the simulation, and half to
more than two-thirds of the non-CO2 OPEX in Group 2, depending on the simulation. G&A costs
are 16.7% of all non-CO2 OPEX costs in all simulations. The remainder of the non-CO2 OPEX is
spent on Water Disposal (3 to 17% for simulations of Group 1 and 1% to 17% for simulations of
Group 2), well plugging and abandonment and site restoration (11% to 16% for simulations of
Group 1 and 10% to 14% for simulations of Group 2), and liquid lifting (less than 10% for all
simulations).

Net Revenue: For Group 1, net revenue ranges from $707.5 million to $5,771 without 45Q and

$865.5 million to $6,746 million with 45Q for across all simulations when the oil price is $60 per
STB (Figure 214). If the oil price increases to $80 per STB, net revenue increases to $943.3
million to $7,695 million without 45Q and $1,101 million to $8,670 million with 45Q. For Group 2,
net revenue ranges from $193.9 million to $1,582 without 45Q and $237.2 million to $1,849
million with 45Q for across all simulations when the oil price is $60 per STB (Figure 215). If the
oil price increases to $80 per STB, net revenue increases to $258.6 million to $2,109 million
without 45Q and $301.9 million to $2,376 million with 45Q.

Napoleon
Economic model results for the Napoleon Oilfield are presented for a 12-year project and a 20-
year project in Table 42 and Table 43, respectively. This section discusses the results of the 12-

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                                        223

year projects followed a discussion of the results of the 20-year projects. Because the field has
many of the original producing wells, the only scenario considered was one in which existing
infrastructure is used to produce the whole field. There are 55 existing wells, yielding 11
injection patterns of four producers to every injection well.

Twelve-Year Projects

Capital Expenses: Capital costs were more variable for the simulations for the Napoleon field
compared to the CAPEX for Group 1 of the Albion-Scipio Oilfield. CAPEX in the Napoleon
Oilfield varied by more than two times, from $18.1 million to $36.9 million (Figure 216). CAPEX
in the Napoleon Oilfield varied by more than two times, from $18.1 million to $36.9 million.

Purchasing injection well surface equipment is the largest capital cost for three of the four
simulations ($8.9 million for all simulations). For the High RF simulation, however, the CO2
recycling plant was the largest capital expense ($18.9 million, or more than half of the $36.9
million CAPEX costs). This is because this simulation requires a plant with the capacity to
handle more than 18 MMscf of CO2 per day compared to a capacity of less than 10 MMscf of
CO2 per day for the other simulations.

CO2 Costs and OPEX: Carbon dioxide costs, which are considered operational expenses, vary
by 8.4 times for simulations with the Napoleon Oilfield (Figure 216). CO2-related expenses were
dominated by the purchase of CO2, which outstrips CO2 recycling expenses by 5.6 to more than
20 times. Carbon dioxide recycling expenses vary by nearly 30 times between the simulations.
Total non-CO2 OPEX vary by around 1.4 times for simulations of the Napoleon Oilfield. Well
O&M expenses account for around 40% to 60% of non-CO2 OPEX, depending on the
simulation. G&A costs are 16.7% of non-CO2 OPEX costs in all simulations. The remainder of
the non-CO2 OPEX is spent on water disposal (6% to 21%), well plugging and abandonment
and site restoration (10% to 14%), and liquid lifting (11% or less for all simulations). For most
simulations, well O&M is the largest annual operational expense (Figure 216). The High RF
simulation is the exception. The amount of CO2 that must be purchased makes this cost slightly
larger in this compared to the well O&M costs.

Net Revenue: Net revenue ranges from $89 million to $440 million without 45Q and $102million
to $522 million with 45Q for across all simulations when the oil price is $60 per STB (Figure
217). If the oil price increases to $80 per STB, net revenue increases to $118 million to $587
million without 45Q and $132 million to $669 million with 45Q.

Twenty-Year Projects

Capital Expenses: Capital costs were more variable for the simulations for the Napoleon field
compared to the CAPEX for Group 1 of the Albion-Scipio Oilfield (Figure 218). CAPEX in the
Napoleon Oilfield varied by more than two times, from $18.1 million to $36.9 million. Purchasing
injection well surface equipment is the largest capital cost for three of the four simulations ($8.9
million for all simulations). For the High RF simulation, however, the CO2 recycling plant was the
largest capital expense ($24.4 million, or more than half of the $42.6 million CAPEX costs). This
is because this simulation requires a plant with the capacity to handle nearly 24 MMscf of CO2
per day compared to a capacity of less than 10 MMscf of CO2 per day for the other simulations.

CO2 Costs and OPEX: CO2 costs, which are considered operational expenses, vary by 8.4
times for simulations with the Napoleon Oilfield. CO2-related expenses were dominated by the
purchase of CO2, which outstrips CO2 recycling expenses by 5.6 to more than 20 times. CO2
recycling expenses vary by nearly 30 times between the simulations. Total non-CO2 OPEX vary
by around 1.4 times for simulations of the Napoleon Oilfield. Well O&M expenses account for
around 40% to around 60% of non-CO2 OPEX, depending on the simulation. G&A costs are
16.7% of all non-CO2 OPEX costs in all simulations. The remainder of the non-CO2 OPEX is
spent on Water Disposal (6% to 21%), well plugging and abandonment and site restoration
(10% to 14%), and liquid lifting (11% or less for all simulations). For most simulations, well O&M
is the largest annual operational expense (Figure 218). The High RF simulation is the exception.
The amount of CO2 that must be purchased makes this cost slightly larger in this compared to
the well O&M costs.

Net Revenue: Net revenue ranges from $89 million to $440 million without 45Q and $102
million to $522 million with 45Q for across all simulations when the oil price is $60 per STB
(Figure 219). If the oil price increases to $80 per STB, net revenue increases to $118 million to
$587 million without 45Q and $132 million to $669 million with 45Q.

Discussion

Cash Flow

12-Year Project in the Albion-Scipio: The number of years with positive cash flow, the year of
the project where it becomes profitable, IRR, NPV, and CO2 break-even price are presented in
Table 44 for all simulations in both simulation groups. Net cumulative cash flows are shown for
all simulations when oil is $60 per STB and $80 per STB in Figure 220 and Figure 221,
respectively. For Group 1, only the most optimistic case is profitable in any of the simulations.

When the oil price is $60 per STB, 45Q tax credits are required to make the simulation profitable
(NPV of $550 million). When the oil price is increased to $80 per STB, the project is profitable
with or without 45Q, although the inclusion of the tax credits does improve the economics (NPV
of $872 million without 45Q and $1,479 million with 45Q). These simulations all become
profitable in year three or four of operations. The CO2 break-even cost exceeds the price of CO2
available from dilute sources (e.g., $70 per tonne) for each of these simulations. For the
simulation where oil is $60 per STB and the 45Q tax credits are not claimed, the CO2 break-
even price drops to $64.32 – a cost of capture that is easily achievable for purer sources, such
as ethanol plants and nitrogen plants. For all other simulations, the CO2 break-even price is
negative, meaning an entity would have to pay the operator for disposal of CO2, oil would need
to be more than $80 per STB, and/or another source of income would need to be secured for
the project to be profitable.

Using existing infrastructure greatly improves the economics for the Albion-Scipio Field. Both
the optimistic case and the moderate recovery factor with a short period to peak recovery were
profitable with or without 45Q for scenarios where oil was $60 or $80 per STB, with NPV
ranging from $19 million to $705 million and CO2 break-even prices ranging from $88.99 to
$330.07 per tonne. The moderate recovery scenario with a long period to peak recovery was
also profitable if oil was $80 per STB and 45Q tax credits were claimed. The CO2 break-even

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                        228

price for this simulation was $107.65 per tonne. These simulations all become profitable
between years two to five of operations. Other scenarios for this simulation were profitable at a
lower price of CO2. The CO2 break-even price is $43.05 when oil is $80 per STB but 45Q credits
are not claimed and $55.00 when oil is $60 per STB and 45Q credits are claimed, both
achievable costs for CO2 for relatively pure sources. The remaining scenario for this simulation
(oil is $60 per STB and 45Q tax credits are not claimed) are not profitable and the break-even
price of CO2 is negative, meaning additional income sources would be required. The pessimistic
case is not profitable in any of the scenarios and the CO2 break-even price is negative.

negative, meaning an entity would have to pay the operator for disposal of CO2, oil would need

Like the 12-year project, using existing infrastructure greatly improves the economics for the
Albion-Scipio Field. Both the optimistic case and the moderate recovery factor with a short
period to peak recovery were profitable with or without 45Q for scenarios where oil was $60 or

prices ranging from $70.06 to $320.81 per tonne. The moderate recovery scenario with a long
period to peak recovery was also profitable if oil was $80 per STB and 45Q tax credits were
claimed. The CO2 break-even price for this simulation was $106.21 per tonne. These
simulations all become profitable between years two to six of operations but are unprofitable
after years 12 to 17. Other scenarios for this simulation were profitable at a lower price of CO2.

claimed and $50.15 per tonne when oil is $60 per STB and 45Q credits are claimed, both

(oil is $60 per STB and 45Q tax credits are not claimed) is not profitable and the break-even
price of CO2 is negative, meaning additional income sources would be required. The pessimistic
case is not profitable in any of the scenarios and the CO2 break-even price is negative.

Spurring Investment in CO2 Capture

The scenarios described thus far have assumed that CO2 is available in the quantities needed
at a given market price ($70 a tonne for the Albion-Scipio Field, satisfied by a less pure CO2
source, and $40 a tonne for the Napoleon, satisfied by a purer CO2 source). A more realistic
scenario in the short term would be for the CO2-EOR operator to provide a guarantee to
purchase a specific amount from a CO2 source so that the source is incentivized to build the
capture plant. To simulate this, the pessimistic (Low RF) and optimistic (High RF) cases for the
12-year and 20-year projects in the Napoleon Oilfield were used. The altered simulations
assume that all conditions are the same except that the amount of CO2 purchased per year is
held constant at the maximum annual CO2 purchase modeled (17,358 for the Low RF scenario
and 135,423 for the High RF scenario). Three different options were explored for the excess
CO2 not injected by in the Napoleon Oifleid: (1) a $8 per tonne fee to simulate either a disposal
fee paid to a third party for saline injection or the transfer of the CO2 to another oilfield via
pipeline, (2) no cost for the excess CO2 to simulate and offtake agreement of excess CO2 for
nearby Class VI or CO2 EOR, and (3) $8 per tonne income from the sale of the CO2 to another
operator for saline injection or CO2-EOR.

The results of this effort for 12-year and 20-year projects are provided in Table 48 and Table 49,
respectively. While the requirement to obtain the maximum amount of CO2 impacts the
economics of these projects, particularly for 20-year projects that must pay an $8 per tonne
disposal fee, the impact is not enough to make a project unprofitable. The NPV is impacted by
$1 million or less for 12-year or 20-year projects, and the IRR is largely unchanged. The CO2
break-even price is impacted, however, because additional CO2 must be purchased, particularly
in later years of the project. This is most notable for the high recovery factor simulations, which
decrease by more than 20% from the CO2 break-even prices of the original simulations. The
economics may be improved by the resale of the excess CO2. The current model assumes that
excess CO2 can be sold for a net of $8/tonne, which may be low because the purchase price is
originally $40 per tonne.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                         238

9.2 Development Plan Discussion

The economic model suggests that a project is viable at the Napoleon Oilfield in all but the most
conservative simulation (Low RF) at the current oil price of around $60 per STB and claiming
45Q tax credits could make this simulation economically viable as well. These results depend
on the availability of CO2 at $40 per tonne, which is well within the per tonne capture cost at an
ethanol plant (DOE/NETL, 2023). A requirement to purchase CO2 at same volume for the entire
project does not impact the economics of these simulations drastically because of the nature of
CO2 demand at for CO2-EOR in the field. In the early years of a project, CO2 is injected but has
not reached the producing wells. As the project progresses, CO2 is produced and recycled at an
increasing rate. Thus, the simulations in which CO2 must be purchased at a constant rate are
similar to the simulations in which CO2 is purchased as needed in the early years of the project
when the impact to NPV and IRR are greatest and only diverge as the project progresses.

The use of existing infrastructure was found to have the most profound impact on project
economics. Developing the entire Albion-Scipio Oilfield is uneconomic in all but the most
optimistic scenarios because only a little over a quarter of the wells that originally produced this
field are still active. The economics are improved when considering only the existing
infrastructure because the single largest capital expenditure (new well drilling and completion) is
negated; however, these simulations assume that the wells are placed in a way that would allow
for the creation of injection/producing well patterns conducive to CO2-EOR operations. In
addition, the CO2 demand, particularly for full field development, would necessitate capture from
a larger, more dilute industrial source. This would increase the cost of CO2 purchased during
operations. The Napoleon Oilfield does not have these same limitations because many of the
wells that originally produced the field are active. In addition, the lower CO2 demand means the
CO2 can be obtained by a purer CO2 source like a nearby ethanol plant.

Other sources of CO2 could be considered but are likely not as economically advantageous.
There are no nearby geologic sources of CO2 and their use would require high capital costs for
transport via pipeline or high operating costs for transport via train. In addition, the CO2 obtained
from a nearby industrial source would be more cost effective than purchasing industrially
manufactured CO2, which was purchased at cost of $150 to $165 per tonne during this project,
exceeding the per tonne capture costs from industrial facilities (DOE/NETL, 2023) by two to four
times.

The immediate next steps to support CO2-EOR operations in the oilfield are to continue studying
the field to determine if the assumptions the model makes are correct. This can be done by
introducing additional data and layers of complexity to the model assumptions. Limitations of the
economic models described in this report include a simplified development schedule. The
models assume that CAPEX is incurred entirely within the first year of the project and that
operations occur within the entire oilfield or all existing wells (in the case of the Group 2
simulations of the Albion-Scipio Oilfield) at the same time. Staged field development may impact
the NPV and IRR of the project. This staged development may help to limit the size of the CO2
recycling plant, particularly if the CO2 demand is higher than anticipated. The economics of
these projects may also be improved through saline storage of excess CO2 acquired from
purchase agreements. This would provide an outlet for excess CO2 that could derive additional
revenue from 45Q tax credits. In addition to field development strategies, additional modeling
that considers the heterogeneity of the geologic and reservoir properties of the field must be
done to ensure an accurate representation potential production of from CO2-EOR of the oilfields
of interest.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               237

Finally, an effort to characterize the existing infrastructure in the oilfields of interest and the
additional infrastructure required for CO2-EOR is also needed. This effort should ensure the
mechanical integrity of the existing wells, evaluate the feasibility of converting a subset of these
wells to CO2 injections wells, and ensure that the wells spacing is correct for CO2-EOR
operations. Additional infrastructure needed to obtain and transport CO2 to the injection wells,
separate and recycle produced CO2, and store and transport produced oil and water for refining
and disposal, respectively, must also be sited and acquired.

Subtask 8.2. Development Strategy Plan

The Development Strategy Plan is intended to guide project development in the TBR in
southern Michigan. The plan describes how methodologies developed during the project will be
used to accelerate CO2-EOR adoption in TBR fields. The plan is geared for use by individual
operators because production in the region is conducted by small- to medium-sized
independent producers. To this end, the plan covers the requirements of building out a CO2-
EOR project in the area while recognizing the level of access to resources and capital these
operators face.

The plan helps to identify, assess, and develop solutions to technical, regulatory, and economic
challenges to enable wide-scale field development. Specifically, the plan covers (1) geologic
characterization of the reservoir; (2) required oil properties to implement CO2-EOR; (3) required
surface infrastructure; (4) meeting the greenhouse gas reporting requirements of the federal
government and state of Michigan; and (5) developing a monitoring, reporting, and verification
plan.
 1. Geologic Characterization

This study has thoroughly characterized the geologic conditions of the Trenton-Black River
trend using the available data. Trend-wide characterization was completed in Task 2. However,
the significant heterogeneity of the vuggy porosity and fracture systems in this trend continue to
present a challenge both during primary production and EOR operations. These unknowns
present themselves as major risks for drilling success, reservoir presence, injection and
production strategies, infrastructure utilization, and other aspects of field development, all of
which can strongly impact project economics. Given this remaining uncertainty, the team
makes the following recommendations to continue characterizing the TBR pore system, drive
down uncertainty, and improve recovery factors.

Improve understanding of pore system using borehole geophysics

Geophysical techniques used for finding HTD fields are well established- 3D surface seismic
with high-quality processing and thorough attribute analysis is effective for identifying HTD
reservoirs (Hart et al., 2009). But these techniques fall short when it comes to seeing inside the
reservoirs, specifically in identifying fracture and vug density and porosity distribution within the
targeted reservoir. Because this study does not deal with exploration for new TBR HTD fields, it
does not address improvements in reservoir identification and will instead focus on reservoir
characterization.

The key limiting factor for 3D surface seismic assessment of these reservoirs is the relatively
thin nature of the vuggy primary porosity and the discontinuous nature of the fracture system.
Vug trains can range from less than a foot thick to several tens of feet, far too small to be
imaged directly by most surface geophysical techniques. As a result, vug presence should be
inferred via the presence of other features (faults, folds, sags) that are thought to be involved in

Battelle | May 2026                                                                               238

hydrothermal dolomite formation, and seismic attributes like attenuation or anisotropy that give

us clues as to vug or fracture presence but do not provide direct evidence as to porosity type,
quality, or continuity or whether fractures are open or closed. Thus, it is very difficult to directly
or indirectly identify vuggy flow pathways in these highly complex reservoirs. Similarly, fractures
within the reservoirs can vary in scale from feet to miles in length, may be singular or in swarms,
and can range from tightly cemented and impermeable to open with darcy-scale permeability.

Moreover, a single fracture or fracture swarm can exhibit all of these characteristics at once,
resulting in seismic responses that may vary wildly across a single feature, all at scales that are
far below the resolution of today’s most cutting-edge land-based 3D surveys.

This necessitates the use of borehole geophysical techniques such as VSPs, image logs, cross-
well seismic tomography, and other techniques. Image logs have been utilized extensively
across the play, but because of their depth of investigation, they provide limited information
about the character and extent of highly variable vug and fracture systems.

Single- and multi-well downhole seismic acquisitions have been shown to be effective both for
identification of fractures farther away from wellbores and for identification of CO2 location within
reservoirs (Gritto et al., 2004). Where well spacing is sufficiently close (less than 1000’ between
wells), cross-well seismic is recommended, as it provides the highest resolution data available.
These opportunities are limited: only 17 active wells in the trend are located within 250 ft of
another active well, an additional six wells are located with 500 ft of their nearest neighbor, and
63 wells are located between 500-1,000 ft of their nearest neighbor (Figure 228).

These techniques may be able to illuminate key vug or fracture pathways, allowing for improved
reservoir modeling and identification of preferential flow pathways. Acquisition campaigns
should be undertaken prior to CO2 injection to identify key flow pathways, allowing for better
planning of producer-injector arrangement, and during CO2 injection to understand real-time
CO2 migration and to improve conceptual, static, and dynamic models of TBR reservoirs. Data
gathered during injection can also be useful for monitoring, reporting, and verification, providing
strong evidence of containment.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                239

 Figure 228. Active TBR wells colored by distance to nearest neighbor. High-quality cross-well seismic
 can be acquired in wells <250 ft apart, moderate quality in wells <500 ft apart, and low quality in wells
    500-1,000 ft apart.

Increase scope of lab testing to better understand impact of fractures and large vugs

In this study, lab testing investigated CO2-oil interactions, surfactant interactions with oil, water,
and the rock face, and tested the ability of surfactant-enhanced CO2 flood to improve oil
recovery in TBR reservoirs. It also investigated the porosity, permeability, and character of
vuggy pore networks and matrix pore networks in core samples taken from TBR reservoirs and
compared oil chemistry from primary production to oil chemistry after surfactant-enhanced CO2
stimulation. The main conclusions from this work support the idea that surfactant-enhanced
CO2 stimulation should improve oil recovery within the TBR trend, and should be significantly
more effective than water flooding. Field tests support these conclusions, with one notable
caveat: Lab testing indicated that the CETAC-30 surfactant used in the field test produces a
durable foam under reservoir conditions, and that the foam could be useful in diverting CO2 from
high-transmissibility pathways like large vugs and open fractures toward lower transmissibility
pathways like smaller vug systems and matrix porosity. This behavior was not observed in the
field test, where pressure and temperature data at offset wells indicates that CO2 moved more
or less freely across the reservoir.

It is believed that because there are vugs and fractures within the reservoir that are too large to
be effectively sampled at core scale, laboratory testing protocols did not fully characterize the

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                       240

full range of permeability and transmissivity, and the impact that the presence of exceptionally
transmissive features would have on behavior of oil, gas, water, and CO2 within the reservoir.
The maximum vug size identified in the core analysis was 11.1 mm in diameter (Figure 229),
orders of magnitude smaller than the largest vugs that exist within the field. Moreover, no
samples used to assess oil recovery contained large, open fractures. This likely caused an
overprediction of production uplift, with core floods showing incremental recovery of up to 61% of
OOIP.

Repeating core flooding testing on larger core samples or manufactured media that contain
large, open fractures and vugs to better assess incremental recovery is recommended.

Figure 229. CT scan of the largest pore identified in laboratory-tested core samples (sample #6-44).

Repeat core flooding & oil recovery tests at a broader range of pressures

All core flooding tests and surfactant tests were conducted at 1,200 psi, a high pressure where
CO2 is in the liquid or supercritical phase and oil and CO2 behave as pseudo-miscible to
miscible fluids. The team does not recommend undertaking a water repressurization operation
prior to CO2 stimulation, as this would require a vast quantity of water, damage CO2 storage
capacity and recovery efficiency, and extend project timelines. This means that operators
should inject CO2 into ambient reservoir conditions, where CO2 would behave as a gas until
sufficiently high pressure is established to cause a phase change. Core flooding tests that
assess the behavior and effectiveness of CO2 and surfactant at pressures that represent the
entire range of potential reservoir conditions (<100 psi to 1,300 psi) are recommended so field
operators can understand the behavior of CO2 and oil in this lower pressure regime, allowing for
optimized early-time CO2 injection and oil production strategies.

Investigate the benefits of other conformance technologies, such as polymer flooding

Our lab testing indicates that foam flooding in the core samples that were used provided at least
some CO2 mobility control. Given the lack of CO2 mobility control observed during field testing, it

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                    241

is not believed that CETAC or other surfactants provide sufficient mobility on their own in
reservoirs where the dominant flow pathways are large vug systems and open fractures.

It is recommended to investigate polymer flooding as a means for diverting injected CO2 and/or
water from highly permeable vugs and fractures toward smaller, lower-permeability features
such as small vugs and matrix pores. Studies by Zhang et al. (2011), Wu et al. (2023), Prakesh
et al. (2024) and others indicate that polymer floods, and polymer-alternating-gas (PAG) floods
can be effective for improving mobility control and increasing volumetric sweep for both miscible
and immiscible CO2 floods, and in the presence or absence of surfactants. Polymer behavior
can be highly sensitive to surfactant chemistry, water chemistry and salinity (Lee et al., 2019;
Wu et al., 2023), so care should be taken to analyze candidate polymers and surfactants at
reservoir conditions, in the presence of reservoir oil, and in the presence of both reservoir water
and any water that may be used as an injectate.

Use larger well, seismic, production and laboratory datasets to generate high
definition geomodels

The data set for the Lee 26 (Rice Creek) field that was used as this projects field test site was
relatively limited: Four wells, a moderate- to high-quality 3D seismic volume, and incomplete
production data. Because the wells were effectively in a straight-line, three-dimensional
distribution of reservoir characteristics within the 3D static geomodel was highly uncertain away
from the plain of the wells. Because of the relatively thin reservoir compared to depth, seismic
porosity inversions were insufficiently high-resolution to provide meaningful control on porosity
distribution. And because the production data set had very limited pressure and production
volume data for some parts of the field life (and no samples or analysis of reservoir fluid
composition were available at any point during field life), generating a history-matched reservoir
depletion model was challenging. These challenges resulted in a reservoir model that did not
fully predict actual reservoir performance, as described in the Task 7 section of the final report.

The project team recommends that operators planning to implement CO2-EOR in the TBR trend
utilize the much larger datasets available to them in the Napoleon, Stoney Point, and Albion-
Scipio trends to generate higher-resolution geomodels and more robust reservoir history
matches. This will allow for improved prediction of reservoir behavior and give operators a
better understanding of how to plan CO2-EOR campaigns in the trend.

 2. Required Oil Properties

Based on the analysis of the mobilized oil in the Lee field, and comparison with analog oil for
OFIP from the Hoffman field, it appears that the primary components mobilized are in the C12-
C23 range, with some slightly heavier components also being mobilized. Lighter hydrocarbons
(C1-C3) are essentially non-existent because of the depleted nature of the field, and it appears
that the heavier hydrocarbons (C30+) are also left behind in the pore spaces of the reservoir.
Paraffin buildup has been observed in this and other fields, indicating the presence of paraffinic
compounds in the in-situ fluids. Based on the observation that C30+ concentration is much
lower in the mobilized oil, it appears that the mobilization of these longer hydrocarbon chains is
being inhibited within the reservoir. This could either be due to pore-throat restrictions in the
matrix porosity systems or paraffin buildup within the depleted reservoir.

Given these observations, it appears that an optimal reservoir fluid for surfactant-enhanced
CO2-EOR in the TBR trend is a lighter oil, containing more medium-weight hydrocarbons and

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              242

minimal heavy hydrocarbon molecules (paraffins/asphaltenes) that are resistant to mobilization
by surfactant/CO2. Most of the oils in this trend are lighter (API of 38+), likely containing many of
these medium-weight hydrocarbons that appear to be preferentially mobilized. If a field
containing a fluid that exhibited minimal paraffin issues during primary production could be
identified, this would likely present an optimal candidate for a surfactant-enhanced CO2 flood.
Given that some amount of oil was mobilized in the Lee field, a history of paraffin buildup should
not preclude the viability of a reservoir from this style of EOR treatment.

It must be noted that data is limited on Lee reservoir fluid from field testing due to minimal
production of in-situ fluids during the test. While conclusions can be drawn from the single
analysis performed, additional data (particularly time-series compositional data) would provide a
much stronger basis for recommended oil properties. The lack of baseline oil composition from
the Lee field does create challenges in interpreting the optimal fluid characteristics for a flood of
this nature. However, the Hoffman oil appears to provide a solid analog to Lee field discovery
fluids based on all available data, increasing confidence that the compositional trends observed
between the two oil samples are indeed reflective of fluid interactions occurring within the
reservoir.

 3. Required Surface Infrastructure

Surface infrastructure required for a CO2-EOR project includes pipelines to transport CO2 to the
field, distribution lines to deliver CO2 to the individual injection wells, wellhead equipment for
injection and productions wells, equipment for artificial lift of fluids from the well, distribution
lines to deliver produced fluids to separators, CO2 recycling equipment and compressors,
storage tanks for produced oil for transport to refineries, and storage tanks for produced water
for transport to disposal sites. The generalized block-flow diagram in Figure 230 shows how
these equipment interconnect at a typical CO2-EOR site.

Generally, the most economic method to transport large quantities of CO2 is via pipeline
(Carbon Storage Leadership Forum, 2021; Svensson et al., 2004). Several CO2-emitting
facilities exist close the oilfields of interest in this study (Table 50). These include industrial
facilities with relatively pure streams of CO2 (ethanol) and industrial and electric power facilities
with more dilute streams of CO2 (hydrogen plants and power plants). The distance from these
nearby sources to the fields of interest is also shown. These sources were used, in conjunction
with forecasted CO2 demand, to develop a price of capture for the economic analyses
completed for the project.

Once the CO2 arrives at the oil field, the CO2 must be delivered to individual injection wells
using a distribution network. For the purposes of this project, it is assumed that this distribution
network delivers CO2 from the surface facilities (CO2 pipeline inlet and the CO2 recycling plant)
to the injection wells via pipeline. For the purposes of economic and field development

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                      244

modeling, it is assumed a single injection well is required for every four production wells.
Existing production wells can be converted into injection wells or new injection wells can be
drilled as necessary between production wells. After CO2 is injected and fluids are produced, a
network of gathering lines delivers the fluids to separators. For the purposes of this study, it was
assumed that the separators are co-located with the pipeline inlet and CO2 distribution system.
The separators route oil and water to storage containers for off-site refining and disposal,
respectively. Separated CO2 is routed to the CO2 recycling plant where it is compressed and
distributed back to the injection wells where the cycle repeats.

Siting project infrastructure requires an analysis of existing rights-of-way and potential sensitive
areas (Figure 230). Battelle (2024) provided an accounting of high consequence areas and
other potential project obstacles and developed a routing philosophy to improve safety and limit
project delays related to permitting pitfalls or public opposition. Every project has unique on-the-
ground conditions, permitting potential, and tolerance for the risk of public opposition; however,
Table 51 shows the philosophy proposed by Battelle (2024) utilizing the protective protocol of
the features shown in Figure 231.
