 4. Underground Injection Control (UIC) Permitting and Regulation
Michigan received primacy for the UIC Class II, the class of wells that includes CO2 injections
wells for CO2-EOR, in 2022 (U.S. EPA, 2022). The program is administered by the Michigan
Department of Environment, Great Lakes, and Energy (EGLE). Michigan Administrative Code
R. 324.802 now describes the requirements operators must adhere to in order to drill, convert,
and/or operate an injection well. The full requirements are available in the Michigan
Administrative Code and summarized below:
 1. Notify oil, gas, and injection well permittees and landowners within 1,320 feet of
 the proposed well (herein referred to as the area of interest).
 2. Complete a plat with wells, ownership information, landowners, and nearby water
 supplies within the area of interest.
 3. Submit the completion report and geologic records, per R 324.418(a), and
 borehole records, per R. 324.419(1), for an existing well that is to be converted to
 a Class II well. This well must meet the requirements outlined in R 324.201(1)
                    and R 324.201(2).
 4. Provide plugging records for abandoned wells and construction records for
 existing active wells within the area of interest, including a corrective action plan
 for dealing these penetrations that might become a conduit for fluid migration

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                            247

 from the injection zone to underground sources of drinking water (USDWs).
 5. Create a wellbore diagram for the proposed well or existing well that is to be
 converted to a Class II well and includes the names and top and bottom depths
 of geological formations penetrated that are USDWs.
 6. Provide proof that injection of fluids will not exceed the fracture pressure gradient.
 7. Provide a plugging and abandonment plan.

 The application also provides a plan for operations that details the maximum daily injection rate
 and pressure, information about injected fluids, representative fluid samples, and geologic name
 of injection interval(s) and its separation from the deepest USDW. The application also requires
 that the operator identify faults, major structural features, and other geological and operational
 considerations (e.g., karst, mines, lost circulation zones) that may impact reservoir containment
 or well integrity. The operator is also required to provide a plan to mitigate any impacts to
 containment or well integrity. Finally, the operator is required to submit evidence that the well
 contraction is protective of USDWs.

 The timeline for the Class II application follows the steps outlined in Table 52. Landowners and
 well leaseholders within the Area of Interest must be notified of the application. The timeline for
 permit approval could theoretically be completed within 30 to 60 days of the submission of an
 application if no valid comments or objections are submitted to EGLE by land or leaseholders. If
 valid comments are received, then the application may take up to 120 days to complete, a
 public hearing may be required, and the applicant will have to show compliance with the
 regulations and demonstrate that the operations are not a risk to public safety or USDWs.

 Table 52. Potential timeline for a Class II application in Michigan, including individual
 permitting steps, and the timeline in instances where no valid comments or objections are

 2. This notification, completed by the EGLE supervisor within 10 days of the receipt of the application, will
 include project information that identifies the applicant, location, and depth of the Class II well; notes the
 injection interval depth and formation name; details the maximum injection pressures and rates; and
 solicits comments or objections to be submitted to EGLE within 30 days of the notice.

 Battelle | May 2026                                                                                                         248

Class II injection well construction requirements are promulgated in R. 324.804. These
requirements outline that the well must be constructed to prevent fluid migrations (e.g., with
casing, cement, tubing, packers, etc.) and outlines acceptable testing methods to demonstrate
external mechanical integrity and fluid migration prevention. The rules also state that all casing
annuli must be accessible at the surface, operators must ensure fluids are confined to the
injection zone, and that the injection interval must be at least 500 feet below the lowest USDW
in karst areas.

Prior to receiving authority to inject, a testing program, outlined in R 324.806, must be
undertaken. This program requires mechanical integrity testing. The permittee must inform
EGLE at least five (5) days prior to conducting the test and provide a report and form within 14
days of the testing completion. At this point, the permittee may receive verbal and then written
authorization to inject from the EGLE supervisor.

Operational requirements listed in the regulations include the maximum injection pressure
(calculated using the equation in R 324.807); operational testing requirements (R 324.808);
weekly, monthly, quarterly, and annual monitoring and record keeping and filing (R 324.810);
notification procedures in the even of a loss of mechanical integrity (R 324.811); the process for
requesting and completing temporary abandonment of the Class II well (R 324.812); the
authorization of the EGLE supervisor to require the cessation of Class II injection operations in
the instance of public safety or health or protection of USDWs (R 324.813); and the process for
Class II permit modifications (R 324.815).

Meeting greenhouse gas reporting requirements and qualifying for 45Q tax credits

Greenhouse gas reporting requirements have recently changed due to the recission of Subpart
RR of the U.S. EPA Greenhouse Gas Reporting Program (GHGRP). As a result, the reporting
requirements for a CO2 storage facility and the way in which the 45Q tax credits are claimed
have changed.

Subpart RR established mandatory monitoring, reporting, and verification requirements for
facilities that inject CO₂ underground for long‑term geologic sequestration, and applied all wells
that are used to inject CO2 for long term or permanent storage in geological reservoirs. This
includes all Class VI wells, which are not unlikely to be a part of any development plan for the
TBR trend, but it also applied to EOR wells that expect to permanently store any volume of CO2.
CO2 volumes that could be verified as permanently stored using agreed upon MRV plans would
be eligible to receive 45Q tax credits. Since most economically viable CO2-EOR scenarios for
this trend require the operator to seek 45Q tax credits, this would have applied to most CO2
injection wells in the trend.

The United States Internal Revenue Service (IRS) has indicated that it will provide new
regulations for 45Q tax credits, but in the interim, the IRS has provided guidance on an
alternative method for quantifying and documenting permanent EOR-related CO2 storage via
the International Organization for Standardization (ISO) provision ISO 27916:2019. This
document lays out a framework for quantifying, documenting and verifying permanent CO₂
storage via CO₂‑EOR operations, and provides guidance for verification of the portion of CO₂
that becomes geologically stored. It also allows operators to differentiate between storage of
CO2 from anthropogenic sources (which would qualify for tax credits) and CO2 from natural
sources (which would not qualify for tax credits).

Battelle | May 2026                                                                              249

It is recommended that all operators who intend to undertake CO2-EOR in the TBR trend build
an operations management plan that is consistent with ISO 27916:2019, regardless of whether
they intend to seek 45Q credits.

The state of Michigan does not have separate reporting requirements for greenhouse gas
emissions. Thus, if the changes to GHGRP are finalized, oil and gas producers in Michigan will
no longer be required to report their emissions to the federal or state government.

45Q Tax Credits are the primary way to monetize CO2 saline storage and can provide an
additional source of revenue for CO2-EOR. Originally valued at $20/tonne for saline storage and

$10/tonne for CO2 utilization, subsequent provisions in the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act, the
Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act (also known as the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law), and
the One Big Beautiful Bill Act have incrementally raised the value of the tax credit to $85/tonne
for both permanent saline storage and CO2-EOR for CO2 that is captured from an industrial
facility.

Qualification for the 45Q Tax credits has several steps. Obtaining the credit is contingent on the
injection of CO2 into a well that is qualified under the UIC program administered by the EPA or
the individual states that have obtained primacy (§1.45Q-3[b]). Class VI wells, which are those
that inject CO2 for permanent geological storage, are administered by the U.S. EPA Regional
Offices in most states, except for the six states with primacy over the UIC Class VI program:
Arizona, Louisiana, North Dakota, Texas, West Virginia, and Wyoming. Class II wells, which
include wells that inject CO2 for the purposes of EOR, are largely administered by the states,
including the states of Kentucky and Ohio (U.S. EPA, 2021). Additional requirements related to
the minimum amount of CO2 that must be injected annually,

The next requirement to claim 45Q tax credits is a reporting mechanism. Initially, the law
required the claimant to submit self-certified verification of their permanent storage of CO2 in
accordance with the U.S. EPA GHGRP, Subpart RR (§1.45Q-3[d]). The U.S. EPA, however,
has proposed that the current Subpart RR and other provisions of the GHGRP, be scrapped by
June 2026 (U.S. EPA, 2025). This would potentially leave companies seeking to claim 45Q tax
credits with no mechanism to report their storage to the IRS (Carbon Capture Coalition, 2025).
In response, the IRS released a notice addressing the cancellation and providing an alternative
approach for claiming the 45Q tax credits (Internal Revenue Service [IRS], 2025). The notice
says that if the U.S. EPA has not launched the electronic Greenhouse Gas Reporting Tool (e-
GGRT) for reporting year 2025, provisions set forth in Section 3 of the Guidance, “Safe Harbor
for Calendar Year 2025 Secure Geological Storage”, under sections 3.02(2) and 3.02(3) of the
guidance, will ensure that those wishing to claim the 45Q tax credits comply with reporting
requirements promulgated under 45Q. The guidance states that the Annual Report that is
normally submitted to e-GGRT must instead be submitted to an independent engineer or
geologist for verification. The notice states that the evaluating engineer or geologist must be a
registered professional engineer (PE) or professional geologist (PG) in any state and certify that
the report is accurate and complete according to the reporting rules for 45Q. For CO2-EOR, it
may be preferable to report verified storage according to the provisions set forth in ISO
27916:2019. This route is not affected by the cancellation of Subpart RR.

Finally, the IIJA placed requirements for facilities that to be eligible for the tax credits, they must
adhere to prevailing wage requirements set forth in §1.45-7 and the apprenticeship
requirements set forth in §1.45-8. The wage determination sets forth that the taxpayer must
ensure that “laborers and mechanics employed by the taxpayer or any contractor or

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  250

subcontractor in the construction, alteration, or repair of the facility are paid [the prevailing
wage]” (26CFR§1.45-7). Apprenticeship requirements state that qualified apprentices must be
hired to perform at least 15% of the construction, alteration, or repair work conducted at the
facility (26CFR§1.45-8). The reporting and recordkeeping requirements for the prevailing wage
and apprenticeship requirements are set forth in §1.45-12.
 5. Developing monitoring, reporting, and verification plans
Based on the economic analysis completed in Subtask 8.1, the team believes that most
operators attempting CO2-EOR in the TBR trend will likely benefit from claiming 45Q tax credits.
Projects not claiming 45Q tax credits are subject only to the relatively straightforward MRV
standards set forth by the EPA under 40 CFR Parts 144 and 146, as well as Michigan state-
specific requirements. Since the State of Michigan has primacy for UIC Class 2 injection wells,
MRV is handled through the MiSTAR injection reporting application, and must meet all reporting
requirements as shown in Table 53 (MiSTAR, 2025).

Table 53. Table of MiSTAR reporting requirements for UIC Class II injection wells in the State of
Michigan (EGLE, 2026)

For operators planning to apply for 45Q tax credits, additional MRV measures are needed. MRV
plans are intended to verify the safe, permanent storage of CO2 and account for lifecycle
emissions of greenhouse gases caused by project activities. Specific requirements for the MRV
Plan include:

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                 251

 • The maximum monitoring area (MMA) and active monitoring areas (AMA).
 The MMA typically includes the outer boundaries of any field or subsurface
 reservoir that will undergo CO2 injection, plus a 0.5 mile buffer.
 • Leakage pathway identification and monitoring, which must be able to
 identify any leakage of CO2 from the EOR complex through geological or
 well‑related leakage pathways. Potential pathways include but are not
 limited to:
  Faults and fractures
  Lateral migration out of the reservoir
  Migration due to induced or natural seismicity
  Diffuse leakage through trapping or sealing formations
  Existing Wellbores (including abandoned wells within the MMA)
  New wellbores
  Injection and production equipment
 • Monitoring strategies to detect and quantify surface leakage as well as
 established baselines to help determine surface leakage. These may
 include but are not limited to:
  Visual inspections
  Injection well surveillance with pressure gauges, flow meters,
 temperature gauges, infrared CO2 monitoring, spectroscopy,
 and other strategies.
  Production well surveillance with pressure gauges, flow meters,
 temperature gauges, infrared CO2 monitoring, and other
 strategies.
  Mechanical Integrity Testing (MIT)
 • Variables for the mass balance equation for the lifecycle analysis
 (LCA) to account for equipment leaks, CO2 ventilation, and CO2 in
 produced fluids
 • Well identification numbers, well name, well type, and status for all wells within the
 MMA, and
 • Date to begin data collection for equation included in Subpart RR (Eq. 1)
 Note: 1. Only applicable for CO2-EOR projects

Monitoring plans will be specific to each project and should be clearly thought out prior to project
implementation. Although regulations for some aspects of CO2-EOR MRV planning have
changed in recent years, the Northern Niagaran Pinnacle Reef Trend (NNPRT) CO2 MRV plan
created by Battelle and Core Energy for DOE Project #DE-FC26-05NT42589 provides a
comprehensive handrail for EPA-approved MRV strategies (Mawalker et al., 2020).

10.0 Summary and Outlook on CO2-EOR for the
 Trenton-Black River Trend in Southern Michigan
Over the course of six years of collaboration between Battelle and project partners, all stated
objectives of the program have been completed, including full geological characterization of the
TBR trend, laboratory and modeling experiments to determine the optimum composition and
design of CO2-EOR operations in the TBR trend, execution of a field test of chemically-
enhanced CO2 in a TBR well, and integration of the data and learnings gathered during these
efforts into a full-trend development plan.

Detailed reporting on these activities, their outcomes, and implications for trend-wide
development is provided in the preceding sections and the accompanying appendices and
attachments. This report and encompassed data will provide TBR field operators with detailed
information on what worked, what did not work, and how to proceed with production optimization
of their TBR assets using chemically-enhanced CO2-EOR.

CO2-EOR is a relatively well understood and broadly implemented strategy for increasing
incremental production across the oil and gas industry, but its application has been primarily
focused on reservoirs with limited heterogeneity. The intention of this project was show first that
the same physical mechanisms that improve recovery factors in homogeneous reservoirs
(namely wettability alteration, viscosity alteration, oil swelling, and mobility control) are at play in
heterogeneous reservoirs. This was proven by the project’s laboratory studies and dynamic
simulations, with the potential exception of mobility control, which needs further study. The
second intention was to demonstrate via direct field testing that CO2-EOR can work in a strongly
heterogeneous reservoir. While the field test strategy implemented during this project did not
succeed in producing oil, data gathered during the test sheds light on what may work for field
operators who try chemically-enhanced CO2-EOR within their own reservoirs, significantly
reducing the level of uncertainty carried by first-of-a-kind commercial efforts that could (and
should) follow this test. Simultaneously, the project has identified several large-volume ethanol
plants and other sources of CO2 emissions in the region and provided a handrail that CO2
emitters and field operators can leverage to capture, transport, and inject that CO2 into their
fields. This project has also shown that, in many cases, the economics of CO2-EOR in the TBR
are attractive. And finally, by completing a project of this scope in the southern Michigan Basin,
the project has contributed to the knowledge base and operational experience of field operators,
state regulatory agencies, local service companies, and state universities, with CO2-EOR
projects which should allow follow-on projects to proceed safely and efficiently.

The outlook for chemically-enhanced CO2-EOR in Trenton-Black River hydrothermal dolomite
trend is good. Some challenges still remain, but opportunities for growth are clear and the
pathway toward successful deployment is relatively straightforward. CO2-EOR can be a central
part of regional CO2 utilization strategies over the coming years and can be a point of leadership
for the State of Michigan in the regional and national CCUS landscape.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                                  253

Attachment A: Advanced Field Characterization and
Machine Learning Based Data Integration, Task 2 Final
Report
Full Task 2 topical report included as separate Attachment A (https://doi.org/10.2172/3367193).

The U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) has a long history of supporting research in advancing
technology and methodologies to improve enhanced oil recovery (EOR). Although EOR is not
uncommon, extensive studies stem from the Permian Basin, which may not be applicable to
other major plays/basins. Additionally, there are extensive, historically produced plays that have
not been explored using EOR or new techniques. There is a critical gap in applying new EOR
methodologies to reservoirs/plays outside of the Permian Basin and in historical fields. This
FOA addresses AOI 1B to DOE: “enhanced oil recovery technologies for conventional
resources in un-tested reservoirs/plays.”

The primary objective of the proposed research is to carry out a comprehensive laboratory
experiment, computer modeling, and field testing-based evaluation of chemically enabled CO2-
EOR in the Southern Michigan Basin (MB)—with a focus on the conventional Trenton/Black
River (TBR) play. This work will be conducted in the complex multi-porosity (i.e., matrix, vuggy,
and fracture porosities) and/or hydrothermally altered dolomite (HTD) reservoirs developed
along fault systems. These reservoirs are especially challenging for EOR due to
heterogeneities, compartmentalization, and presence of thief zones, and as a result, there is a
heightened need to customize the CO2 injection-program for satisfactory incremental oil
recovery. Injection of pure CO2 alone may not be effective in recovering stranded oil. These
challenges will be addressed through advanced field characterization, integrated physics-based
machine learning (ML) and data analytics, laboratory process development, and optimized field
tests. The research concept involves the integration of multiple data types to evaluate fields in
the study area to determine those with the lowest technical and environmental risk and optimal
setting for EOR. Laboratory experiments will be used to optimize a CO2 flood specific to HTD
rock properties and subsequently design and simulate injection scenarios that offer wettability
alteration, foaming, and reduced surface tension. This approach will improve oil recovery from
matrix porosity and mitigate the impact of fracture zones. Results will provide strategies to
improve oil recovery in complex carbonate formations and development plans for historical
plays, thus increasing the likelihood of the application of EOR methods for similar reservoirs and
numerous operators across a wide range of reservoirs and fields.

The Advanced Field Characterization and Machine Learning Based Data Integration task (Task
2) involved a systematic geologic characterization of the TBR play in the SMB which included
the development of comprehensive datasets, advanced data analytics, risk assessment, and
piggyback field characterization. These activities aimed to address the complex carbonate
systems by evaluating the extent of fractures, dolomitization, facies, and reservoir properties
with the primary objective of informing the static and dynamic modeling,
field injection test, and providing input into the development strategy plan. The task was divided
into four subtasks:
 • Subtask 2.1 – Data compilation, review, and analysis

Battelle | May 2026                                                                            259

 • Subtask 2.2 – Risk Assessment
 • Subtask 2.3 – Advanced Field Characterization
 • Subtask 2.4 – Integrated Physics-Based Machine Learning and Advanced Data
 Analytics

Several activities were planned and executed under Task 2 to meet the project objectives.
Figure A-1 illustrates the flow of results between subtasks, which will lead to the development of
a static earth model (Task 4).

Figure A-1. Workflow illustrating the flow of results from data compilation and analysis to
machine learning and static earth modeling.

Data Compilation, Review, and Analysis
Data was compiled for the TBR play for three fields: Albion-Scipio (location of the field injection
test), Stoney Point, and Napoleon. Together, this included information for more than 900 wells
with more than 7,600 wireline logs curves and 48 wells with core data. The data ranged in age,
vendor, and type creating challenges in integration and analysis. Workflows were established to
digitize, quality check, correct, and normalize the data to generate a higher quality database.
Additionally, advanced data was collected through the processing of existing image logs and
high-resolution scanning of existing whole core to characterize important reservoir features.
Results included:

 • Fractures and vugs were commonly observed in dolomitic samples
 • Porosity ranged from 0.1-20.7%, with dolostone having the highest average
 • Permeability ranged from 0.01-939 mD, with dolostone having the highest average

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             260

The 3D seismic data over the Albion-Scipio field was analyzed by project partners which include
well tie analysis, horizon mapping, attribute analysis, and fault and fracture identification. Three
attributes were identified as potential attributes to link to petrophysical work, including RMS,
instantaneous frequency, and variance. More than 50 faults were identified in the volume which
included a few large faults with numerous smaller faults/fractures.

The petrophysical analyses yielded a set of computed logs to represent the effective porosity
and permeability of the reservoir. The data was used to compute a set of attributes for each well
including average wireline log properties (porosity and permeability), facies thicknesses
(dolomite, limestone, cap dolomite), and reservoir attributes (net thickness, porosity feet, and
net to gross ratio). Results included:

 • Stoney Point field had the thickest cap dolomite while the Albion-Scipio
 field had the greatest summed occurrence of dolomite.
 • Log measured porosity and computed permeability was highest in the Napoleon field
 • Zone attributes (net thickness, porosity feet, and net to gross ratio)
 were highest in the Napoleon field, followed by the Albion-Scipio
 field
The advanced data (image logs and CT scans) were used to develop a set of flags and
indicators for vugs and fractures. The features were characterized to determine classification,
dip, azimuth, and frequency of occurrence. Results included:
 • The samples were dominated by small to large mesopores (1/16mm-
 4mm) with larger pores (megapores) due to secondary porosity (Vugs
 and fractures)
 • Numerous vugs and fractures were identified with up to 20% secondary porosity

 Advanced Field Characterization

The McCann 1-20 well was drilled in late 2020 to collect modern data, characterize the injection
site, and develop a production history. The wireline log data analysis identified three zones of
interest due to the presence of enhanced porosity with vugs and fractures:
 • 3,840-3,845 ft- dolomitic with bedded vugs, but water saturated
 • 3,851-3,855 ft- dolomitic and highly fractured
 • 3,863-TD ft- dolomite with vugs and fractures, oil saturated

 Integrated Physics-Based Machine Learning and Advanced Data Analytics

All the databases and types were integrated into a single dataset, which required the use of
advanced data analysis techniques to match and merge appropriately. The purpose of the
integrated database was to 1) identify electrofacies, 2) develop permeability prediction models,
and 3) develop vug and fracture prediction models.

Cluster analysis techniques were applied to the wireline log dataset to identify how the data
naturally clustered into groups. The resulting clusters were compared with geologic
interpretations to determine geologic meaning and importance of each cluster. Six clusters were
identified which represented high porosity dolomite, low to medium porosity dolomite, non-
reservoir limestone, and shale.
Core measured porosity and permeability data, along with wireline log data were explored to
develop permeability predictions. Due to the heterogeneity of the carbonate reservoir, a

Battelle | May 2026                                                                             261

permeability model could not be accurately fit. Alternatively, a relationship was established
between porosity and permeability for each electrofacies to provide a permeability estimate for
modeling purposes.
The vug and fracture flags were evaluated with the wireline log data to determine if a predictive
model could be developed for the presence of the features. Unfortunately, the vugs and
fractures could not be predicted with the wireline log database. The database yielded important
information about feature frequency and characteristics which will be used to develop a 3D
fracture model.

 Risk Assessment

A series of risk assessments were designed to evaluate the planned injection site and
surrounding areas which included an evaluation of existing wellbores, geohazards, caprock
assessment, and a risk screening-based analysis and activity-based “what if” analysis. A
systematic evaluation of the wellbores was used to identify any potential leakage risks based on
construction records, plugging records, and cement bond logs. Overall, there were few wells
that had insufficient information within the Albion-Scipio field, but none within proximity to the
injection site. The evaluation of geohazards and caprocks showed there were no red flags in the
area and system. Additionally, the other analyses showed the only risks were those that were
typical with oil and gas operations.

Overall, this task developed a comprehensive and integrated database which captured the
variability of the complex carbonate reservoir. Moving forward, efforts will be focused on
analyzing data in 3-dimensions to develop a representative static earth model, which includes
changes in lithology, facies, and secondary porosity features. Additionally, key field and pool
characteristics will be identified to be used in a comparative study to aid in the scale-up
assessment and development strategy plan.

Battelle | May 2026                                                                           262

Attachment B: UT Austin Laboratory Experiments to
Optimize CO2-EOR Fluids for Complex Carbonate
Reservoirs
Full report of laboratory work is included as Attachment B to this final report. This work was
completed by Dr. Kishore Mohanty and associated faculty and students at UT Austin.

Task 3 of the project was conducted at The University of Texas at Austin. It consisted of core
characterization, MMP determination, CO2 flood, foam flood and compositional simulation. CT
scanning showed that the cores are heterogeneous and have a complex pore geometry.

Mercury porosimetry showed a dual pore size distribution from nanometers to 100 microns. The
relative permeability of oil can be fitted to a Corey expression, but not the water relative
permeability. These cores are oil-wet. Slim tube experiments were conducted for reservoir oil
with CO2. The MMP for CO2 with Michigan oil was 2100psi at 40°C. The MMP for CO2 with
Napoleon oil was 1300psi at 40°C. The MMP for CO2 with McCann live oil was 1300psi at 40°C.
Additives ME-1 and ME-2 cannot contribute to an improvement in miscibility performance
between Michigan oil and CO2. Continuous CO2 flooding with 0.5 PV CO2 injection could
improve 14.4% OOIP over water flooding. WAG injection could help control CO2 mobility and
increase the oil recovery to 20.1% OOIP. Foam stability tests and contact angle tests were
conducted to screen foam agents. And CO2 foam core flooding experiment was carried out to
evaluate the effectiveness of foam flooding in reservoir core samples. CETAC-30 can generate
better foam and can tolerate high salinity. Also, it can alter the wettability from oil-wet to water-
wet. Soloterra 843 is not a good foaming agent at reservoir conditions and performed worse at
high salinity. LMDO can generate the most stable foam among these three surfactants.

However, it cannot alter the rock wettability. CO2-foam core flooding by using surfactant CETAC-
30 can increase oil recovery by 24% OOIP over the water flooding. This is 9.6% higher oil
recovery compared with CO2 flooding and 4% additional oil recovery over the WAG flooding.
Compositional simulations showed that introduction of 10%-20% rich gas can significantly
decrease MMP between crude oil and CO2.

Introduction
Task 3 of the project was conducted at The University of Texas at Austin. It consisted of core
characterization, MMP determination, CO2 flood, foam flood, and simulations. The results of
these subtasks are described below.
Task 3.1 Core Characterization
The objective of this task is to describe characteristics of reservoir core samples by a series of
laboratory tests. First, CT-scanning was applied to identify the presence of vugs and fractures
within the core samples. Following this, two core samples were used to determine pore size
distribution and capillary pressure by mercury porosimetry. After that, one composite core
mount was used to determine the oil and water relative permeabilities by the unsteady state
method. Then imbibition tests were performed to determine the wettability of these core
samples.
3.1.1 Core Samples
Thirteen core samples with a diameter of 1.5 inches were obtained from the Napoleon field.
The basic information of these core samples is shown in Table 3.1.1. These core samples have
a high-level of heterogeneity and complex multi-porosity regions, including many vugs and
fractures. The air permeabilities of these core samples were measured under different pressure
drops. The results are shown in Figure 3.1.1, which indicates that the permeability range of
these core samples is very wide, from several millidarcy to thousands of millidarcy.

3.1.2 CT-Scan
Core sample #6-44 was CT-scanned to identify its vug and fracture content, utilizing Micro-
CT technology with a resolution of 34.632μm. Figure 3.1.2 shows the CT image of the core
cross section and Figure 3.1.3 displays a 3D CT image of the core sample. These images reveal
numerous small and large vugs in the core sample, and no obvious presence of fractures.
Notably, the largest pore size measures approximately 11.1mm, which is shown in Figure 3.1.4.

3.1.3 Mercury Porosimetry
Mercury porosimetry was conducted on two core samples from Napoleon field, #1-40 and #6-
41. The average air permeability of #1-40 was 34.6mD and the average air permeability of #6-
41 was 3.7mD. Intrusion volume of mercury versus applied pressure were obtained, and the
pressures were converted into pore throat sizes.
Figure 3.1.5 shows the mercury porosimetry curves for core sample #1-40, including the
cumulative intrusion and incremental intrusion versus pore throat diameter. It indicates that
core sample 1-40 has a dominant pore throat size, 14.97μm. For the rest of pores, it has another
dominant pore throat size, 80.87μm, which might be attributed to microfractures or vugs
present in the core sample. Figure 3.1.6 shows the mercury porosimetry curves for core sample
#6-41. It has one dominant pore throat size, 26.96μm, which means core sample #6-41 is more
homogeneous than core sample #1-40. The largest pores connected to the surface of core
sample #1-40 and # 6-41 are very close, which are 180.38μm and 180.54μm respectively.
From mercury porosimetry results, the capillary pressure curve can be obtained. Figure 3.1.7
shows the capillary pressure versus mercury saturation for these two core samples. These two
core samples have the same threshold pressure, which is 1.0 psi. The capillary pressure curve
of #6-41 is flatter than that of #1-40, which means that core sample #6-41 has more uniform
pores.

Figure 3.1.6 Mercury porosimetry curves for core sample #6-41

 Figure 3.1.7 Capillary pressure curves for core sample #1-40 and #6-41

Mercury porosimetry was also conducted on two core samples from well Rowe A-2 and well
Nerreter, #R11 and #N11, with the permeability of 0.1mD and 0.15mD respectively.
Figure 3.1.8 shows the mercury porosimetry curves for core sample #R11, including the
cumulative intrusion and incremental intrusion versus pore throat diameter. It indicates that
core sample R11 has two dominant pore throat sizes, 23.26μm and 0.07μm, which were
attributed to microfractures or vugs and matrix respectively. Figure 3.1.9 shows the mercury
porosimetry curves for core sample #N11. It also has two dominant pore throat sizes, 105.49μm
and 0.009μm, and has more dispersed pore throat size distribution. For these two core samples,
the extremely low matrix pore throat sizes also reveal the ultra-low permeabilities.

3.1.4 Oil/Water Relative Permeability Test
One composite core mount was used to determine oil and water relative permeabilities by the
unsteady state method. The core mount was composed of three core samples, #1-54, #1-45, and
#6-48. The properties of this core mount are shown in Table 3.1.3. Its permeability was 53mD,
which was close to the reservoir average permeability. The properties of crude oil and
formation brine are shown in Table 3.1.4 and Table 3.1.5, respectively.
In the unsteady state method, the core mount was first saturated with the crude oil at irreducible
water saturation and aged at 100°C for two weeks. After that, crude oil was injected into the
core mount at several different flow rates to calculate the oil relative permeability at irreducible
water saturation. Then, formation brine was injected into the core mount at a constant flow rate
of 0.15ml/min (5ft/day) to displace crude oil. The effluents coming from the core mount and
the pressure difference between the inlet and outlet were monitored during the process. Finally,
the water relative permeability at the residual oil saturation was calculated.
The oil and water relative permeabilities were estimated from experimental data by the Johnson,
Bossler, and Naumann (JBN) method. The calculated results are shown in Table 3.1.6. Figure
3.1.10 shows the oil-water relative permeability curves from the unsteady state experiment. It
should be noted that the relative permeability curves are obtained over the limited saturation
range of 0.458≤Sw≤0.64. The relative permeability curves between Swirr = 0.298 and Sw =
0.458 cannot be obtained from the unsteady state method. The Corey-correlation (Eq. (1) and
Eq. (2)) is used to match the experimental results. The parameters in Corey-correlation are
shown in Table 3.1.6 and the Corey type relative permeability curves are shown in Figure 3.1.8.
The oil relative permeability curve matches very well; however, the water relative permeability
curve match is worse.

3.1.5 Imbibition Tests
Imbibition tests were conducted using two core samples, #1-31 and #6-44. The properties of
these two core samples are shown in Table 3.1.8. The procedure commenced by saturating the
core samples with formation brine, followed by flushing with crude oil until irreducible water
saturation was achieved. Subsequently, the core samples were aged at 100°C for two weeks.
Following this, core samples were immersed in formation brine at reservoir temperature (40°C)
and the volume of oil displaced was measured.
Figure 3.1.11 shows the oil recovery with time. After 15 days, the oil recovery of core sample
#1-31 was 3.06%, and the oil recovery of core sample #6-44 was 5.58%. These two core
samples can be considered as oil-wet because of the low imbibition oil recovery. Also, the core
sample with the higher permeability had a higher imbibition oil recovery compared to the core
sample with the lower permeability.

                  Diameter (cm)                  3.81               3.81

3.1.6 Conclusions

 CT scanning showed that the cores are heterogeneous and have a complex pore geometry.
 Mercury porosimetry showed a dual pore size distribution from nanometers to 100 microns.
 The relative permeability of oil can be fitted to a Corey expression, but not the water
 relative permeability.
 The cores are oil-wet.

 13

Task 3.2: MMP Determination
Minimum miscibility pressure (MMP) is one of the most crucial parameters in any gas injection
project. It is defined as the lowest pressure at which the injected gas and the reservoir oil
become miscible through a multi-contact process at the reservoir temperature. CO2 floods are
efficient if conducted at pressures at or above MMP where CO2 develops multi-contact
miscibility with the oil. Slim tube experiments were conducted to determine MMP for various
reservoir oils. The effects of CO2 additives were also studied by slim tube experiments.
3.2.1 Methodology

Material and Conditions
(1) Experimental fluids
Displacing phase: pure CO2
Displaced phase: three different kinds of oils were used to determine MMP. The properties of
crude oils are shown in Table 3.2.1. Michigan oil and Napoleon oil were tested as dead oils,
while McCann oil was a live oil, having been pre-saturated with methane.

 Table 3.2.1 Properties of crude oils
 Crude oil Michigan oil Napoleon oil McCann live oil
      Gravity (API°)                  37.5                 42.6                40.8
    Viscosity (25°C, cp)              14.7                  5.5                 8.5
    Viscosity (40°C, cp)               8.3                 4.25                6.05
    Density (25°C, g/ml)              0.83                0.805                0.82
 Molecular weight
                                     201.3                202.21                215.75
 (g/mol)
       GOR (scf/bbl)                   --                    --                  220

(2) Slim tube:
A 20 ft coiled slim tube was used for the experiments to determine MMP. The tube was made
of stainless steel with an inner diameter of 0.26 in, thickness of 0.063 in, and was packed with
100-150 mesh sand. The porosity was 37.4%.
(3) Temperature: 40°C
(4) Pressure: 1000psi - 2300psi
Experimental equipment
Figure 3.2.1 is the schematic of the set up for slim tube experiment, which includes:
(1) a 20 ft long slim tube
(2) three ISCO pumps: two are used to displacing fluids and the third one is used for back
pressure
(3) two accumulators which contains oil and CO2 respectively
(4) one pressure transducer is used to monitor pressure drop
(5) one view cell is used to observe oil-CO2 phase behavior
(6) one back pressure regulator (BPR) to control the experiment pressure
(7) an effluent collector
 14

(8) two cameras: one is used to monitor the view cell and the other one is used to monitor the
collector.

 Pressure
 Transducer

 Coil

 CO2
 Oil

 View Cell BPR

 ISCO PUMP ISCO PUMP

Figure 3.2.1. Schematic of the set up for MMP determination apparatus using slim tube

Experimental procedure
(1) To guarantee that the porous medium is clean and dry before each run, 2 pore volumes of
toluene is injected at a rate of 0.5 cm3/min followed by 2 PV of supercritical CO2, and then the
coil is subjected to vacuum. During that stage, the absence of leaks is verified with the
assistance of a vacuum gauge.
(2) The temperature is set to the reservoir condition (40°C).
(3) Crude oil is injected at a rate of 1 cm3/min while gradually increasing the back pressure
until the desired test pressure is reached. After the stabilization of pressure and temperature, 1
PV of the dead crude oil is injected to guarantee the system is fully saturated.
(4) 1.2 PV of CO2 is pumped at a rate of 0.018 cm3/min (6.5ft/day) to displace the oil. The oil
recovery and pressure drop are monitored.
(5) The test pressure is set to various values (1000psi - 2300psi) in separate experiments and
the steps (1)-(4) are repeated.
The resulting recovery factors are plotted as a function of pressure. Two regions are identified.
Firstly, the ‘immiscible region’ is characterized by recovery factors that exhibit a strong
dependency on pressure variation. Secondly, the ‘miscible region’, where the recovery factor
either approaches or exceeds 95% and exhibits a weak function of pressure. The MMP is
determined as the point of intersection between these two regions.
3.2.2 Results

MMP determination for reservoir oils
(1) Michigan oil
Slim tube experiments were conducted with Michigan oil at seven different pressures and the
oil recoveries at 1.2 PVI are shown in Table 3.2.5. The 1.2 PVI recovery factors are plotted as
 15

a function of pressure in Figure 3.2.2. The oil recovery increased with pressure. The first five
oil recoveries (1000psi-2000psi) were lower than 95%. However, the last two recoveries
(2100psi and 2300psi) surpassed 95% with a slight difference. Therefore, MMP was
determined as 2100psi.

 Table 3.2.5 Oil recovery at different pressures (Michigan oil)
 Pressure (psi) Oil recovery (%)
 1000 66.4
 1500 76.9
 1750 86.9
 1900 89.4
 2000 93.9
 2100 97.4
 2300 97.9

 Figure 3.2.2 The relationship between oil recovery and pressure (Michigan oil)

(2) Napoleon oil
Slim tube experiments were conducted with Napoleon oil at three different pressures, and the
corresponding oi recovery factors are presented in Table 3.2.6 and Figure 3.2.3. At 1100psi, the
oil recovery was 78.3%, which is below 95%, indicating an immiscible flooding. At 1300psi,
the oil recovery notably improved to 93.4%. Increase of pressure to 1500psi resulted in an oil
recovery of 94.6%, which was close to the previous one. Therefore, the MMP for Napoleon oil
and CO2 is approximately 1300psi at 40°C.

 Table 3.2.6 Oil recovery at different pressures (Napoleon oil)
 Pressure (psi) Oil recovery (%)
 1100 78.2
 1300 93.4
 1500 94.6
 16

 Figure 3.2.3 The relationship between oil recovery and pressure (Napoleon oil)

(3) McCann live oil
Slim tube experiments were performed using McCann live oil, composed of McCann dead oil
combined with methane at a Gas-Oil Ratio (GOR) of 220 scf/bbl. These experiments were
conducted at four pressures, and the resulting oil recovery factors are shown in Table 3.2.7 and
graphically represented in Figure 3.2.4. At 1100psi, the oil recovery was 68%, indicating an
immiscible flooding. At higher pressures (1300psi, 1500 psi, and 1700 psi), the oil recovery
was higher than 95%, indicating a shift to a miscible flooding. Consequently, the MMP for
McCann live il and CO2 was determined as 1300psi.

 Figure 3.2.4 The relationship between oil recovery and pressure (McCann live oil)

The effects of additives
For the target reservoir, the reservoir pressure is lower than the MMP. It has been proposed in
the past that the miscibility of CO2 and crude oil can be improved by solubilizing an appropriate
additive in the CO2.
Two additives from Sasol Company, ME-1 and ME-2, were tested for lowering MMP. The
properties of these two additives are listed in Table 3.2.8. They have high flash point, and the
density is higher than 0.9 g/cm3. And ME-2 has a higher viscosity than ME-1. The slim tube
experiments are conducted with Michigan oil and these two additives mixed with CO2
separately.

 Table 3.2.8 The properties of additives
 Flash Point Density @ 20ºC Viscosity @ 20ºC
 Additives
                                 (°C)                (g/cm3)                  (mPa · s)
           ME-1                   133                 0.958                       12
           ME-2                   145                 0.915                       32

Additive ME-1
Four separate slim tube experiments were conducted using ME-1, and their details are listed in
Table 3.2.9. Firstly, a 2wt% concentration of ME-1 was combined with CO2 and injected
together into the slim tube at 1500 psi. The resulting oil recovery, as indicated in Figure 3.2.5,
was 76.4%. In comparison, the oil recovery for the previous experiment utilizing only CO2 at
the same pressure was 76.9%, showcasing a minimal difference of merely 0.5%. Subsequently,
the experimental pressure was increased to 1750psi. The result is shown in Figure 3.2.6. The
oil recovery at 1.2PV was 83.3%, which was lower than the recovery of the case without
additive, 86.9%. This suggested that the addition of 2wt% ME-1 did not enhance the miscibility
performance at 1500psi and 1750psi.
 18

In the next experiment, the ME-1 concentration was increased to 5wt% at 1750psi. The result
is also shown in Figure 3.2.6. For this case, the oil recovery was 83.3% at 1.2PV injection, also
lower than the previous result without additives.
Moreover, exploring a different approach, where 5wt% ME-1 was co-injected with CO2 rather
than pre-mixing. The result shows that the oil recovery was even lower at 75.7% compared to
the pre-mixed additive experiment at 1750psi.
Thess results suggest that the introduction of additive ME-1, weather pre-mixed or co-injected,
did not yield an improvement in oil recovery, thus no improvement in miscibility performance
between oil and CO2.

Additive ME-2
A single slim tube experiment was performed using ME-2 as an additive. 5wt% ME-2 was
combined with CO2 and injected into the slim tube to displace oil. The resulting oil recovery,
shown in Figure 3.2.7, was 84.7%. There is a 2.2% decrease compared to 86.9% recovery
achieved by CO2 flooding without any additives. Hence, the introduction of ME-2 as an
additive did not contribute to an increase in oil recovery at the tested pressure of 1750psi.

 Figure 3.2.7 The oil recovery at 1750psi with or without additive ME-2

3.2.3 Conclusions
Slim tube experiments were conducted for reservoir oil with CO2. The following conclusions
can be drawn from the results.
• The MMP for CO2 with Michigan oil was 2100psi at 40°C.

 20

• The MMP for CO2 with Napoleon oil was 1300psi at 40°C.
• The MMP for CO2 with McCann live oil was 1300psi at 40°C.
• Additives ME-1 and ME-2 cannot contribute to an improvement in miscibility
 performance between Michigan oil and CO2.

Task 3.3: CO2 Core Flood
The objective of this task is to assess the efficiency of CO2 flooding in enhancing oil recovery.
One composite core was mounted in Hassler sleeves horizontally, and tertiary CO2 floods was
conducted at the reservoir temperature and pressure. Subsequently, a 1:1 WAG injection was
conducted to determine any enhancement in oil recovery.
3.3.1 Materials
For CO2 core flooding experiments, synthetic produced water (SPW) was used as water phase,
which has a total salinity of 256,664.9 ppm, with its ion composition detailed in Table 3.3.1.
And Napoleon oil was used as oil phase. The core mount comprised four small core samples:
#1-5, #6-15, #6-43, and #6-42. Table 3.3.2 describes the properties of the core mount. It had an
average permeability of 14 mD and a porosity of 8.6%.

 Table 3.3.1 Synthetic produced water ion composition
 Ions (mg/L) Brine 1
                                    Na+              51,802.1
 K+ 5,286.3
                                   Ca2+              32,697.7
                                   Mg2+               5,898.2
                                    Cl-              156,207.1
                                   SO42-              4,763.5
                                    TDS              256,664.9

 Table 3.3.2 Properties of the core mount for CO2 core floods
                     Properties           Core mount: 1-5, 6-51, 6-43, 6-42
                    Length (cm)                        16.23
 Diameter (cm) 3.81
 Permeability (mD) 14
                    Porosity (%)                         8.6

3.3.2 Methodology
To investigate the effectiveness of CO2 flooding for enhanced oil recovery, core flooding
experiments were conducted at a pressure of 1200 psi and a temperature of 40°C using the
setup shown in Figure 3.3.1. The core samples were horizontally oriented to account for gravity
segregation. To prepare the core samples, they were first vacuumed and then saturated with
SPW. Crude oil was then injected to establish the condition of irreducible water saturation,
after which the core samples were aged at 90°C for two weeks.
After aging, water flooding was carried out first by injecting at least 2 pore volume (PV) of
SPW at a flow rate of 1 ft/day until no oil was produced from the core samples. This was
followed by separate CO2 flooding and WAG flooding tests. For CO2 flooding, 0.5 PV of CO2

 22

was injected at a flow rate of 1 ft/day, followed by a chase water flooding (CWF). For WAG
flooding, 0.5 PV CO2 and 0.5 PV SPW were injected into the core samples alternatively, with
a slug size of 0.05 PV, at a flow rate of 1 ft/day, then followed by a CWF. During the tests, the
pressure difference between the two ends of the core samples was monitored, and the effluent
from the core samples was collected and measured over time.

 Figure 3.3.1 Schematic of the set up for CO2 flooding experiments
3.3.3 Results

Continuous CO2 flooding
Figure 3.3.2 shows the oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop data versus pore
volume injected (PVI) for continuous CO2 flooding. The experimental procedure involved 4
PV of SPW, followed by 0.5 PV of CO2, and then another 1.5 PV of SPW injection. During the
water flooding, breakthrough of water occurred at 0.16 PVI, and a total of 36.5% of the original
oil in place (OOIP) was recovered at the end of water flooding. Subsequently, during the
injection of CO2, breakthrough of CO2 was observed at 0.1 PVI of CO2 injection. At the end of
experiment, the incremental oil recovery was 14.4% OOIP, which was attributed to the multi-
contact miscible behavior of CO2 and oil. Not all the oil was recovered because of the viscous
fingering and high-level heterogeneity of the core sample. The relative pressure drop was
calculated based on the pressure drip generated by single-phase oil flow at the same flow rate
of 1 ft/day. The relative pressure drop during CO2 flooding was found to be small, less than
1psi, indicating that the CO2 tended to bypass the smaller pores in the core samples.

 23

 Figure 3.3.2 Oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop for CO2 flooding
 experiment
WAG flooding
Figure 3.3.3 shows the data on oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop versus pore
volume injected (PVI) during WAG flooding. The water flood was followed by a WAG flood
with the same amount of CO2. The water flood recovered 42.5% OOIP, hither than that in water
flood in CO2 flood. The tertiary WAG flood yielded an additional 20.1% oil, indicating that the
WAG flood could improve oil recovery over the continuous CO2 flood in this core mount.
Moreover, breakthrough of CO2 was observed at 0.25 PVI of WAG injection, and the relative
pressure drop during WAG injection was higher than that of CO2 flooding, which indicated that
WAG could help control CO2 mobility.

 Figure 3.3.3 Oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop for WAG flooding
 experiment

 24

3.3.4 Conclusions
Continuous CO2 flooding with 0.5 PV CO2 injection could improve 14.4% OOIP over water
flooding. WAG injection could help control CO2 mobility and increase the oil recovery to 20.1%
OOIP.

 25

Task 3.4: CO2 Foam Flood
The objective of this task is to develop proper foam formulation, which can alter wettability of
the rock to preferentially water-wet condition. Subsequently, to evaluate the performance of
CO2 foam flooding in the reservoir core samples. To achieve this, foam stability tests and
contact angle tests were carried out to screen foam agents. After that, CO2 foam flooding
experiment was conducted in a core mount using the selected foam formulation.
3.4.1 Materials
A nonionic surfactant, Soloterra 843, was provided by Sasol Chemicals. A cationic surfactant,
CETAC-30, and a zwitterionic surfactant, LMDO, were obtained from Stepan Company. There
are two brines from well SWD and NW1 could be used to make surfactant solution with total
salinities of 30,412.6 ppm and 165,573.7 ppm respectively. The ion composition of these two
brines is shown in Table 3.4.1. Napoleon oil was also used as oil phase in this task. The core
mount used in this study comprised three small core samples: #6-54, #1-45, and #6-48. Table
3.4.2 describes the properties of the core mount. It had an average permeability of 50 mD and
a porosity of 11%.

 Table 3.4.1 Ion composition of two brines used for surfactant solution
 Ions (mg/L) SWDB NW1B
                         Na+               6,400            35,000
                          K+                520              3,100
                        Ca2+               3,700            20,000
                        Mg2+                740              3,900
                          Cl-            19,025.6         103,463.7
                        SO42-                27               110
                         TDS             30,412.6         165,573.7

 Table 3.4.2 Properties of the core mount for CO2 foam core floods
                      Properties            Core mount: 6-54, 1-45, 6-48
                    Length (cm)                        12.16
 Diameter (cm) 3.81
 Permeability (mD) 50
 Porosity (%) 11

3.4.2 Methodology

Foam stability test
The foam static stability tests were performed at the reservoir pressure (1200psi) and
temperature (40°C) to screen the surfactants in two brines. The surfactant concentration was
0.5wt% and the foam quality was 80%. Foam was generated by co-injecting surfactant solution
and CO2 through a 7 μm filter at 2ml/min, as shown in Figure 3.4.1. A high-pressure view cell
with a height of 13 cm was used to collect the foam and the height of foam was measured over
time. Moreover, foam stability tests were carried out in the presence of oil by injecting 1-2 ml
of crude oil into the view cell after the foam was collected. The foam stability was characterized
 26

by the half-life of foam.
Contact angle test
Contact angle tests were conducted to identify if the surfactants could alter the wettability of
the oil-wet rock to water-wet. The Texas Cream carbonate core chips were used in the tests.
First, the core chips were aged in crude oil for two weeks to make them become oil wet. After
that, the core chips were merged in brine and surfactant solutions separately. After 1 day and 2
days, the contact angles were measured by putting a drop of oil below the core chips.
Foam core flooding experiment
To investigate the effectiveness of CO2 foam flooding for enhanced oil recovery, core flooding
experiment was conducted at reservoir conditions using the setup shown in Figure 3.4.1. The
core samples were horizontally oriented to account for gravity segregation. A high-pressure
view cell was used to ensure foam generation before injection into the core samples. The core
samples were vacuumed first and saturated with SPW. Crude oil was injected to establish
irreducible water saturation, after which the core samples were aged at 90°C for two weeks.
Water flooding was conducted first by injecting at least 2 PV of SPW at a flow rate of 1 ft/day
until no oil was produced from the core samples. For CO2 foam flooding, 0.625 PV of foam
was injected at a flow rate of 1 ft/day, followed by a chase water flooding. The injected volume
of CO2 was the same as CO2 and WAG flooding experiments. During the tests, the pressure
difference between the two ends of the core mount was monitored, and the effluent from the
core samples was collected and measured over time.

 Figure 3.4.1 Schematic of the set up for CO2 foam flooding experiment

 27

3.4.3 Results

Foam stability
Foam stability without crude oil
Three surfactants were used to generate CO2 foam in two brines, and the evaluation of foam
over time was presented in Figure 3.4.2 and Figure 3.4.3, respectively. It can be observed that,
in the lower salinity condition (SWDB), half-life of foam generated by 0.5wt% Soloterra 843
was 30 minutes (Figure 3.4.2 (a)). However, as the salinity increased (NW1B), the foam
exhibited extreme instability, failing to fully occupy the view cell (Figure 3.4.3 (a)).
For the foam generated by surfactant CETAC-30, the foam half-life in SWDB was 1.5 hours
(Figure 3.4.2 (b)) and increased to 6 hours in NW1B (Figure 3.4.3 (b)). This observation
suggests that CETAC-30 can tolerate high salinity, generating more stable foam in high salinity
conditions.
For foam generated by surfactant LMDO, the foam half-life in SWDB was 30 hours (Figure
3.4.2 (c)) and decreased to 17 hours in NW1B (Figure 3.4.3 (c)). The results indicate that
LMDO can generate the strongest foam among these surfactants in absence of crude oil,
regardless of brine conditions.

Foam stability with crude oil
Foam stability tests were performed in the presence of crude oil. The evaluation of foam over
time was shown in Figure 3.4.4 and Figure 3.4.5. In the presence of oil, the half-life of foam
generated by Soloterra 843 in SWDB was only 8 minutes (Figure 3.4.4 (a)). The case of NW1B
was not tested because of unstable foam generated. For the foam generated by surfactant
CETAC-30, the foam half-life in the presence of oil was 30 minutes in SWDB (Figure 3.4.4
(b)) and 22 minutes in NW1B (Figure 3.4.5 (a)). The foam generated by LMDO exhibited a
similar performance, with a half-life of 30 minutes in SWDB (Figure 3.4.4 (c)) and 25 minutes
in NW1B (Figure 3.4.5 (b)). These findings reveal that the presence of oil had a negative impact
on foam stability. The difference in half-life time of these surfactants in the presence of oil was
not significant.

 28

 (a) 0.5 wt% Soloterra 843 in SWDB

 (b) 0.5 wt% CETAC-30 in SWDB

 (c) 0.5 wt% LMDO in SWDB
Figure 3.4.2 The evolution of foam generated in SWDB without oil

 29

                   (b) 0.5 wt% CETAC-30 in NW1B

                                 (a) 0.5 wt% CETAC-30 in NW1B

 (b) 0.5 wt% LMDO in NW1B
 Figure 3.4.5 The evolution of foam generated in NW1B with oil
Contact angle
The contact angle tests focused on surfactants CETAC-30 and LMDO, omitting Soloterra 843
due to its limited foam generation capability. Figure 3.4.6 shows the contact angle results
observed after 1 day and 2 days. The core chip merged in produced water remained oil-wet
even after 2 days, evident from the oil drop spreading on the rock surface. The chip merged in
CETAC-30 solution demonstrated a transition: after 1 day, it exhibited an intermediate wet with
a contact angle of 90°, evolving into a water-wet with a contact angle of 70° after 2 days. The
chip merged in LMDO solution displayed a contact angle of 120° after 2 days, indicating a
change in the contact angle but maintaining oil wet.

 32

 Figure 3.4.6 Contact angle tests results
Foam flooding
According to the results of foam stability tests and contact angle tests, CETAC-30 was selected
as foam agent because of its satisfied foam stability and wettability alteration capability. The
CO2 foam core flooding experiment was carried out using 0.5wt% CETAC-30 in SWDB. The
experimental procedure involved injecting 4 PV of SPW, followed by 0.625 PV of foam
injection, and then another 1 PV of chase water flooding. Figure 3.4.7 presents the oil recovery,
water cut, and relative pressure drop data as a function of PVI. Initial water flooding recovered
39.1% OOIP. Subsequently, during the injection of foam, breakthrough of CO2 was observed
at 0.38 PVI, which was later compared to CO2 flooding and WAG flooding. At the end of
experiment, the incremental oil recovery achieved through foam flooding was 24% OOIP,
higher than that of previous two experiments. During the foam flooding, the relative pressure
drops exhibited fluctuations with the highest value of 9. This fluctuated pressure drip indicates
that some CO2 was pushed into smaller pores and displaced oil. Overall, foam flooding with
CETAC-30 can effectively control the CO2 mobility and transfer CO2 into smaller pores,
leading to a later CO2 breakthrough and an increased oil recovery compared to CO2 flooding
and WAG flooding.

 33

 Figure 3.4.7 Oil recovery, water cut, and relative pressure drop for CO2 foam flooding
 experiment

3.4.4 Conclusions
Foam stability tests and contact angle tests were conducted to screen foam agents. And CO2
foam core flooding experiment was carried out to evaluate the effectiveness of foam flooding
in reservoir core samples. The following conclusions can be drawn from the results:
• CETAC-30 can generate better foam and can tolerate high salinity. Also, it can alter the
 wettability from oil-wet to water-wet.
• Soloterra 843 is not a good foam agent at reservoir conditions and performed worse at high
 salinity.
• LMDO can generate the most stable foam among these three surfactants. However, it
 cannot alter the rock wettability.
• CO2- foam core flooding by using surfactant CETAC-30 can increase oil recovery by 24%
 OOIP over the water flooding. This is 9.6% higher oil recovery compared with CO2
 flooding and 4% additional oil recovery over the WAG flooding.

 34

3.5.2 Results
Slim tube simulation of live oil
Slim tube simulations were carried out using live oil and pure CO2 at several different pressures,
and the resulting oil recovery factors are shown in Figure 3.5.1. From the simulation results,
the MMP between live oil and CO2 was identified as 1444psi, the intersection where live oil
and CO2 became miscible. To investigate the effects of introducing rich gas in CO2, separate
simulations were conducted using 10mol% and 20mol% rich gas combined with CO2, and the
corresponding resulting oil recovery factors are shown in Figure 3.5.2 and Figure 3.5.3.
Introducing 10mol% rich gas led to an MMP decrease to 1200psi, while a higher concentration
of 20mol% rich gas resulted in a further reduction to 977psi. These observations indicate that
the introduction of rich gas in CO2 can help decrease the MMP of live oil and higher mole
content of rich gas results in lower MMP.

Slim tube simulation of dead oil
Slim tube simulations were carried out using dead oil and pure CO2 at several different
pressures, and the resulting oil recovery factors are shown in Figure 3.5.4. The MMP between
dead oil and CO2 was identified as 1373psi. To investigate the effects of introducing rich gas
in CO2, separate simulations were conducted using 10mol% and 20mol% rich gas combined
with CO2, and the corresponding resulting oil recovery factors are shown in Figure 3.5.5 and
Figure 3.5.6. Introducing 10mol% rich gas led to an MMP decrease to 1190psi, while a higher
concentration of 20mol% rich gas resulted in a further reduction to 837psi. These observations
indicate that the introduction of rich gas in CO2 can help decrease the MMP of dead oil and
higher mole content of rich gas results in lower MMP.

3.5.3 Conclusions

Compositional simulations showed that introduction of 10%-20% rich gas can significantly
decrease MMP between crude oil and CO2.

3.6 Overall Conclusions
Task 3 of the project was conducted at The University of Texas at Austin. It consisted of core
characterization, MMP determination, CO2 flood, foam flood and compositional simulation.

• CT scanning showed that the cores are heterogeneous and have a complex pore geometry.
 Mercury porosimetry showed a dual pore size distribution from nanometers to 100 microns.
 The relative permeability of oil can be fitted to a Corey expression, but not the water
 relative permeability. These cores are oil-wet.
• Slim tube experiments were conducted for reservoir oil with CO2. The MMP for CO2 with
 Michigan oil was 2100psi at 40°C. The MMP for CO2 with Napoleon oil was 1300psi at
 40°C. The MMP for CO2 with McCann live oil was 1300psi at 40°C. Additives ME-1 and
 ME-2 cannot contribute to an improvement in miscibility performance between Michigan
 oil and CO2.
• Continuous CO2 flooding with 0.5 PV CO2 injection could improve 14.4% OOIP over water
 flooding. WAG injection could help control CO2 mobility and increase the oil recovery to
    20.1% OOIP.
• Foam stability tests and contact angle tests were conducted to screen foam agents. And CO2
 foam core flooding experiment was carried out to evaluate the effectiveness of foam
 flooding in reservoir core samples. CETAC-30 can generate better foam and can tolerate
 high salinity. Also, it can alter the wettability from oil-wet to water-wet. Soloterra 843 is
 not a good foaming agent at reservoir conditions and performed worse at high salinity.
 LMDO can generate the most stable foam among these three surfactants. However, it
 cannot alter the rock wettability.
• CO2-foam core flooding by using surfactant CETAC-30 can increase oil recovery by 24%
 OOIP over the water flooding. This is 9.6% higher oil recovery compared with CO2
 flooding and 4% additional oil recovery over the WAG flooding.
• Compositional simulations showed that introduction of 10%-20% rich gas can significantly
 decrease MMP between crude oil and CO2.

 39

Attachment C: UT Austin Bureau of Economic Geology
Seismic Interpretations of Fractured Trenton-Black River
Carbonate in Southern Michigan
Full report of seismic analysis and interpretation is included as Attachment C to this final report.
This work was completed by Dr. Shuvajit Bhattacharya at the University of Texas Bureau of
Economic Geology.

The primary objective of this project was to improve the understanding of geologic complexity
and reservoir properties in the Trenton and Black River (TBR) formations in the Michigan Basin.
The team’s methodology centered on integrated geophysical analysis using 3D seismic
datasets, well logs, and seismic inversion techniques. 3D seismic datasets from the Albion-
Scipio field and Lee 26 field were interpreted to analyze fault distribution, fracture styles, and
reservoir geometry. Conventional well logs were used to generate synthetic seismograms for
horizon recognition, and post-stack model-based seismic inversion was applied to estimate
acoustic impedance and porosity. The study also included a reconnaissance core analysis at
the Michigan Geological Repository for Research and Education (MGRRE), and filtering
techniques were used to correct porosity estimates in the Utica Shale and the TBR section.

Our results demonstrate that 3D seismic may be able to improve property mapping accuracy in
complex and compartmentalized reservoirs, as 2D seismic cannot capture lateral variability or
localized fault and fracture distribution. Porosity inversion revealed that reservoir-quality HTDs
in the TBR have lower acoustic impedance and higher porosity near fault and fracture zones,
confirming the match between inversion-based and well-log-based measurements. The team
concluded that precise seismic processing and migration are necessary to identify key geologic
features, and that integrated geophysical approaches provide valuable insights for CO2
utilization and storage in Trenton-Black River hydrothermal dolomite reservoirs.

Battelle | May 2026

 264

 Final report on the project DE- FE0031792 on tasks related to “Seismic interpretations of
 fractured Trenton-Black River carbonate in Southern Michigan”
 Shuvajit Bhattacharya
 Bureau of Economic Geology
 The University of Texas at Austin
Introduction
Structurally controlled hydrothermal dolomite (HTD) oftentimes have a good reservoir quality,
which makes them a potential target for CCUS. HTD reservoirs present interesting technical
challenges and opportunities for exploration and development. These reservoirs are commonly
found in Michigan, Texas, western Canada, and other parts of the world (Hart et al., 2009; Saller,
2013; Hurley and Budros, 1990; Jiang et al., 2017). There are a few published studies on the TBR
HTDs in the Michigan Basin (Gregg and Sibley, 1984; Hurley and Budros, 1990; Budai and Wilson,
1991; Grammer and Harrison, 2013; Dunseith et al., 2018; Bhattacharya et al., 2022; Haagsma et
al., 2022; Bhattacharya et al., 2023).
Sags associated with HTD reservoirs were recognized in the 1930s, for the Ordovician Dover
(Trenton) field in southwestern Ontario (Burgess, 1960, reported in Hurley and Budros, 1990;
Davies and Smith, 2006). The Dover field is considered as a structurally controlled HTD reservoir,
with high correlation of productive wells to sags at the top Trenton level (Davies and Smith,
2006). Although structural sags as well as amplitude dimming associated with structurally
controlled HTD reservoirs can be identified on 2D seismic sections, 3D seismic technology can
greatly improve defining and mapping the full geometry of these complex and
compartmentalized reservoirs.
We present the results from integrated 3D seismic-assisted geophysical interpretations of the
Trenton and Black River (TBR) formations from two oil fields (Albion-Scipio and Lee) in the
Michigan Basin, United States (Figure 1). As the injection well drilled within the Albion-Scipio field
was not suitable for the project, the Lee field was selected as the next field site for further
experiments and interpretations. We interpret two 3D seismic datasets from each field to analyze
fault distribution and styles, and integrate it with the image log and deep shear log-based analysis
and acoustic impedance from seismic inversion.

 1

Figure 1: Approximate locations of the TBR fields in Southern Michigan (modified after
Haagsma et al., 2022).
Lessons learned
 • 2D seismic cannot capture the lateral variability of complex HTD reservoirs, localized
 fracture, and fault distribution. Therefore, 3D seismic is critical to better understand the
 geologic complexity and map the reservoirs.
 • Seismic data needs to be processed and migrated accurately near sags associated with
 negative flower structures, carbonate dissolution and diagenesis. Otherwise, these
 features would be missed on the stack image.
 • Sparsely-distributed wells with vintage logs are helpful at the exploration stage; however,
 significant processing needs to be done and compare them with modern logging data for
 accurate reservoir characterization.
 • Acquisition, comparison, and integration of core, image logs, and far-field multi-
 component sonic logs (e.g., deep shear) in the same borehole are helpful to interpret,
 validate, and map sub-seismic-scale fractures. Many fractures (excluding tensile
 fractures) present at the core scale are not always observed on both image logs and deep
 shear logs and vice versa.
 • Operational challenges may hinder collecting good-quality data, for example, some of the
 multi-component acoustic tools (e.g., deep shear) require the borehole to be filled with
 fluid, which may not always be possible and adds to the operational costs and time.
 • Access to both pre-stack and post-stack seismic is critical for further investigation of
 reservoir complexities. This also helps if there is a need for reprocessing the dataset to
 remove scattered noise from glacial boulders, unwanted noise and multiples from
 carbonates, etc.
 • Machine learning models utilizing seismic data for rock property predictions do not
 always work in in complex geology, example, fractured carbonates with little velocity

 2

 variations. Better quality input data (e.g., 3D seismic) with high signal-to-noise ratio and
 reprocessing of vintage seismic are needed before deploying machine learning tools. In
 such cases, physics-based seismic inversion is the recommended approach.

3D seismic dataset features
We used two 3D seismic datasets from the Albion-Scipio and Lee fields. The general 3D
acquisition features are shown in Table 1.
