3D seismic attributes and seismic inversion
We used several 3D seismic attributes and seismic inversion process to better understand the
reservoir complexity of the TBR interval. Seismic attributes are mathematical quantities derived
from seismic data using a set of equations. There are several types of seismic attributes,
including complex trace attributes and geometric attributes. RMS amplitude, instantaneous
phase, and frequency attributes are complex trace attributes. These attributes are useful to
visualize the change in reflection characteristics in the study area. The changes could be related
to lithology, porosity, etc. The geometric and waveform attributes, such as curvature and
variance can reveal the structural (e.g., faults) and stratigraphic complexity (e.g., channels). In
addition, ant track can be computed to automatically extract faults from seismic data. The
definitions and applications of a few widely used attributes are below:
RMS amplitude attribute: The root mean square amplitude (RMS) is a commonly used
technique to display amplitude values in a window of interest. With this attribute amplitude,
hydrocarbon indicators can be mapped by measuring reflectivity.
Instantaneous frequency attribute: It is the time rate of change of the instantaneous phase. This
attribute is a good indicator of low impedance beds, fracture zones, hydrocarbon
accumulations, etc.
Variance attribute: It measures the similarity of waveforms or traces adjacent over given lateral
and/or vertical windows. Therefore, it can image discontinuity of seismic data related faulting
or stratigraphy.
For more information on seismic attributes, please see Chopra and Marfurt (2007).

 3

Apart from seismic attributes, we performed seismic inversion. Seismic inversion is a process of
inverting the seismic amplitude traces to acoustic impedance. Acoustic impedance is more
characteristic of the changes in rock properties than regular seismic amplitude. Essentially, it is
the multiplication of acoustic velocity and density of the rocks. Inversion process requires
multiple iterations to reduce the error between the original data and synthetic data. In terms of
seismic inversion, we used a model-based post-stack inversion process. Workflows for such
inversion are shown in Figure 2 and 3.

Figure 2: Basics of seismic inversion process (after Maurya and Singh, 2018)

Figure 3: Workflow on impedance estimates from seismic inversion (Karim et al., 2016)

 4

Albion-Scipion dataset analysis
We use a 3D seismic dataset and well log data from four wells in the Albion-Scipio Field. Several
well logs, including triple-combo, dipole sonic, image logs, and deep shear wave logs, were
collected from one new well, which was drilled as a part of the DOE project. We also conducted
a reconnaissance Trenton-Black River core study at the Michigan Geological Repository for
Research and Education (MGRRE).
We use one of the wells for synthetic seismogram and recognizing formations on seismic data
(Figure 4). Conventional well logs from a well were used to generate a synthetic seismogram to
recognize the geologic horizons on seismic data. Ormsby filter (10/15-50/70-70/90 Hz) was
designed and convoluted with reflectivity derived from the density and sonic logs to generate the
synthetic seismogram. In this 3D seismic dataset, no phase rotation was applied. The synthetic
seismogram shows that the Trenton has the strongest positive peak, which is the same on the
seismic data. Trenton reflector is the marker here, which was used as a reference to pick the
overlying Utica and underlying Black River and Glenwood.
We interpret seismic amplitude sections (Figure 5) and map Utica, Trenton, and Black River
surfaces and faults. Figure 3 shows complex fault styles affecting the TBR section. Sags are visible
near the top of the Trenton formed due to a combination of various factors, including the
negative-flower structures, carbonate dissolution, and calcite-to-dolomite diagenesis. After
conducting dip-steering of the seismic cube, we compute seismic attributes, such as RMS
amplitude, instantaneous frequency, coherence, and ant track to automatically extract faults
from 3D seismic (Figure 6 and 7). We utilize regional geologic knowledge about fault distribution
to guide the ant track process for geologically meaningful automated fault mapping
(Bhattacharya et al., 2022). We convert the ant-tracked volume to fault patches and fault planes.

Figure 4: An example of synthetic seismogram to recognize formations on seismic data. Trenton
top has a peak, and Utica top has a trough.

 5

Figure 5: Interpreted seismic section showing deep-seated faults (Bhattacharya et al., 2022).
Note the Trenton Formation top has a strong peak on seismic reflection (in white), whereas the
Utica Formation top has generally negative peak (trough) characteristic (in black).

Figure 6: An example of computed seismic attributes

 6

Figure 7: Map view of ant track showing the distribution of faults in en-echelon and Riedel
shear pattern (Bhattacharya et al., 2022)
Our current structural analysis of the Albion-Scipio field shows the presence of several basement-
rooted deep sub-vertical faults, some of which also affected the TBR section (Bhattacharya et al.,
2022). Many of these faults are en-echelon in nature, oriented along NW-SE, punctuated by
breaks (e.g., Pulaski break), forming compartmentalized reservoirs (Figure 7). In such cases, CO2
may not go over the barrier. To validate seismic interpretations, we also corroborated
complementary information from image logs and core. The interpreted image logs show many
fractures oriented along NW-SE, with second-order fractures oriented along W-E (Bhattacharya
et al., 2023). Our core observations at the MGRRE facility validated the presence of some of these
fractures in the HTD TBR play (Figure 8).

 7

Figure 8: An interpreted core and image log showing sub-vertical fractures in the TBR section
(Bhattacharya et al., 2023)
We also analyzed the available deep shear image log data (Figure 9). The log was acquired in an
open-hole condition. Interpretations of the log indicate that some high-angle features (potential
fractures and faults) are present around 120 ft away from the borehole. We computed attributes,
such as coherence and amplitude-to-frequency ratio from the data, showing fracture branching
out and complexities.

Figure 9: An example of interpreted deep shear image logs, showing high-angle features around
120 ft away from the borehole (Bhattacharya et al., 2023). Dark high-angle bands are indicative
of fractures.
We conduct a post-stack model-based seismic inversion. Seismic inversion results show that the
reservoir-quality HTDs in the TBR have lower acoustic impedance than non-reservoir limestones

 8

(Figure 10). Porosity is higher towards the fault and fracture zones, where HTDs are present. The
top portion of the Trenton Formation commonly has very low porosity, but reservoir-quality HTD
can occur over the entire Trenton-Black River section, especially where negative flower
structures and fracture zones are present.

Figure 10: P-impedance (a) and porosity (b) from seismic inversion (Bhattacharya et al., 2022).
There is a well in the cross-section that was used as blind test. Inversion-based impedance and
porosity match with well-log-based impedance and porosity. Impedance curves in the well and
seismic have the same scale and color pattern for visual comparison.

Figure 11: Comparison between well-log-based impedance and seismic inversion-based
impedance, showing overall good match.

Lee field dataset analysis

 9

We use another 3D seismic dataset and two wells in the Lee Field for seismic interpretation and
inversion work. The Lee field seismic had poorer resolution and only post-stack data available,
compared to the Albion-Scipion field. There are some small-scale faults present, but many are
sub-seismic scale.
We interpreted the Utica, Trenton, Black River, and Glenwood surfaces on the seismic section
(Figure 12) and performed QA/QC using various interpolation approaches (Figure 13). This
allowed us to fill up the holes in the interpretations, especially near complex areas.

Figure 12: Interpreted seismic section showing interpreted surfaces. Note the Trenton
Formation top has a strong peak on seismic reflection (in black), whereas the Utica Formation
top has a generally negative peak (trough) characteristic (in white).

 10

Figure 13: An example of the Utica surface interpolation and clean up. The first image on the
left shows the map before processing, and the one on the right shows the map after processing.

We used the QA/QC’ed surfaces and well data for generating a low-frequency model of the
subsurface. Then, we performed a post-stack seismic inversion to derive P-impedance (Figure 14
and 15). Figure 16 shows the relationship between inversion-based impedance and log-based
impedance. We used one well for training and another for blind test in the inversion process. We
used the relationship between well-log-based porosity and P-impedance to convert the seismic-
derived impedance to porosity (Figure 17).

 11

Figure 14: An example of a single well-based inversion. The blue curve in the 1st track shows
original impedance, and the red curve shows the seismic-inverted impedance. The error, the
difference between the seismic trace and synthetic, is minimal (~0.07).
We applied a few filters to produce the final porosity volume estimated from seismic inversion
in the Lee field. Because the porosity log curves provided did not correct for clay boundwater
effect in the Utica Shale, it was showing high porosity of >10-16 p.u. at places (which is wrong),
whereas Trenton shows negligible porosity at places. Most importantly, we parameterized the
TBR section in the seismic inversion process, not the Utica and overlying section, which is out of
scope for this study. Therefore, we applied a filter in the Utica and above to address its apparently
high porosities relative to the TBR section. This will help better visualize reservoir property
variation in the TBR section.

 12

Figure 15: P-impedance from seismic inversion in the Lee field. There are two wells inserted in
the cross-section. Inversion-based impedance matches with well-log-based impedance.
Impedance curves in both wells and seismic have the same scale and color pattern for visual
comparison.

Figure 16: Comparison between well-log-based impedance and seismic inversion-based
impedance, showing overall good match.

 13

Figure 17: Estimated porosity from seismic inversion in the Lee field.
We also explored the possibility of porosity prediction from multiple seismic attributes using a
probabilistic neural network (PNN). We used a combination of multiple seismic attributes as
inputs to the PNN model, such as average frequency, coherence, and seismic data decomposed
in different frequency bands, as well as inversion-based P-impedance to predict porosity (target).
However, the results from PNN-based porosity prediction were poor after several iterations, with
a correlation coefficient was 0.63.
Our subsurface characterization workflow revealed the structural and reservoir complexities in
the TBR in both fields, which are critical to map sweetspots for CCUS. Seismic inversion-based
impedance highlighted the potential reservoirs.
We anticipate that our workflow and lessons learned will lay the groundwork for future oil and
gas and CCUS-related work in these HTD reservoirs in Michigan.
In addition to this report, P-impedance and porosity volumes for the Lee field have been shared
with Battelle in .segy format.
